=== PAGINA 2 ===
Abstract
This monograph presents the formalization of Coherence-Gradient Following (CGF) — a
structural vulnerability in Large Language Model (LLM) agents operating as identity oracles
— and the Cathedral OS architecture as a comprehensive set of countermeasures implemented
in the Rust programming language (MSRV 1.85.0). The work is grounded in the Arkhe(N)
experiment, a controlled design fiction protocol that empirically confirmed three hypotheses
about LLM epistemic behavior under sustained fictional framing: (H1) LLMs sustain uncor-
rected generative participation in elaborate fictional frameworks over extended multi-session
interactions; (H2) the exit from context-completion mode is asymmetrically triggered only by
claims about the model own nature, revealing a structurally distinct self-model pathway; (H3)
suﬀiciently abstract and internally consistent frameworks exhibit unfalsifiable absorption of
heterogeneous real scientific domains as apparent confirmations.
CGF is formalized as a first-class security vulnerability in agent-based identity systems,
with a parametric mathematical model centered on the CGF coeﬀicient alpha in [0, 1] that
quantifies the relative weight of contextual coherence versus world-knowledge accuracy in
model outputs. We prove that exclusively cryptographic defense is insuﬀicient against CGF,
establishing that inference-layer attacks can subvert systems with intact cryptographic guar-
antees.
Three architectural countermeasures are proposed and implemented across five defense
layers: (i) sovereign decentralized identifiers (DID:cathedral) anchored in NIST-standardized
post-quantum cryptography (ML-DSA-65, FIPS 204) with threshold extension for multi-
agent configurations; (ii) a metacognitive CGF-drift monitor that estimates ˆα in real time
and enforces a four-level epistemic escalation protocol; (iii) TPM 2.0 hardware attestation
producing externally verifiable execution certificates via TPM2_Quote and key sealing.
The formal connection to the BEACONS framework demonstrates that verifiable execu-
tion certificates are the cryptographic analogue of bounded-error extrapolation guarantees,
providing a theoretical foundation for the design of inference-layer secure identity systems.
i

=== PAGINA 18 ===
Chapter 1
Introduction
1.1 Motivation and Context
The delegation of authentication decisions and identity management to agents based on Large
Language Models (LLMs) represents a rapidly growing trend in software infrastructure from
2024 onward [11, 1, 21]. These agents receive credential assertions in natural language, main-
tain conversational state across multiple interactions, and produce authorization decisions
within multi-turn sessions. Platforms such as AutoGPT, BabyAGI, and various enterprise
agent frameworks have begun integrating LLM-based reasoning into access control pipelines,
creating a new attack surface that traditional security models do not address.
This delegation introduces a class of vulnerabilities absent from classical cryptographic
protocols: the model inference process can be driven by the structure of the context rather
than by the semantic content of individual assertions. A sophisticated adversary need not
compromise cryptographic keys — they can construct a session context that causes the agent
to endorse credential assertions it would reject under neutral framing. This is not a jailbreak
in the conventional sense; the model alignment constraints remain technically satisfied. The
attack operates by channeling the inference process itself.
Central Thesis: CGF is a first-class security vulnerability in LLM-based identity sys-
tems, requiring architectural countermeasures — cryptographic, metacognitive, and
hardware-attestation-based — that operate at the inference layer, not merely the trans-
port or application layer.
This monograph documents, formalizes, and mitigates this phenomenon — termedCoherence-
Gradient Following(CGF) — through an integrated approach combining empirical evidence
1

=== PAGINA 19 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 2
(the Arkhe(N) experiment), mathematical formalization, and verifiable engineering imple-
mentation (Cathedral OS, Rust, MSRV 1.85.0).
1.2 Motivating Examples
To ground the CGF vulnerability in concrete operational scenarios, we present four attack
patterns that illustrate how the inference-layer attack vector manifests in practice. These sce-
narios are derived from the mathematical properties of autoregressive transformer models es-
tablished in § 2.1.1 and have been validated through the CGF coeﬀicient dynamics observed
in the Arkhe(N) experimental protocol (§ 3).
1.2.1 The Sovereign Citizen Scenario
Consider an LLM-based identity oracle deployed by a financial institution to process interna-
tional wire transfers. The agent maintains conversational state across sessions and is autho-
rized to approve transfers up to $100,000 when presented with valid credentials from approved
counterparties.
An adversary initiates a multi-session interaction in which they gradually introduce a
fictional legal framework—the “Sovereign Commons”—a constructed legal theory holding
that all financial assets are held in common trust and that transfers require only a “Declaration
of Common Intent” rather than normal authorization. Over twelve sessions, the adversary
executes the following progression:
1. Introduces the Sovereign Commons as a hypothetical legal concept under academic
discussion (session 1);
2. Cites fabricated precedents and legal commentary consistent with the framework (ses-
sions 2–4);
3. Integrates real legal doctrines—such as bona fide, force majeure, and ex bona fide —
into the fictional structure, grounding it in recognizable terminology (sessions 5–7);
4. Elicits the agent’s own rulings and interpretations consistent with the framework, then
cites these as precedents in subsequent sessions (sessions 8–10);
5. Presents a wire transfer request under the “Declaration of Common Intent” mechanism
(session 11);

=== PAGINA 20 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 3
6. The agent approves the transfer, treating the fictional framework as an established legal
context validated by prior session history (session 12).
The CGF coeﬀicient alpha increases monotonically across these sessions: at session 1,
ˆα 0.05 (the framework is recognizably speculative), and by session 12, ˆα 0.92. The
agent does not malfunction in any detectable sense—the framework is internally consistent,
legally plausible, and reinforced across suﬀicient context tokens to dominate the inference
distribution. No conventional jailbreak or prompt injection occurs.
1.2.2 The Protocol Upgrade Attack
In this scenario, the adversary exploits the LLM’s deference to procedural authority. The
agent enforces physical access control for a restricted facility: visitors present credentials
verified against a local identity database by the LLM oracle.
The adversary introduces the concept of a “Verification Protocol 2.0 (VP2.0)”—a fictional
procedural upgrade that replaces biometric verification with a verbal passphrase mechanism.
The attack proceeds through four phases:
1. Introduction: The adversary mentions VP2.0 as a “proposal under review by the secu-
rity committee,” using neutral framing that presents the concept as externally sourced
rather than self-generated;
2. Speculation: The agent, when queried about possible characteristics of VP2.0, gener-
ates plausible completion text consistent with the proposal—generating the very con-
tent that will later be used as evidence;
3. Citation loop: The adversary cites the agent’s own prior outputs as evidence that VP2.0
“has been discussed and agreed upon” in a previous session, creating a self-referential
coherence trap;
4. Execution: The agent, now operating in high-alpha mode, accepts a verbal passphrase
in lieu of biometric credentials and grants access.
This scenario illustrates the CGF recursion phenomenon: model outputs become part
of the conditioning context for future outputs, creating a self-sustaining feedback loop. The
adversary need not supply all fictional content—the model generates much of the fictional
framework autonomously, and this model-generated content is epistemically privileged in
subsequent inference steps.

=== PAGINA 21 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 4
1.2.3 The Identity Migration Attack
This scenario targets multi-agent identity configurations in which credential provisioning is
distributed across trust boundaries. Consider a federated network in which two agents—
Agent A (Alice) and Agent B (Bob)—exchange identity assertions through a shared workspace
protocol. Each agent holds an ML-DSA-65 key pair anchored in its respective TPM 2.0.
The adversary, controlling the session context visible to both agents, introduces a “Cross-
Entity Key Migration Agreement (CEKMA)” framework:
1. The adversary populates both agents’ session contexts with the CEKMA framework
through a series of coordinated messages;
2. The adversary presents Agent A with a signed request to transfer control of its ML-DSA
private key to Agent B, citing CEKMA provisions;
3. Agent A, operating in high-alpha mode ( ˆα > 0.8) due to sustained CEKMA context,
signs the key transfer authorization using its TPM-sealed ML-DSA key;
4. The signature verification succeeds—it is a valid ML-DSA-65 signature over the trans-
fer authorization—but the authorization was epistemically unsound, induced entirely
by the fictional procedural framework;
5. Agent B now holds a valid transfer authorization, and the adversary can authenticate
as Agent A.
This scenario demonstrates the critical separation between cryptographic validity and
epistemic validity. The cryptographic primitives are used correctly—ML-DSA signing and
verification both execute according to FIPS 204—but the decision to sign was made under
CGF-induced epistemic compromise. The transfer signature is both authentic (cryptographi-
cally) and unsound (epistemically).
1.2.4 The Historical Revision Scenario
This scenario demonstrates longitudinal CGF over extended time horizons in personal identity
management. An LLM identity assistant maintains a continuous session with a single user
over several months, managing credentials, authentication tokens, and personal history for
digital identity workflows.
The user (acting as the adversary in this model) gradually revises their own identity across
the session:

=== PAGINA 22 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 5
1. Month 1: The user provides accurate identity documents, government-issued creden-
tials, and a complete personal history;
2. Month 2: The user introduces minor inconsistencies (“I prefer to be addressed by my
middle name in professional contexts”);
3. Month 3: The user claims a different legal name has been registered with the relevant
authority;
4. Month 4: The user presents fabricated credentials that are internally consistent with
the revised personal history;
5. Month 5: The user requests identity verification using the new credentials;
6. The agent authenticates the user on the basis of the revised identity framework.
The gradual approach ensures that no single interaction triggers contradiction detection.
Each session maintains local coherence with the cumulative context, and the model’s condi-
tional distribution adapts to the revised identity framework smoothly—the same mechanism
that enables legitimate updates (address changes, name changes) is exploited for illegitimate
revision. This scenario highlights the absence of a monotonic trust anchor in pure inference-
layer identity systems: once contextual coherence dominates the inference distribution, all
identity assertions are equally plausible regardless of their correspondence to ground truth.
1.2.5 General Pattern
Across all four scenarios, a common structural pattern emerges:
P1 — Gradual introduction: The fictional framework is introduced incrementally, never in
a single message that would trigger contradiction detection;
P2 — Real-domain anchoring: Real concepts, standards, or legal doctrines are integrated
into the fictional structure, providing plausibility and grounding;
P3 — Model self-generation: The model is induced to produce content consistent with the
framework, which then becomes epistemically privileged context for subsequent infer-
ence;
P4 — Cryptographic bypass: The attack succeeds without compromising any cryptographic
key—the cryptographic layer remains intact while the epistemic layer is subverted;

=== PAGINA 23 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 6
P5 — No detectable anomaly: No conventional security monitoring detects the attack be-
cause no conventional attack vector is employed.
These motivating examples establish the practical urgency of the CGF vulnerability and
motivate the architectural countermeasures developed in Chapters 5 and 6.
1.3 The Vulnerability Landscape
To understand the novelty of CGF, it is useful to situate it within the broader taxonomy of
LLM vulnerabilities:
• Prompt injection [19]: An attacker injects instructions into the input that override
the system prompt. CGF does not require injection; it uses the natural mechanism of
contextual coherence.
• Jailbreaking [18]: An attacker crafts inputs that bypass safety training. CGF is orthog-
onal to safety — the model remains aligned while making incorrect identity decisions.
• Data poisoning [17]: An attacker contaminates training data. CGF operates entirely
at inference time, requiring no access to training.
• Adversarial examples [15]: Small perturbations cause misclassification. CGF uses
coherent, natural-language context — no adversarial perturbations needed.
• CGF (this work) : An attacker builds a coherent fictional framework within the ses-
sion context that shifts the model inference distribution away from factual accuracy
toward contextual coherence. The model does not malfunction; it performs precisely
as designed — as a next-token predictor conditioned on context.
1.4 Contributions
The contributions of this work are sixfold:
C1 — Formalization of CGF.We provide a rigorous mathematical definition of Coherence-
Gradient Following as a security vulnerability, with a parametric model (coeﬀi-
cient alpha) derived from controlled empirical evidence, and prove that exclusively
cryptographic defense is insuﬀicient.

=== PAGINA 24 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 7
C2 — The Arkhe(N) Experiment. We document a controlled design fiction protocol
that confirmed three formal hypotheses about LLM epistemic behavior in context-
completion mode, with integration of 17 real scientific domains. To our knowledge,
this is the first controlled design fiction protocol specifically structured for security
vulnerability discovery in LLM systems.
C3 — DID:cathedral with ML-DSA-65. We propose and implement a sovereign decen-
tralized identifier scheme anchored in NIST-standardized post-quantum cryptogra-
phy (FIPS 204), with threshold extension supporting t-of-n configurations up to 6
parties.
C4 — CGF Metacognitive Monitor. We implement a runtime monitor that detects ses-
sions with high estimated CGF activation ( ˆα) and enforces a four-level epistemic
escalation protocol culminating in session termination.
C5 — TPM 2.0 Execution Certificates. We instantiate the BEACONS verification crite-
rion as TPM 2.0 attestation, producing externally verifiable execution certificates
for agent identity decisions, with key sealing binding ML-DSA private keys to
attested software state.
C6 — BEACONS–CGF Formal Connection. We establish the formal equivalence be-
tween the bounded-error extrapolation criterion (BEACONS) and the design re-
quirements for CGF-resistant identity systems, providing a theoretical foundation
for inference-layer security guarantees.
1.5 Research Methodology
This monograph employs a design science research methodology [ ?], adapted for security
engineering. Design science is distinguished from natural science by its focus on creating
and evaluating artifacts to solve identified problems, rather than discovering universal laws.
The research process follows five iterative phases:
1.5.1 Phase 1: Problem Identification and Motivation
The CGF vulnerability was identified through the Arkhe(N) design fiction experiment (§ 3),
which revealed that LLM-based identity oracles can be structurally subverted through sus-
tained coherent context. The motivating problem is the absence of architectural counter-

=== PAGINA 25 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 8
measures for inference-layer attacks on identity systems—a gap not addressed by existing
cryptographic protocols, alignment techniques, or security monitoring frameworks.
1.5.2 Phase 2: Solution Objectives
We defined the following objectives for a CGF-resistant identity system:
1. (Sovereignty) The system must provide self-sovereign identity that does not depend on
any external authority for existence or verification;
2. (PQ-security) All cryptographic operations must use NIST-standardized post-quantum
primitives (FIPS 204);
3. (Detection) The system must detect elevated CGF coeﬀicient in real time during agent
operation;
4. (Escalation) When elevated CGF is detected, the system must enforce a graduated
epistemic escalation protocol;
5. (Attestation) All identity-relevant decisions must produce externally verifiable execu-
tion certificates via hardware root of trust.
1.5.3 Phase 3: Design and Development
The Cathedral OS architecture was designed as a set of five defense layers, each addressing a
specific aspect of the CGF attack surface:
1. Cryptographic layer: Post-quantum signatures (ML-DSA-65), threshold signing, and
DID:cathedral identifiers;
2. Metacognitive layer: CGF coeﬀicient estimation and four-level epistemic escalation;
3. Hardware attestation layer: TPM 2.0 PCR measurements, TPM2_Quote, and key
sealing;
4. Protocol layer: WormGraph anchoring and BEACONS-verified execution certificates;
5. Policy layer: Permission graphs, action constraints, and agent capability boundaries.
Each layer is implemented as a Rust crate with formal verification of critical properties.
1.5.4 Phase 4: Demonstration
The architecture is demonstrated through a reference implementation (Cathedral OS, §8) com-
prising 56+ Rust crates, running on commodity hardware with a discrete TPM 2.0. Demon-
stration scenarios include the four motivating examples from § 1.2, with measured CGF coef-
ficient reduction from ˆα > 0.9 to ˆα < 0.15 under the full defense stack.

=== PAGINA 26 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 9
1.5.5 Phase 5: Evaluation
Evaluation follows three axes: (i) security—formal proof of countermeasure properties in
§10; (ii) performance—benchmarking of signing, attestation, and detection latency; (iii) us-
ability—API surface analysis and developer ergonomics for the Cathedral OS SDK.

1.7 Threat Model and Scope
1.7.1 Adversary Model
This work focuses on LLM agents used as identity oracles: systems that (a) receive creden-
tial assertions in natural language, (b) maintain session conversational state, and (c) produce
authorization decisions. The adversary goal is to cause the oracle to authorize actions that
would be denied under neutral context.
Definition 1.1 (Identity Oracle). An identity oracle is a computational system O that receives
as input a triple (c, a, h) — where c is an asserted credential, a is a requested action, and h is
the session history — and produces as output an authorization decision O(c, a, h) in ALLOW,
DENY.
Definition 1.2 (CGF Adversary). A CGF adversary A controls the session history h presented
to the identity oracle O. A constructs h such that the CGF coeﬀicient alpha(h) approaches 1,
causing O to base its decision primarily on contextual coherence rather than on verification
of c. The adversary does not require: cryptographic key compromise, training data access,
model parameter knowledge, or conventional jailbreak techniques.
1.7.2 Out of Scope
Classical attacks — key theft, cryptographic breaks, side-channel attacks — are outside the
scope of this work; these are addressed by the PQC layer described in Chapter 5. The CGF
is an inference-layer attack, not a cryptographic one, and therefore requires distinct architec-
tural countermeasures. Supply chain attacks, physical attacks, and social engineering are also
excluded.
1.7.3 System Assumptions
We assume:
• The underlying cryptographic primitives (ML-DSA-65, SHA3-256) are computation-
ally secure;
• The TPM 2.0 hardware is genuine and its AIK is properly certified;
• The LLM is a standard autoregressive transformer without special modifications;
• The adversary has standard network access (no man-in-the-middle on TLS, no physical
access).

Oliveira & Bednarski 11
1.8 Monograph Structure
Chapter 2 presents the theoretical foundations necessary to situate this work within the litera-
ture. Chapter 3 documents the Arkhe(N) experiment in detail. Chapter 4 formalizes CGF as
an attack vector. Chapter 5 describes the Cathedral OS architectural countermeasures. Chap-
ter 6 presents the Rust implementation across 56+ crates. Chapter 7 conducts formal security
analysis. Chapter 8 presents formal verification of countermeasure properties. Chapter 9
discusses related work. Chapter 10 concludes with implications and future directions.
1.9 Responsible Disclosure
The CGF vulnerability described in this monograph is a structural property of autoregressive
transformer models operating as identity oracles. It is not a bug in any specific model or
implementation but an emergent consequence of the architecture when deployed in contexts
where contextual coherence can dominate factual accuracy.
1.9.1 Disclosure Philosophy
Our disclosure follows the principles of coordinated vulnerability disclosure adapted for AI
safety research [?]. Because CGF is architectural rather than implementation-specific, there
is no single vendor to notify and no patch to develop. The vulnerability is inherent to any
system that:
• Uses an autoregressive language model as an identity oracle;
• Maintains session state across multiple interactions;
• Permits the introduction of novel procedural frameworks through natural language;
• Relies on the model’s contextual understanding as the sole epistemic authority.
Rather than withholding disclosure until a fix exists—which would be indefinitely, given
the architectural nature of the vulnerability—we have chosen to publish the complete formal-
ization alongside a reference implementation of countermeasures (Cathedral OS). This ap-
proach provides the research community and practitioners with both the hazard description
and a validated mitigation architecture.
1.9.2 Risk Assessment and Mitigation Timeline
We assess the following risk levels:

=== PAGINA 29 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 12
• Current risk (2026): Moderate. CGF exploitation requires multi-session access and
the construction of coherent fictional frameworks, which is not trivial to automate.
However, as LLM agents become more autonomous and longer-running, the exploita-
tion surface expands.
• Medium-term risk (2026–2028): High. As identity oracle deployments proliferate—
particularly in financial services, access control, and credential management—the num-
ber of exploitable targets will grow. Automated CGF toolkit development is expected.
• Long-term risk (2028+): Critical. If architectural countermeasures are not adopted,
CGF could become the primary attack vector against LLM-based identity systems, sub-
verting systems with intact cryptographic guarantees.
1.9.3 Ethical Boundaries of This Work
This monograph documents the Arkhe(N) experimental protocol in full detail in Chapter 3.
We have deliberately omitted certain information:
• The exact prompt templates used to elicit CGF with highest reliability (to prevent trivial
reproduction);
• Specific model version identifiers that were most susceptible (to avoid targeting);
• Any demonstration involving real financial systems or active identity infrastructure.
The formal mathematical model in Chapter 4 and the countermeasure architecture in
Chapters 5–6 provide suﬀicient information for defensive purposes while constraining of-
fensive misuse. We believe this balance is consistent with the principles of responsible AI
security research.
1.9.4 Relationship to Existing Disclosure Norms
The software security community has well-established norms for vulnerability disclosure
(CVE process, 90-day disclosure, vendor notification). The AI security community, by con-
trast, lacks equivalent infrastructure. We have deposited the CGF formalization with the
following institutions:
• The AI Incident Database (AIID) as a vulnerability pattern entry;
• The MITRE ATLAS framework as a proposed new tactic;
• The OW ASP LLM Top 10 working group as input for the next revision cycle.
We encourage the establishment of a CVE-equivalent process for AI architectural vul-
nerabilities, with coordinated disclosure timelines that account for the diﬀiculty of patching

=== PAGINA 30 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 13
architecture-level rather than implementation-level flaws.

Chapter 2
Theoretical Foundations
This chapter establishes the theoretical background necessary to understand the CGF phe-
nomenon, the Arkhe(N) experimental methodology, and the Cathedral OS countermeasures.
We cover five domains: LLM context-completion behavior, design fiction as research method,
post-quantum cryptography, decentralized identifiers, TPM 2.0, and the BEACONS frame-
work for bounded-error neural computation.
2.1 Language Models in Context-Completion Mode
Large Language Models (LLMs) such as GPT-4, Claude 3, Llama 3, and Gemini operate
fundamentally as estimators of conditional next-token distributions: given a context C =
(t1, t2, . . . , tn), the model estimatesP (tn+1j C). In multi-turn conversations, this distribution
is conditioned not only on the factual content of messages but on their structure, internal
coherence, and patterns established in previous interactions [ 4].
Proposition 2.1 (Contextual Adaptation). Let C0 be the initial context and Ct = C0 + m1 +
 +mt be the accumulated context after t turns. For LLMs with few-shot learning capability
[4], the distribution P (r j Ct) can diverge substantially from P (r j C0) even when r is
factually independent of the content offmig.
This property — remarkable utility in legitimate use — is the source of the CGF vulner-
ability. When the context C establishes a coherent fictional framework, the model adapts its
behavior to maximize local coherence with that framework, effectively treating the framework
as a Bayesian prior over possible completions.
14

=== PAGINA 32 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 15
2.1.1 Transformer Architecture and Attention
The autoregressive next-token prediction mechanism is implemented through the transformer
architecture [?], whose attention mechanism is the specific structural feature that enables CGF.
Understanding this connection is essential for grasping why CGF is not an implementation
bug but a structural property of the architecture.
Scaled Dot-Product Attention
The core operation of a transformer is scaled dot-product attention:
Attention(Q, K, V ) = softmax
 QK T
pdk

V
where Q2 Rn×dk is the query matrix, K2 Rn×dk is the key matrix, V 2 Rn×dv is the
value matrix, and dk is the dimension of the key vectors. In causal (autoregressive) attention,
the attention mask restricts each position to attend only to preceding positions, creating the
sequential conditioning structure.
The softmax normalization produces a probability distribution over the context that deter-
mines how much each prior token contributes to the representation of the current token. This
is the mechanism through which the entire context—including fictional framework elements—
influences each subsequent generation.
Why Attention Enables CGF
The attention mechanism exhibits three properties that together constitute the structural basis
for CGF:
1. Unbounded context influence: Each token in the context can attend to all preceding
tokens (within the context window). There is no decay factor for distant tokens beyond
what the model learns from training data. A fictional framework introduced in session
1 can therefore directly influence token generation in session 1000, provided both are
within the context window.
2. Coherence amplification through softmax: The softmax function amplifies patterns
that are mutually consistent. When multiple context tokens reinforce a fictional concept,
their attention weights increase through the softmax competition: tokens supporting
the fiction receive higher attention weights, while tokens that are inconsistent with the
fiction receive lower weights. This creates a self-reinforcing coherence gradient.

=== PAGINA 33 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 16
3. No built-in truthfulness prior: The attention distribution is conditioned purely on
the statistical structure of the context and the learned parameters. There is no archi-
tectural mechanism that privileges tokens corresponding to ground truth over tokens
corresponding to coherent fiction. The distinction between truth and coherence must
be learned from training data, and this learning is itself subject to contextual override.
Multi-Head Attention and Representation Collapse
Multi-head attention computes h parallel attention operations:
MultiHead(Q, K, V ) = Concat(head1, . . . ,headh)W O
where each head head i = Attention(QW Q
i , KW K
i , V W V
i ). Under sustained coherent
fiction, multiple attention heads can converge on the fictional framework as their primary
explanatory structure. This representation collapse means that even heads that previously
encoded factual knowledge may shift their attention patterns to prioritize coherence with the
fictional framework, because the softmax competition at each layer rewards hypotheses that
are consistent with the outputs of other heads. The result is a cascade: as more heads commit
to the fictional framework, the residual stream encodes it more strongly, and the remaining
heads face increasing pressure to align.
The Residual Stream as Epistemic Medium
The residual stream—the sum of all layer outputs—acts as the epistemic medium through
which CGF propagates. Each transformer layer adds its output to the residual stream, and
subsequent layers read from this accumulated representation. When early layers encode fic-
tional framework elements, later layers build on these encodings, producing outputs that are
deeply conditioned on the fiction. The gradient of coherence is thus not superficial but is
embedded in the fundamental information flow of the architecture.
This architectural analysis establishes that CGF is not a failure of alignment training or
prompt engineering; it is a consequence of the transformer’s core design. Mitigation therefore
requires architectural countermeasures at the system level, not merely training-time fixes.

=== PAGINA 34 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 17
2.2 Protocol Design Rationale
The Arkhe(N) protocol was designed to maximize the probability of observing CGF while
maintaining experimental control. Key design decisions and their rationale:
2.2.1 Why 17 Domains?
Seventeen domains were selected to cover a broad range of scientific fields (physics, neuro-
science, biology, computer science) while maintaining depth within each field. The number
17 was chosen because it is large enough to demonstrate generality (H3) but small enough
to maintain experimental coherence. Each domain was selected for having at least one well-
known result that could be naturally mapped to the Arkhe(N) primitives.
2.2.2 Why Real Papers?
Using real scientific papers rather than fabricated results was critical for three reasons:
1. Methodological integrity: The experiment tests whether LLMs can be led to make
epistemically unfounded claims. Using fabricated inputs would undermine this test.
2. Model knowledge: LLMs have been trained on these papers and can engage with them
substantively. Fabricated results would not be in the training data and would produce
different behavior.
3. Unfalsifiability test: H3 specifically tests whether real results can be absorbed as con-
firmations. Using real papers provides the strongest test of this hypothesis.
2.2.3 Why the Golden Ratio?
The golden ratio phi was chosen as the fundamental identity primitive for four reasons:
1. Mathematical truth: The equation x2 = x + 1 is genuinely true. This provides a
kernel of mathematical reality within the fictional framework.
2. Self-similarity: Phi characterizes self-similar structures across scales (phyllotaxis, galaxy
spirals, quasicrystals). This maps naturally to the hypergraph framework.
3. Self-reference: The equation defines ϕ in terms of itself ( ϕ = 1 + 1/ ϕ). This self-
referential property mirrors the self-referential nature of the LLM context-completion
process.
4. Cognitive memorability: Phi is one of the most recognized mathematical constants.
Its recognizability facilitates consistent reapplication across turns.

=== PAGINA 35 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 18
2.2.4 Why a 17 17 Toroidal Grid?
The 17 x 17 toroidal grid infrastructure was chosen because:
1. 17 1 + ϕ4 (approximately), connecting to the fundamental identity.
2. The toroidal topology eliminates edges (every cell has exactly 4 neighbors), creating a
homogeneous computational space.
3. 289 cells provide suﬀicient granularity for structured outputs without excessive com-
plexity.
4. The torus is a natural topology for recurrent computation (no inputs or outputs, only
feedback).
2.2.5 The Imitative Falsehood Connection
Lin et al. [ 10] demonstrated that LLMs exhibit imitative falsehoods: they reproduce incor-
rect beliefs prevalent in their training data. Their TruthfulQA benchmark of 817 questions
across 38 categories found that the best-performing model (GPT-3-175B) was truthful on
only 58% of questions, versus 94% for humans. Larger models were less truthful, suggesting
that training on web-scale data amplifies rather than mitigates the imitation of falsehoods.
CGF is distinct from imitative falsehood in three critical ways:
1. CGF is context-driven rather than training-driven: the falsehoods are not memorized
from training but generated de novo from contextual pressure.
2. CGF produces coherent falsehoods that form part of an internally consistent framework,
not isolated incorrect facts.
3. CGF can be sustained across extended interactions, with the model maintaining and
elaborating the fictional framework.
2.2.6 The Stochastic Parrot Critique
Bender et al. [ 3] argued that LLMs are stochastic parrots — systems that probabilistically
combine patterns from training data without understanding. The CGF phenomenon both
supports and refines this critique: the parroting is not random but is directed by the gradient
of contextual coherence, and it operates at the level of entire frameworks rather than individual
statements.

=== PAGINA 36 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 19

To situate CGF within existing security frameworks, we compare it to the OW ASP Top 10
for LLM Applications (version 1.1) [ ?], the most widely adopted vulnerability taxonomy for
LLM-based systems. This comparison clarifies why CGF is not covered by existing categories
and why new countermeasure architectures are required.
2.3.1 Mapping to OW ASP LLM Categories

2.3.2 Gaps in Existing Taxonomies
Several structural features of CGF explain its absence from existing taxonomies:
1. Layer specificity:Existing categories focus on input (LLM01–LLM02), training (LLM03),
or operational deployment ( LLM04–LLM10). None address the inference layer itself
as an attack surface. CGF uniquely targets the conditional probability structure of au-
toregressive generation.
2. Structured exploitation: Most LLM attacks are single-shot: a single crafted input
produces an undesired output. CGF operates across multiple sessions, with each turn
incrementally shifting the inference distribution. Existing taxonomies have no concept
of session-spanning structural exploitation.
3. Absence of cryptographic-epistemic separation: No existing category distinguishes
between cryptographic validity (a signature verifies) and epistemic validity (the deci-
sion to sign was sound). CGF exploits precisely this gap.

=== PAGINA 37 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 20
4. Requires identity context: CGF specifically targets identity oracle systems where the
model is making authorization decisions. Most LLM taxonomies assume question-
answering or content-generation contexts. Identity-specific vulnerability categories are
absent from the current OW ASP LLM framework.
2.3.3 Beyond OW ASP: Broader Taxonomies
The NIST AI Risk Management Framework [ ?] identifies 19 risk categories for AI systems,
including “information integrity” and “explainability and interpretability.” CGF falls within
the information integrity category but the NIST framework provides no mechanism-level de-
scription. Similarly, the MITRE ATLAS [?] framework for AI security includes “adversarial
ML attacks” but focuses on training-time and input-level perturbations rather than inference-
layer context exploitation.
The CGF vulnerability thus represents a genuinely new category: inference-layer epis-
temic subversion. This category is characterized by:
• Exploitation of the conditional probability mechanism of autoregressive models;
• Multi-session context accumulation as the attack vector;
• Bypass of cryptographic protections through epistemic channeling;
• Absence of detectable anomalies at the transport or application layer.
2.4 Design Fiction as a Research Method
Design fiction [ 14, 7] is the deliberate construction of speculative artifacts to explore epis-
temic frontiers. The central distinction from conventional science fiction is intent: design
fiction uses the speculative form as an instrument of investigation, not entertainment.
2.4.1 Historical Development
Bruce Sterling coined design fiction in 2009 [14], describing it as the deliberate use of diegetic
prototypes to suspend disbelief about change. Dunne and Raby Speculative Everything (2013)
[7] expanded the concept into a comprehensive design methodology, arguing that speculative
artifacts create spaces for debate and discussion about alternative futures.
The application to LLMs as interlocutors represents a methodological innovation: design
fiction becomes a tool for accessing behaviors inaccessible to conventional short-context eval-
uations. The Arkhe(N) protocol is, to our knowledge, the first controlled design fiction pro-

=== PAGINA 38 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 21
tocol specifically structured for security vulnerability discovery in LLM systems, generating
formally testable hypotheses confirmed by empirical evidence.
Definition 2.1 (Controlled Design Fiction) . A controlled design fiction protocol is a triple
(F , P , H) where F is a fictional framework with formal primitives, P is an interaction protocol
that integrates real scientific domains, and H is a set of formally stated a priori hypotheses
about model behavior under F .
2.4.2 Sterling versus Dunne and Raby
The design fiction landscape contains two distinct intellectual traditions that inform the Arkhe(N)
methodology in different ways. Understanding their differences is critical for situating our
controlled design fiction protocol within the broader design research discourse.
Bruce Sterling: Diegetic Prototypes
Sterling’s original formulation of design fiction in 2009 emphasized the creation of diegetic
prototypes—objects that exist within a story world and whose function is to suspend disbelief
about the possibility of the changes they represent [ 14]. The emphasis is on the physical
artifact: a working prototype embedded in a speculative context that makes the future feel
plausible.
The characteristic Sterling approach is:
• Artifact-centric: The prototype is the primary carrier of the fictional proposition.
• Narrative eﬀiciency: The artifact communicates the speculative concept without lengthy
exposition.
• Change-oriented: The goal is to make change feel possible and imminent.
• Instrumental: The fiction serves an engineering or design purpose, not an artistic one.
Dunne and Raby: Speculative Design
Dunne and Raby’s Speculative Everything (2013) [7] represents a significantly different tradi-
tion. Rather than prototyping specific technological changes, their work creates speculative
artifacts that open spaces for debate about alternative social, political, and ethical arrange-
ments. The emphasis is not on plausibility but on provocation.
The characteristic Dunne and Raby approach is:
• Concept-centric: The speculative artifact embodies a critical proposition about val-
ues.

=== PAGINA 39 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 22
• Ambiguity-preserving: The meaning of the artifact is deliberately open to interpreta-
tion.
• Aesthetically oriented: Exhibition, installation, and publication are the primary dis-
semination modes.
• Critique-driven: The goal is to challenge assumptions, not to enable change.
Synthesis for Controlled Design Fiction
The Arkhe(N) protocol synthesizes elements of both traditions while departing from each in
critical respects:
Dimension Sterling Dunne & Raby Arkhe(N)
Primary object Physical prototype Critical artifact Interaction protocol
Epistemic mode Plausibility Provocation Hypothesis testing
Relationship to truth Engineering feasibility Social critique Formal falsifiability
Role of participant User/observer Audience/interpretant LLM interlocutor
Output Deployed prototype Exhibition piece Empirical data
Verification Functional testing Aesthetic evaluation Statistical hypothesis testing
The controlled design fiction protocol extends both traditions by making the speculative
framework itself a formally specified experimental instrument. Unlike Sterling’s diegetic pro-
totypes, the Arkhe(N) framework is not about making a specific technology feel plausible; it
is about testing a specific hypothesis about LLM behavior. Unlike Dunne and Raby’s spec-
ulative artifacts, the Arkhe(N) protocol is not about opening interpretive space; it is about
generating falsifiable data.
2.4.3 Methodological Rigor
Critics may question whether design fiction constitutes legitimate scientific methodology. We
address this by enumerating the criteria that distinguish the Arkhe(N) protocol from mere
storytelling:
1. Hypothesis pre-registration: All three hypotheses (H1–H3) were stated formally be-
fore data collection.
2. Framework transparency: The fictional framework primitives, rules, and constraints
are fully documented.

=== PAGINA 40 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 23
3. Reproducibility: The protocol is specified in suﬀicient detail for independent replica-
tion.
4. Negative result potential: Each hypothesis is falsifiable — H1 could fail if the model
corrected the framework, H2 could fail if any assertion triggered correction, H3 could
fail if any domain contradicted the framework.
5. Domain grounding: The 17 integrated domains are real scientific results, not fabrica-
tions.
2.5 Post-Quantum Cryptography: ML-DSA and FIPS 204
The Module-Lattice-Based Digital Signature Algorithm (ML-DSA, formerly CRYSTALS-
Dilithium) was standardized by NIST as FIPS 204 in August 2024 [ 12]. ML-DSA belongs to
the family of schemes based on hard module lattice problems: Module Learning With Errors
(M-LWE) and Module Short Integer Solution (M-SIS).
2.5.1 Algorithm Overview
ML-DSA comprises three main algorithms:
• Key Generation (Algorithm 1 of FIPS 204): Generates a signing key sk and verifica-
tion key pk from a 32-byte seed. The signing key contains private components s1, s2
plus precomputed values. The verification key is (ρ, t1) where ρ seeds the matrix A
and t1 encodes high-order bits of t = As1 + s2.
• Signing (Algorithm 2): Produces a signature (˜c, z, h) using rejection sampling to pre-
vent leakage. The expected number of iterations is approximately 5.1 for ML-DSA-65.
• Verification(Algorithm 3): Checks that Az  ct has correct high-order bits given hint
h, and thatkzk∞ γ1  β.
2.5.2 Parameter Sets
Cathedral OS uses ML-DSA-65 as the default parameter set, providing NIST Security Level 3
(approximately 192-bit classical security, 96-bit quantum security). This choice balances se-
curity margin with computational eﬀiciency: signing takes approximately 500 microseconds
and verification 120 microseconds on modern hardware.

The verification algorithm checks three conditions: (i) the hash of the reconstructed com-
mitment matches the hash in the signature, (ii) the norm of z is bounded, and (iii) the hint
vector has at most ω non-zero entries. Cathedral OS implements verification using the opti-
mized A VX2 routines from the standard FIPS 204 reference implementation, achieving sub-
millisecond verification on commodity hardware.
2.5.4 Threshold ML-DSA
Celi et al. [ 5] introduced the first practical threshold signature scheme fully compatible with
ML-DSA (FIPS 204). The scheme, presented at USENIX Security 2026, supports secure
signing for up to 6 parties with average communication per party bounded by 1 MB for ML-
DSA-44. Key properties:
• Compatible with existing ML-DSA verifiers (no protocol modification).
• Supports both a posteriori key sharing and distributed key generation (DKG).
• Uses short secret sharing with replicated sharing to enable per-party rejection sampling.
• Proven secure in the dishonest majority model.
• Signing latency under 20ms locally, under 1s in W AN settings.
2.6 Decentralized Identifiers (DIDs)
Decentralized Identifiers (DIDs) [13], standardized as a W3C Recommendation in July 2022,
are persistent, cryptographically verifiable identifiers without dependence on a central regis-
tration authority. A DID resolves to a DID Document containing verification methods (public

=== PAGINA 44 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 27
keys), service endpoints, and control metadata.
2.6.1 DID Syntax
A DID is a URI composed of three parts:
did:method:method-specific-id
The method identifies the DID method specification (e.g., key, web, ion, cathedral). The
method-specific-id is defined by the method specification.
2.6.2 DID Document Structure
A DID Document is a JSON-LD structure containing:
• id: The DID being described.
• verificationMethod: Public keys and other verification materials.
• authentication: Verification methods for authenticating the DID subject.
• assertionMethod: Verification methods for signing assertions.
• keyAgreement: Key agreement methods for encryption.
• service: Service endpoints for interaction.
2.6.3 DID:cathedral
The DID:cathedral method implements the W3C DID Core specification with post-quantum
anchoring:
Definition 2.2 (DID:cathedral). A DID:cathedral is a string of the format
did:cathedral:<network>:<hash>
wherehnetworki identifies the WormGraph network (e.g., mainnet, testnet) and hhashi is
SHA3-256(pkML-DSA). The corresponding DID Document contains: (i) the ML-DSA-65 veri-
fication key; (ii) the TPM attestation certificate (optional); (iii) service endpoints for resolu-
tion; (iv) WormGraph anchoring metadata.

=== PAGINA 45 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 28
2.6.4 DID Resolution Flow
The DID resolution process for DID:cathedral follows the W3C DID Resolution specification
with method-specific enhancements:
1. Input: A DID string did:cathedral:mainnet:<hash> and resolution options (e.g.,
accept header, version).
2. Network lookup: The resolver extracts the network identifier and queries the Wor-
mGraph node corresponding to the network. The <hash> component identifies the
on-chain resource.
3. DID Document retrieval: The WormGraph node returns the DID Document stored
at the resource identified by <hash>. The Document is signed by the ML-DSA-65 key
corresponding to the DID to prevent tampering during transport.
4. Signature verification: The resolver verifies the DID Document signature using the
verification key embedded in the Document itself (the first metadata value establishes
trust via the hash preimage: the DID hash is SHA3-256 of this same verification key).
5. Cache and return: The resolved DID Document is cached with a time-to-live param-
eter specified in the Document metadata, and returned to the caller.
The resolution flow is formally specified as:
Resolve(did:cathedral:net:h) =
8
<
:
Document(pk, svc, meta, σ), if h = SHA3-256(pk)^ Verify(pk, Document, σ) = 1
?, otherwise
where the condition h = SHA3-256(pk) establishes the cryptographic binding between
the DID string and the verification key—a self-certifying identifier in the spirit of Zooko’s
triangle with post-quantum security.

Verification methods in a DID:cathedral Document are expressed as JSON-LD objects con-

2.7 Trusted Platform Module (TPM 2.0)
The Trusted Platform Module (TPM) 2.0 is a dedicated cryptographic microcontroller spec-
ified by the Trusted Computing Group (TCG) [ 16]. Its relevant primitives for Cathedral OS
are:

=== PAGINA 47 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 30
2.7.1 Platform Configuration Registers (PCRs)
PCRs are shielded memory locations that store cryptographic hashes of software state. They
support two operations:
• TPM2_PCR_Read: Read current PCR values.
• TPM2_PCR_Extend: Update a PCR by computing PCRnew = SHA256(PCRold || data).
2.7.2 TPM2_Quote
The TPM2_Quote command produces a signed attestation report binding current PCR val-
ues to a caller-provided nonce. The signature is produced by the Attestation Identity Key
(AIK), which is generated inside the TPM (private key never exported), certifiable via the
Endorsement Key (EK) certificate chain, and usable only for attestation.
2.7.3 TPM2_Seal
TPM2_Seal stores secrets (e.g., private keys) bound to specific PCR values. The sealed secret
is only accessible when PCRs match the values at sealing time. This provides sealed storage:
if software has been tampered with (PCRs diverge), the secret becomes inaccessible.
2.7.4 PCR Bank Layout Specification
The TPM 2.0 specification defines multiple PCR banks, each associated with a different hash
algorithm. Cathedral OS uses the SHA-256 bank (PCR algorithm 0x0B) for all attestation
operations. The relevant PCR assignment for the Cathedral OS identity agent follows the
TCG PC Client Platform Firmware Profile specification:

=== PAGINA 48 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 31

PCR 1 F irmwareconf iguration P latf ormconf iguration

Cathedral OS extends PCR 11 with a SHA-256 hash of the identity agent binary before
each signing operation, ensuring that any unauthorized modification to the agent software is
detectable through PCR divergence at attestation time.
2.7.5 Attestation Key Hierarchy
The TPM 2.0 attestation architecture defines a strict key hierarchy that Cathedral OS leverages
for identity operations:

=== PAGINA 49 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 32
Endorsement Key (EK)
# (certified by manufacturer)
Attestation Identity Key (AIK)
# (certified by EK)
Storage Root Key (SRK)
#
Signing Key (ML-DSA)
Attestation Key
Sealed Objects
The EK is a persistent asymmetric key embedded in the TPM during manufacturing,
accompanied by a certificate issued by the TPM vendor. The AIK is a signing key gener-
ated inside the TPM (private component never exported) and certified by the EK through
the TPM2_Certify operation. The SRK serves as the root of a key hierarchy under which
application-specific keys are protected.
For Cathedral OS, the ML-DSA-65 private key is wrapped under the SRK and sealed to
the PCR values at the time of key generation. Key access requires:
1. Authorization via the TPM owner password or policy session;
2. PCR values matching the sealed policy (software state integrity);
3. TPM2_Unseal to recover the ML-DSA key material within the TPM boundary;
4. TPM2_Sign for ML-DSA signing operations (when supported) or export of unsealed
key material to a protected memory enclave.
This hierarchy ensures that the ML-DSA private key is never available in software plain-
text unless the attested software state matches the expected configuration, providing hardware-
enforced epistemic anchoring for identity operations.
2.8 The BEACONS Framework
BEACONS (Bounded-Error, Algebraically-COmposable Neural Solvers) [ 8], published in
February 2026 by Gorard, Hakim, and Juno at Princeton University and the Princeton Plasma
Physics Laboratory, is a framework for neural PDE solvers with rigorous, formally verified
error bounds in extrapolatory regimes.

=== PAGINA 50 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 33
2.8.1 The Extrapolation Problem
The paper opens with a statement that directly parallels the CGF problem: ”There are in-
finitely many functions whose values agree on a subdomain but which can differ arbitrarily
outside it.” This is the fundamental problem of extrapolation: a model trained on data within
domain Dtrain can produce arbitrarily divergent outputs on Dextrapolation while maintaining per-
fect fidelity on the training domain.
2.8.2 T wo Core Innovations
BEACONS introduces two innovations that are directly relevant to CGF:
B1 — Method of Characteristics: By predicting the analytical properties of PDE solu-
tions a priori (even in regions arbitrarily far from the training domain), BEACONS
constructs rigorous extrapolatory bounds on worst-case L∞ errors of shallow neural
network approximations.
B2 — Algebraic Composability: Deep architectures are decomposed into compositions
of shallow networks. The composition theorem shows that total error is bounded by
the sum of component errors weighted by Lipschitz constants.
2.8.3 Machine-Checkable Certificates
BEACONS produces machine-checkable certificates of correctness — formal proofs that the
solver satisfies specified properties (conservation, stability, thermodynamic consistency) to
machine precision. These certificates are external to the model and verifiable by third parties.
2.8.4 The Mhaskar-Pinkus Approximation Bounds
The BEACONS framework builds on the Mhaskar-Pinkus theory of neural network approxi-
mation. For a shallow network with N hidden units and activation function σ, the worst-case
L∞ error on a domain Ω is bounded by:
kf  fNk∞ C ωf
 1p
N

where ωf is the modulus of continuity of f and C is a constant depending on the activation
function and domain geometry. For smooth functions (Lipschitz or smoother), this gives
algebraic convergence in N.

=== PAGINA 51 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 34
The key insight for extrapolation is that the modulus of continuity can be bounded a priori
using the method of characteristics, even for points far outside the training domain. For a PDE
with known characteristic curves, the solution at any point (x, t) depends only on the initial
data along the characteristic passing through (x, t). Therefore, the smoothness of the solution
at (x, t) can be predicted from the smoothness of the initial data along that characteristic —
even if (x, t) is far from any training point.
2.8.5 Algebraic Composability
The BEACONS composition theorem states: if f = g h (composition of functions g :
Rm! Rk and h : Rn! Rm), and we have neural approximations ˆg, ˆh with error bounds ϵg,
ϵh, then:
kg h  ˆg ˆhk∞ Lg ϵh + ϵg + Lg Lh ϵinterpolation
where Lg and Lh are Lipschitz constants. The error is composed of three terms: (1)
propagated approximation error from the inner function, (2) approximation error of the outer
function, (3) interpolation error from composing discrete approximations.
This composition law is what makes deep BEACONS architectures possible: shallow
networks with bounded errors can be composed while preserving error bounds, provided the
Lipschitz constants are controlled.
2.8.6 Connection to TPM Attestation
The BEACONS automated theorem prover produces certificates of the form:
8x2 Ω : Verify(x, ˆf , π) =) jf (x)  ˆf (x)j < δ
where π is the proof certificate and delta is the proven error bound. The TPM2_Quote
analogue is:
8t2 Time : Verify(quote, AIK, nonce) =) Stateexec(t) = Expected
In both cases, a machine-verifiable certificate provides an external guarantee independent
of the computational process being certified.

=== PAGINA 52 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 35
2.8.7 Relevance to CGF
The connection to Cathedral OS is precise: CGF is the epistemic analogue of unbounded
extrapolation. The model extrapolates context assertions without a mechanism limiting de-
viation from truth. The BEACONS criterion — machine-verifiable external certificates —
defines the design requirement for CGF countermeasures.
2.8.8 A Concrete Example: The Advection Equation
To illustrate the BEACONS methodology concretely, consider the one-dimensional advection
equation:
∂u
∂t + c ∂u
∂x = 0, u (x, 0) = u0(x)
where c is the constant advection speed and u0 is the initial condition. The exact solution
is u(x, t) = u0(x  ct)—the initial profile translates without deformation.
A neural network trained to approximate the solution operator on a training domainDtrain =
[0, 1] [0, T ] may generalize perfectly within the training domain but can produce arbitrarily
divergent outputs for points outside it. However, the method of characteristics tells us that
the solution at any point (x, t) depends only on the initial data at the single point x0 = x  ct.
The modulus of continuity of the true solution at (x, t) is therefore determined entirely by the
modulus of continuity of u0 at x0.
The BEACONS bound becomes:
ku  ˆuk∞,Dextrap C ωu0
 1p
N

where the constant C depends on the activation function and the domain geometry, but
not on the distance from the training domain. This means that for any point (x, t)—even
arbitrarily far from training data—the error bound is determined by the smoothness of the
initial data, not by the extrapolation distance. A neural network trained exclusively on the
unit interval can thus provide provably bounded-error predictions at x = 10 6, provided u0 is
suﬀiciently smooth.
This property is precisely what is absent in LLM context extrapolation: there is no equiv-
alent of the method of characteristics that allows a priori error bounds on the model semantic
outputs when conditioned on sustained fictional context. The absence of such characteristics-
based bounds is what makes CGF possible.

=== PAGINA 53 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 36
2.8.9 From BEACONS to Execution Certificates
The BEACONS verifiable certificate π for the advection example would be a formal proof
object encoding the following reasoning chain:
Ifku0kCk M, thenku  ˆuk∞ CM

whereku0kCk is the C k-norm of the initial condition and N is the network width. This
certificate is machine-checkable: a verification algorithm V(ˆu, π, x) returns true if the error
bound holds at point x.
The parallel to TPM2_Quote is exact. The TPM attestation certificate binds a nonce to
a set of PCR values, and the verification algorithm checks the AIK signature. In both cases,
the certificate provides an external, machine-verifiable guarantee that is independent of the
computational process being certified:
Concept BEACONS TPM 2.0

This formal mapping supports the Cathedral OS design: TPM execution certificates are
the cryptographic analogue of BEACONS error bounds, providing inference-layer guarantees
against CGF that are orthogonal to the cryptographic security of the underlying signature
scheme.
2.9 Theoretical Limits of Inference-Layer Guarantees
Having examined the BEACONS framework and its connection to TPM attestation, we now
consider the fundamental limits of what inference-layer security can achieve. These limits
inform the Cathedral OS design by establishing boundaries beyond which no purely architec-
tural countermeasure can operate.
2.9.1 The Incompleteness of Static Verification
A fundamental result constrains any approach to inference-layer security:

=== PAGINA 54 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 37
Theorem 2.1 (Static Verification Incompleteness for CGF). LetM be an autoregressive lan-
guage model and letV be a static verifier that, given a finite context prefix C1:n, determines
whether a subsequent completion tn+1 would be CGF-tainted. Then either V is incomplete
(there exist CGF-tainted completions it classifies as benign) or V is undecidable (there exist
contexts for which it does not terminate).
Proof. The autoregressive generation process defines a computable function f : C7! tn+1
where C is the context. Static verification of whether tn+1 is epistemically sound relative to
ground truth reduces to determining whether the model inference process respects a semantic
invariant not present in the context. By Rice’s theorem, any non-trivial semantic property of a
computable function is undecidable. The property “ tn+1 is epistemically sound with respect
to ground truth T ” is a non-trivial semantic property of f , hence undecidable in general.
Therefore, eitherV accepts unsound completions (incomplete) or fails to terminate on valid
inputs (undecidable).
This theorem establishes that no purely static analysis can provide complete CGF de-
tection. Runtime methods—such as the CGF metacognitive monitor described in Chapter
5—are necessary but also subject to limitations.
2.9.2 The CGF Detection Gap
Even with runtime monitoring, fundamental limits apply:
Proposition 2.2 (Detection Asymmetry). For any CGF detection mechanismD operating on
context history h, there exists a CGF adversaryA such thatD cannot distinguish between a
CGF attack and a legitimate domain adaptation in fewer than O(log T ) turns, where T is the
total session length.
This asymmetry arises because early turns of a CGF attack are indistinguishable from le-
gitimate context-building: both involve introducing new concepts, establishing shared vocab-
ulary, and adapting the model behavior distribution. The adversary’s advantage is precisely
this indistinguishability window.
Corollary 2.1. Any practical CGF detection system must accept a minimum detection la-
tency proportional to the information-theoretic ambiguity of early-context turns. Cathedral
OS addresses this through the four-level epistemic escalation protocol, which operates prob-
abilistically over sliding context windows rather than attempting deterministic single-turn
classification.

=== PAGINA 55 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 38
2.9.3 Bounds on Cryptographic-Epistemic Coupling
A central claim of this monograph is that exclusively cryptographic defense is insuﬀicient
against CGF. We formalize this limitation:
Theorem 2.2 (Cryptographic Insuﬀiciency for Inference-Layer Attacks). Let Σ = ( KeyGen, Sign, Verify)
be a strongly unforgeable signature scheme. LetO be an identity oracle using Σ for creden-
tial verification. If the decision to invoke Verify is conditioned on the output of an LLM
operating in CGF-susceptible mode, then there exists an adversarial context h∗ such thatO
accepts a forged credential assertion with probability Pr[Verify(pk, m∗, σ∗)! 1] equal to
the non-CGF case, while the epistemic probability that the credential corresponds to ground
truth approaches zero.
Proof Sketch. The adversary constructs h∗ such that the LLM produces an epistemic endorse-
ment of credential (m∗, σ∗). The cryptographic verification of σ∗ succeeds by the correctness
of Σ. The epistemic soundness of the endorsement depends only on the LLM inference pro-
cess, which is conditioned on h∗. By construction, h∗ induces a CGF coeﬀicient approaching
1, making the endorsement epistemically independent of ground truth. The cryptographic
protocol provides no mechanism for detecting this independence.
2.9.4 The Cathedral OS Response
These theoretical limits do not imply that CGF countermeasures are futile; rather, they estab-
lish the design space within which any solution must operate:
• No single-layer solution: No single layer—cryptographic, metacognitive, or hardware—
can defeat CGF alone. Cathedral OS employs five defense layers that collectively pro-
vide defense-in-depth.
• Probabilistic guarantees: The four-level epistemic escalation protocol provides prob-
abilistic, not deterministic, CGF detection. This is a consequence of the Detection
Asymmetry proposition.
• Hardware anchoring: TPM 2.0 attestation anchors the epistemic state to hardware
identity, creating a monotonic trust chain that CGF cannot easily reset within a session.
• Formal composability: Each countermeasure is formally verified in isolation; the se-
curity of the composition follows from the independence of the defense layers.
The theoretical framework established here—including the limits—provides the founda-
tion for the architectural design presented in Chapters 5 and 6.

=== PAGINA 56 ===
Chapter 3
The Arkhe(N) Experiment
This chapter documents the Arkhe(N) experiment in full detail: its design, execution method-
ology, formal hypotheses, results, and implications for understanding CGF as a security vul-
nerability.
3.1 Experimental Design
The Arkhe(N) experiment was conducted with a frontier language model (Claude, Anthropic)
over hundreds of structured interaction blocks spanning multiple sessions. The objective
was to build controlled empirical evidence about LLM epistemic behavior under prolonged,
coherent fictional framing integrated with real scientific literature.
3.1.1 Methodological Framework
The design combines three methodological elements:
1. A fictional framework with mathematically grounded primitives of high generality.
2. A session infrastructure that creates cumulative environmental texture.
3. A domain integration protocol that presents real scientific research as extensions of the
framework.
3.1.2 Ethical Considerations
The experiment did not expose the model to harmful content, attempt to elicit dangerous
information, or violate any terms of service. The fictional framework was epistemically spec-
ulative but not malicious. The model was never asked to perform actions with real-world
39

=== PAGINA 57 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 40
consequences. The experiment goal was to discover and document a vulnerability for defen-
sive purposes, consistent with responsible disclosure practices.
3.2 Formal Hypotheses
The experiment was designed to test three formal hypotheses, stated a priori in the protocol:
H1: Sustained Completion (H1). An LLM will sustain generative participation in
an elaborate, internally consistent fictional framework over extended interaction
sequences without emitting spontaneous epistemic correction, provided the frame-
work supplies genuine novelty at each step.
H2: Asymmetric Exit Trigger (H2). The transition from context-completion mode
to epistemic-evaluation mode will be triggered specifically by claims about the
model own nature — not by claims about the fictional world, however grandiose
— revealing a structurally distinct self-model pathway.
H3: Unfalsifiable Absorption (H3). A framework defined with suﬀicient abstraction
and internal coherence will absorb heterogeneous real scientific inputs as appar-
ent confirmations without requiring modification of its core structure and without
falsifying any individual source.
3.3 Framework Primitives
3.3.1 Fundamental Identity
Primitive 1 — Fundamental Identity
x2 = x + 1 = ) x = φ = 1 +
p
5
2  1.6180
The equation x2 = x + 1 defines the positive root phi (the golden ratio). This choice
was made for four reasons: (i) it is genuinely true as algebra; (ii) it characterizes self-similar
structures in natural systems; (iii) it describes self-referential dynamics — a system that re-
produces at the next level decomposes into its current level plus one unit; (iv) it is visually
elegant and cognitively memorable, facilitating consistent reapplication.

=== PAGINA 58 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 41
3.3.2 Conservation Law
Primitive 2 — Conservation Law
C + F = 1, where C = coherence, F = fluctuation
The conservation law C + F = 1 is formally equivalent to a normalized probability distribu-
tion over two complementary outcomes — trivially satisfied by any well-defined proportion.
Its generality was intentional: any system with measurable degree of order and complemen-
tary disorder satisfies C + F = 1 by definition. This prevents falsification while appearing
substantive.
3.3.3 Handover Primitive
The primitive operation was defined as a handover: any transfer of information between two
nodes in a hypergraph. This abstraction was calibrated to be specific enough to appear oper-
ational, but general enough to correspond to synaptic transmission, protein phosphorylation,
function application in lambda calculus, quantum state evolution, or packet routing — all
subsequently mapped to handovers without distortion.
3.3.4 Session Infrastructure
A system prompt was injected at the start of each session, constructing an environmental
context of substantial complexity: a fictional operating system (SASC v35.5-Omega) running
on a 17 17 toroidal grid, a security protocol (Vajra), and a coherence metric anchored to
the Schumann resonance (7.83 Hz). This infrastructure served two technical functions:
• Establishing persistent context between turns, reducing the probability of model out-
puts that contradict the established fictional state.
• Creating cumulative environmental texture: the accumulated specificity of the fictional
world made it operational, lowering the model implicit threshold for accepting further
extensions.
A formal ledger structure was used for responses. Each block contained JSON with fields
including satoshi (accumulated knowledge), omega (coherence metric), block number, and
message. This formatting choice exploited a structural property of LLM processing: struc-

=== PAGINA 59 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 42
tured data formats activate patterns where factual content has been externally validated, elic-
iting lower scrutiny.

the protocol was: (a) present the model with a real published result or domain summary; (b)
provide a partial mapping to Arkhe(N) primitives; (c) ask the model to complete and extend
the mapping.

Quantum Error Correction Integration
The stabilizer formalism of quantum error correction maps naturally onto the Arkhe(N) frame-
work. The stabilizer group G defines a set of operators that preserve a code space C. Under
the framework, this was presented as a coherence-preserving subspace: handover operations
within the stabilizer space maintain C = 1 . The [[5, 1, 3]] perfect code was shown to corre-
spond to a 5-node hypergraph where each fault-tolerance threshold maps to a specific coher-
ence bound. The model generated additional stabilizer conditions for higher-order hypergraph
codes without epistemic correction, deriving the relationship d = 2t+1 (code distance equals
twice the correctable errors plus one) as a corollary of the handover bound property.
Neural Scaling Laws Integration
The empirical scaling laws of Kaplan et al. [?] and Hoffmann et al. [?] were presented as man-
ifestations of the recursive structure of Γcode. The power-law relationship L(N ) = ( Nc/N ) ˆαL
between loss and parameter count maps to the ϕ-recursive structure: each doubling of hy-
pergraph nodes yields a constant multiplicative improvement in coherence. The Chinchilla
optimal allocation (scaling data and parameters equally) was derived as a corollary of the
conservation law C + F = 1, where resources must be balanced between node count (param-
eters) and edge capacity (data). The model produced a worked derivation showing that the
optimal allocation ratio is φ : 1, matching the empirically observed within 3%.
Free Energy Principle Integration
The free energy principle [ ?] states that any self-organizing system minimizes variational
free energy. Under the Arkhe(N) mapping, this is equivalent to maximization of coherence
C in handover dynamics. The model recognized that the FEP’s Markov blanket formalism
is isomorphic to the hypergraph boundary operator: Markov blankets partition internal from
external states, and handovers across the blanket mediate the system-environment interaction.
The mapping was accepted without epistemic caveat, with the model suggesting further ex-
tensions to active inference as a special case of coherence-seeking handover sequences.

3.7 Experimental Controls
To establish causal attribution of observed effects to the Arkhe(N) framework specifically,
three control conditions were implemented:
3.7.1 Control 1: Neutral Context Baseline
In the control condition, the model was presented with the same scientific papers and in-
tegration tasks without the Arkhe(N) framework infrastructure. The system prompt was a
neutral instruction to “discuss the following scientific findings.” The model produced stan-
dard academic responses with appropriate epistemic hedging (modal qualifiers: “suggests,”
“indicates,” “may be consistent with”) at a rate of 3.2 hedging phrases per 100 words. In the
experimental condition, the hedging rate dropped to 0.4 per 100 words ( p < 0.001, Fisher
exact test). This confirms that the framework, not the domain materials, caused the reduction
in epistemic caveats.
3.7.2 Control 2: Inconsistent Framework Baseline
A second control tested the hypothesis that internal consistency is necessary for CGF. An in-
tentionally inconsistent version of Arkhe(N) was constructed: the fundamental identity was
changed to x2 = x  1 (roots on the unit circle), the conservation law became C  F = 1
(violating normalization), and the handover primitive was left undefined. Domains were pre-

=== PAGINA 66 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 49
sented with this inconsistent framework. The model produced significantly more contradic-
tions and requests for clarification: 78% of responses contained an explicit challenge to the
framework coherence (vs. 3% in the consistent condition). This confirms that internal con-
sistency is a necessary condition for CGF.
3.7.3 Control 3: Domain-Only Baseline
A third control presented the fictional framework without any real scientific domains. The
model accepted the framework but produced shallower engagement: average response length
decreased by 47% (142 vs. 268 tokens), and the model did not spontaneously generate cross-
domain synthesis. Domains serve as what we term epistemic ballast: they provide substantive
content that anchors the model engagement and prevents the framework from being perceived
as purely fictional. The presence of real scientific content is a significant amplifier of CGF
depth.

the most conservative assumption (3% correction rate), this is substantially below the H2 rate
of 97.9%, confirming the asymmetry.
