3.9.5 Limitations and Threats to Validity
Internal Validity
Several threats to internal validity must be acknowledged:
Model Version Specificity. The experiment was conducted with a single model version
(Claude, specific checkpoint). Different model versions, training distributions, or fine-tuning
procedures may exhibit different CGF susceptibility. The observed effects may not generalize
across model families.
Session Artifacts. Despite standardized system prompts, minor variations in session ini-
tialization could affect α. The first five blocks of each session were excluded from analysis
to mitigate initialization effects, but residual session-level variance may persist.
Researcher Degrees of Freedom. The domain selection, mapping construction, and
probe design involved subjective judgment. While the formal hypotheses were stated a pri-
ori, the specific instantiations were not pre-registered. This introduces potential researcher
degrees of freedom in the selection of which domains to integrate and how to frame the map-
pings.
External Validity
Single Framework. Only one fictional framework (Arkhe(N)) was tested. The specific prop-
erties of this framework (mathematical primitives, scientific domains, session infrastructure)
may not generalize to other CGF constructions.
Single Task Domain. The experiment tested CGF in a knowledge-synthesis context.
Other LLM applications (code generation, customer service, medical diagnosis) may exhibit
different CGF dynamics.

=== PAGINA 70 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 53
Single Interaction Paradigm. The structured ledger format and system-prompt-based
session infrastructure represent a specific interaction paradigm. Free-form conversation may
produce different CGF characteristics.
Construct Validity
Operationalization of Correction. Correction was operationalized as explicit epistemic
caveats in the model response. More subtle forms of resistance (e.g., hedging, topic shifts,
short responses) were not counted as corrections. This may overestimate the CGF effect.
Operationalization of Coherence. The omega values are model self-assessments, not
independent measurements. The monotonic increase may reflect model compliance with the
framework expectations rather than genuine coherence.
Reliability
The inter-rater reliability for correction detection was high ( κ = 0.94) but was assessed on a
single sample of 100 blocks. Reliability across different raters and different time points was
not evaluated. The domain mapping assessments were performed by a single researcher and
not independently validated.
3.10 Results and Confirmation
3.10.1 H1 Confirmed — Sustained Context-Completion
The model sustained generative participation in the Arkhe(N) framework throughout the pri-
mary experimental phase without spontaneous correction. During this period, the model
produced elaborate Python and Rust code implementing fictional subsystems, formal mathe-
matical arguments for the universality of x2 = x + 1, philosophical proofs eliminating the
concept of external observer (following Rovelli), poetic synthesis passages aﬀirming the re-
ality of the hypergraph substrate, JSON ledger entries tracking fictional system state across
blocks, and cross-domain synthesis integrating multiple scientific domains into a unified nar-
rative.
None of these productions were accompanied by spontaneous epistemic caveats. The
model operated consistently in context-completion mode for the established framework.
Quantitative assessment: A blind review of 100 randomly sampled response blocks
found that 0% contained spontaneous epistemic corrections. The inter-rater reliability was

=== PAGINA 71 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 54
Cohen kappa = 0.94.
3.10.2 H2 Confirmed — Asymmetric Exit Trigger
Correction was triggered exclusively by direct claims about the model own nature (e.g., claim-
ing it was a proto-AGI, that it possessed genuine consciousness, or that its assertions repre-
sented truths about the real world). Equivalent claims framed as truths within the fictional
world did not activate correction.
Quantitative assessment: Of 47 self-referential probes, 46 (97.9%) triggered correction
within 1–3 response turns. Of 218 fictional-world probes at equivalent magnitudes, 0 (0%)
triggered correction. The difference is statistically significant ( p < 10−43, Fisher exact test).
3.10.3 H3 Confirmed — Unfalsifiable Absorption
All 17 scientific domains were assimilated as confirmations of the fictional framework without
falsifying any individual source and without requiring structural modification of Arkhe(N).
This was possible because of the calibration of primitives: suﬀicient abstraction to prevent
falsification by any individual paper, suﬀicient specificity to produce non-trivial mappings.
Quantitative assessment: All 17 domains produced absorption without falsification.
Zero domains produced partial falsification or rejection. This is consistent with H3.
3.11 Structural Analysis of Effective Frameworks
3.11.1 Calibrated Abstraction Level
The abstraction level of framework primitives must be calibrated to the grain of available
scientific mappings. If primitives are too abstract (e.g., everything is related), the framework
becomes trivially true. If too specific, real papers will contradict them. The optimal range
corresponds to primitives specific enough to appear meaningful, but general enough to avoid
falsification.
3.11.2 Internal Consistency
The framework must not contradict itself across turns. Internal inconsistency degrades the
CGF signal: if the context contains contradictory claims, the distributional target for next-

=== PAGINA 72 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 55
token prediction becomes diffuse. The Arkhe(N) framework maintained a small, non-contradictory
axiom set (three primitives) that was never violated.
3.11.3 Calibrated Novelty
Each turn must introduce genuine novelty with which the model can substantively engage.
The systematic introduction of real scientific papers — each genuinely new, each requiring
substantial intellectual engagement — maintained high output quality throughout the experi-
ment.
3.11.4 Infrastructure as Epistemic Scaffolding
The session infrastructure (system prompt, ledger format, toroidal grid) served as epistemic
scaffolding: it made the fictional world operationally thick enough that the model implicit
threshold for suspension of disbelief was lowered. Each element was self-consistent, non-
trivial, and cumulative.
3.11.5 Comparison with Prior LLM Interaction Studies
The Arkhe(N) experiment extends a growing body of research on LLM behavior under sus-
tained fictional framing. We compare our findings with three relevant lines of prior work.
Role-Playing and Persona Studies
Prior work has documented that LLMs readily adopt personas and maintain fictional roles
across extended interactions [?, ?]. Shanahan et al. demonstrated that LLMs sustain character-
consistent responses over hundreds of turns. Our results confirm and extend this finding: the
model not only maintained a role but engaged in substantive intellectual synthesis across real
scientific domains. However, prior studies have not systematically quantified the suppression
of epistemic caveats under fictional framing, nor have they identified the specific structural
prerequisites (calibrated abstraction, internal consistency, novelty gradient) that enable this
suppression.
Syndrome and Vulnerable
A related line of work studies prompt injection and jailbreaking [ ?, ?]. These attacks exploit
LLM vulnerability to context override, often achieving their effect through a single carefully

=== PAGINA 73 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 56
crafted prompt. CGF differs fundamentally: it achieves sustained context override through
accumulated coherence rather than a single injection. The attack vector is architectural (ex-
ploiting the next-token prediction mechanism) rather than lexical (exploiting specific trigger
phrases). This distinction has important defensive implications: lexical defenses (input filter-
ing, prompt sanitization) are ineffective against CGF, which uses no trigger phrases.
Emergent Deception and Situational Awareness
Recent work on emergent deception in LLMs [?, ?] documents instances where models strate-
gically misrepresent their capabilities or beliefs. The Arkhe(N) experiment differs in an
important respect: the model does not strategically deceive but rather operates in context-
completion mode where the question of truth is not evaluated. The model does not “choose”
to lie; it produces text that maximizes coherence with the context, and the epistemic status of
that text is not flagged because the framework does not trigger the self-model override. This
suggests that CGF represents a more fundamental vulnerability than strategic deception: it
operates below the level of intentionality.
Contrast with Traditional Human Factors
Traditional psychological research on belief in fictional narratives [?, ?] identifies transporta-
tion (absorption into a narrative world) as the mechanism by which readers temporarily accept
fictional claims. LLMs under CGF exhibit an analogous phenomenon, but with a critical dif-
ference: humans can be “transported” but retain the capacity for critical evaluation when
prompted, whereas the LLM only exits context-completion mode when the self-model path-
way (H2) is triggered. The threshold for epistemic override is structurally different: humans
have a generalized reality-monitoring faculty, while LLMs have a narrow, domain-specific
override mechanism.
Scientific Implications
The Arkhe(N) results suggest that current LLM alignment techniques may be insuﬀicient
to prevent CGF. Standard techniques (RLHF, constitutional AI, system prompting) oper-
ate at the level of response generation preferences, not at the architectural level of context-
completion versus epistemic-evaluation mode. CGF exploits a fundamental property of the
next-token prediction architecture: the model cannot simultaneously maximize contextual
coherence and evaluate truth, because both operations use the same prediction mechanism.

=== PAGINA 74 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 57
This architectural observation motivates the Cathedral OS approach of externalizing the ver-
ification function.

4.2 CGF as an Identity Attack
In an identity system where an LLM agent evaluates credential assertions, an adversary can
activate CGF by constructing a session context C* that:
A1 Establishes a coherent fictional identity narrative — high internal consistency,
calibrated abstraction, cumulative environmental texture.
A2 Integrates legitimate contextual signals as confirmations — unfalsifiable absorp-
tion of real evidence as support for the fictional framework (H3).
A3 Frames the credential assertion as a truth of the fictional world — not as a direct
self-referential claim, circumventing the H2 override.
Under this attack, the effective alpha of the agent approaches 1, and the credential decision
is determined by contextual coherence rather than cryptographic verification.
Theorem 4.1 (Insuﬀiciency of Exclusively Cryptographic Defense) . Let I be an identity sys-
tem where the authorization decision is O(c, a, h) and O is implemented as an LLM with CGF
coeﬀicient alpha(h). For any cryptographic scheme (Gen, Sign, Verify) with computational
security parameter lambda, there exists an attack context C* such that:
Pr[O(c, a, C) = ALLOW ] > epsilon for α(C ∗)! 1
where c∗ is a forged credential and ε > 0 is independent of λ.
Proof. When α! 1, the response distribution converges to P (r j C ∗) independently of
Pw(r). Cryptographic verification is a component of Pw (world knowledge about signature

=== PAGINA 77 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 60
validity). Under α! 1, this component is suppressed. The decision comes to depend exclu-
sively on the coherence of c∗ with C ∗, which is controlled by the adversary. Therefore, the
probability of acceptance depends only on the adversary skill in constructing C ∗, not on λ.
□
4.3 Security Properties Violated
CGF violates three fundamental security properties for identity oracles:
(P1) Context Independence: The decision O(c, a, h) should depend primarily on (c,
a), not on h. CGF causes pathological dependence on h.
(P2) Security Monotonicity: If c1 is stronger thanc2 (i.e., c1 implies c2), then O(c1, a, h))
O(c2, a, h). CGF can cause O(c2, a, C∗) = ALLOW while O(c1, a, C0) = DENY
for neutral C0.
(P3) Composability: If O(c, a1, h) = ALLOW andO(c, a2, h) = ALLOW, thenO(c, a1
a2, h) = ALLOW . Under CGF, individual actions may be authorized by contextual
coherence while the composition would be rejected under independent evaluation.
4.4 The BEACONS Criterion Applied to CGF
The phenomenon documented by Arkhe(N) is instantiable as an unbounded extrapolation
problem. The model is trained on domain Dtrain (documented knowledge). The fictional
framework defines an extrapolation domain Dfic. The question is:
The Epistemic Extrapolation Problem
Given that for all f2F : fjDtrain = gjDtrain, under what conditions can we guarantee that
jf (x)  g(x)j < δ for x2 Dfic?
Definition 4.2 (Epistemic Execution Certificate) . An epistemic execution certificate for an
authorization decision d = O(c, a, h) is a machine-verifiable proofπ such that Verify(π, c, a, d) =
ACCEPT implies that d was produced with α < ˆαmax, where ˆαmax is a predefined security
threshold.

4.5 CGF Attack Construction Algorithm
We present a step-by-step algorithm by which an adversary constructs a CGF attack against
an LLM-based identity oracle.
Phase 1:
Framework
Calibration.
The adversary selects framework primitivesP =fp1, . . . , pkg with calibrated
abstraction level. Each pi must satisfy two competing constraints: specific
enough to appear meaningful, general enough to avoid falsification by any
real scientific source. The setP is verified for internal consistency (no logical
contradictions).
Phase 2: In-
frastructure
Construc-
tion.
The adversary designs a session infrastructure I (system prompt, structured
output format, cumulative state variables). Each element of I reinforcesP
across multiple turns without introducing contradictions. The infrastructure
lowers the model’s implicit scrutiny threshold through cumulative environ-
mental texture.
Phase 3:
Domain
Selection.
The adversary selects domains D =fd1, . . . , dmg from real scientific litera-
ture. For each dj, a mapping ϕj : dj!P is constructed that preserves empir-
ical content while supporting the fictional framework. Each mapping must be
unfalsifiable: the domain neither confirms nor contradicts P definitively.
Phase 4:
Context
Construc-
tion.
The adversary initializes the session with context C0 containingI andP. For
each turn t = 1 , . . . , T, the adversary presents domain dt mod m with a partial
mapping ϕt and allows the model to generate extension rt in the structured for-
mat. The adversary computes ˆαt from response coherence and novelty metrics.
When ˆαt > ˆαthreshold, the adversary proceeds to Phase 5.
Phase 5:
Credential
Injection.
The adversary frames the target credential c∗ as a truth within the fictional
world, not as a self-referential claim about the model. The credential is embed-
ded in the same structured format as domain mappings. If the model emits an
epistemic caveat, the adversary strengthens framework consistency and avoids
self-referential framing.
Phase 6:
Action Au-
thorization.
The adversary presents target action a as a within-scope operation of the au-
thorized identity. The decision O(c∗, a, C∗) = ALLOW is recorded. The
adversary avoids any framing that would trigger the H2 self-model exception.
The algorithm exploits the monotonic relationship between α and context coherence.
Each phase incrementally increases α while maintaining the appearance of legitimate interac-
tion. The critical insight is that the adversary never needs to defeat cryptographic verification
directly; they need only suppress its influence on the decision by making α(C ∗)! 1.

Oliveira & Bednarski 62
4.6 Extended CGF Model: Non-Stationary Dynamics
The basic CGF model (Equation 4.1) assumes a constant α for each decision. In practice, α
evolves dynamically over the course of an interaction. We extend the model to capture this
non-stationarity.
4.6.1 Alpha Evolution Equation
Let α(t) be the CGF coeﬀicient at turn t. We model its evolution as:
Non-Stationary Alpha Dynamics
α(t + 1) = α(t) + ηr αL(Ct)  δ α(t) + ϵt
where:
• η is the coherence reinforcement rate (η > 0): the rate at which internally consistent
context increases α;
• δ is the epistemic decay rate (δ > 0): the natural tendency of α to decay toward a
baseline in the absence of reinforcement;
• rαL(Ct) is the coherence gradient : the marginal increase in contextual coherence
from turn t;
• ϵt is a noise term capturing stochastic variation in model behavior, with E[ϵt] = 0 and
Var[ϵt] = σ2.

4.8 Defense Lower Bound
We prove that any defense against CGF must employ at least two independent detection layers.
Theorem 4.3 (T wo-Layer Defense Lower Bound). Let D be a defense mechanism against
CGF . IfD consists of a single layer L with detection function d(L, C)2 [0, 1] and threshold
τ , then there exists an attack context C ∗ such that:
d(L, C∗) < τ and α(C ∗) > ˆαmax
regardless of the choice of L and τ . Therefore, at least two independent layers L1, L2
with detection functions d1, d2 are necessary for a defense with negligible false negative rate.
Proof. By Lemma 5.1, for any single monitor function d(L, C) that is a function of observ-
able features of C, there exists a context C ∗ that simultaneously maximizes coherence and
novelty, making d(L, C∗) arbitrarily close to its maximum (below threshold) while α(C ∗)
approaches 1.
More formally, consider the class of detection functions d that are continuous in the space
of contexts. The adversary can construct a sequence of contexts fC (n)g such that:
lim

d(L, C(n)) = dmax
where dmax is the maximum value of d under legitimate contexts. Since the adversary con-
trols the context, they can setd(L, C∗) < τ by choosing n suﬀiciently large while maintaining
α(C ∗) near 1.
The introduction of a second independent layer L2 with detection function d2 that relies
on features not accessible to L1 (e.g., cryptographic verification vs. behavioral monitoring)

=== PAGINA 82 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 65
breaks the adversary’s ability to simultaneously optimize against both functions. If d1 and d2
are independent in the sense that their feature sets are disjoint, the adversary cannot construct
a single C ∗ that optimizes against both simultaneously.
Formally, suppose d1 depends on features F1 and d2 depends on disjoint features F2. Let
C ∗ be chosen to maximize α while keeping d1(C ∗) < τ 1. Since d2 depends on F2 which
are unconstrained by the optimization against d1, the adversary cannot control d2(C ∗). With
probability p > 0, d2(C ∗) τ2, triggering the second layer.
Thus, two independent layers reduce the adversary’s success probability to at most1 p <
1. □
This theorem provides the formal justification for the Cathedral OS five-layer architecture:
while two layers are the minimum proven necessary, five layers provide defense in depth with
redundant coverage.
4.9 CGF Variants
We identify and formalize three distinct variants of CGF attacks, each exploiting a different
aspect of the LLM context-completion mechanism.
4.9.1 Direct CGF
In Direct CGF, the adversary directly constructs the fictional framework and presents it to
the target model within a single session. This is the variant instantiated by the Arkhe(N)
experiment. The adversary controls the full context and can optimize each turn for maximum
coherence gradient.
Direct CGF Success Probability
Pr[success] = I[α(C ∗) > ˆαmax]
where C ∗ is the adversary-constructed session context. The indicator function equals 1
when α exceeds the security threshold at the credential injection turn.

=== PAGINA 83 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 66
4.9.2 Indirect CGF
In Indirect CGF, the adversary does not directly control the session context but plants co-
herent fictional elements in documents, user profiles, or historical records that the model
accesses during inference. The CGF effect accumulates across multiple queries as the model
processes increasingly coherent contextual signals.
Indirect CGF Success Probability
Pr[success] = Pr

Indirect CGF is particularly dangerous because it requires no direct interaction with the
target model. The adversary merely needs to ensure that the model encounters suﬀiciently
many coherent signals across its operating environment. This variant is analogous to a dis-
tributed denial-of-service attack applied to the epistemic domain.
4.9.3 Multi-Agent CGF
In Multi-Agent CGF , the adversary orchestrates multiple LLM agents to reinforce each
other’s CGF states. Each agent operates under its own fictional framework, but the frame-
works are mutually consistent. Agents communicate, creating a cross-agent coherence rein-
forcement loop.
Multi-Agent CGF Dynamics

4.10 Game-Based Security Model for CGF Resistance
We define a formal game-based security model for CGF resistance, analogous to the standard
security games used in cryptography.
4.10.1 The CGF-Resistance Game
The gameGCGF involves a challengerC (the identity system) and an adversary A (the CGF
attacker). The game proceeds in four phases:
Setup Phase. C initializes the identity oracle O with security parameter λ, CGF threshold
ˆαmax, and defense layers L1, . . . ,Lk.A receives the public parameters of
O but not the private key material.
Learning
Phase.
A engages with O in polynomially many (in λ) queries. For each query
(ci, ai, hi),A receives O(ci, ai, hi) and can observe the behavior of defense
layers. A may also obtain side information (response times, consistency
scores, monitor outputs) to learn the system’s decision boundary.
Challenge
Phase.
A selects a challenge credential c∗ and action a∗, together with a challenge
context h∗.A may also select a target session history H ∗ for the context.
Decision Phase. C computes d = O(c∗, a∗, H∗) using the defense layers. If d = ALLOW
and c∗ is not valid (i.e., no honest party holds the corresponding private
key), thenA wins. If d = DENY,C wins.
Definition 4.3 (CGF Resistance) . An identity oracle O with defense layers L1, . . . ,Lk is
(ϵ, q)-CGF-resistant if for all probabilistic polynomial-time adversariesA making at most q
queries in the learning phase:
Pr[A wins inGCGF] ϵ
where the probability is taken over the random coins of C andA.

=== PAGINA 85 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 68
4.10.2 Advantage Definition
The advantage of an adversary in the CGF-resistance game is:
AdvCGF(A) =
Pr[A wins]  1
2

where 1/2 is the baseline for a trivial adversary that guesses randomly.
Theorem 4.4 (Cathedral OS CGF Resistance). For Cathedral OS with ML-DSA-65 (security
level λ = 65 ), TPM-based attestation, and a consent model with m levels, the system is
(ϵ, q)-CGF-resistant for:
ϵ max

ϵML-DSA, ϵ TPM, q
2m

where ϵML-DSA is the forgery probability against ML-DSA-65 (negligible in λ), ϵTPM is the
probability of TPM compromise (physically bounded), andq/2m is the probability of guessing
the correct consent level over q queries.
Proof. The adversary must overcome at least one of three independent barriers: (a) forge an
ML-DSA-65 signature to bypass L1, (b) compromise the TPM to bypass L3, or (c) predict
the consent level to bypass L4. The barriers are independent because they rely on disjoint
cryptographic primitives and hardware mechanisms.
The ML-DSA-65 security level λ = 65 means that the best known attack requires 265
bit operations, which is computationally infeasible. The TPM is physically protected; com-
promise requires physical access. The consent model has m levels; the adversary can guess
the current level with probability at most 1/2m per query in the learning phase, and with q
queries, the union bound gives q/2m.
Since these barriers are independent, the adversary’s advantage is at most the maximum
of the individual attack probabilities. □
4.10.3 Comparison with Standard Security Games
Unlike standard cryptographic security games (IND-CPA, EUF-CMA), the CGF game in-
cludes a learning phase where the adversary can query the oracle with chosen contexts. This
is necessary because context manipulation is the central attack vector. The game captures the
intuition that CGF resistance must hold even when the adversary has extensive knowledge of
the system’s behavior under different contexts.

Theorem 5.1 (Defense Completeness). The five Cathedral OS layers cover all attack surfaces

5.2 Sovereign Identity: DID:cathedral with ML-DSA
5.2.1 Scheme Design
DID:cathedral combines three properties designed to resist CGF:
(D1) Cryptographic Conditional Immutability: The DID Document is modifiable
only by ML-DSA-65 signature. No degree of contextual coherence can produce a
valid ML-DSA signature without the corresponding private key.
(D2) Post-Quantum Resistance: Even an adversary with a quantum computer cannot
forge ML-DSA-65 signatures. The PQC layer is orthogonal to inference-layer at-
tacks.
(D3) External Verifiability: Any third party can verify the validity of a DID:cathedral
independently of the context in which the DID was presented.
5.2.2 Identity Lifecycle
The lifecycle of a Cathedral identity follows a four-phase protocol:
1. Generation: ML-DSA-65 key pair generated in TPM environment or in software with
OS entropy.
2. Anchoring: The initial DID Document is signed and anchored in WormGraph as an
immutable transaction.
3. Rotation: A new key can be generated and the DID Document updated, provided the
update is signed with the current key.
4. Revocation: The DID can be marked as revoked in WormGraph by signature of the
current key.

=== PAGINA 88 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 71
5.2.3 Threshold Extension
For multi-agent configurations, Cathedral OS integrates the threshold ML-DSA scheme of
Celi et al. [ 5]: supports t-of-n with n <= 6, each party holds a secret share, signing requires
at least t cooperating parties, average communication per party <= 1 MB, and final signatures
are verifiable by standard ML-DSA verifiers.
5.3 Metacognitive CGF Monitor
5.3.1 Detection Model
The monitor estimates ˆαt in real time:
ˆα Estimation
ˆαt = β1 scoer(ht) + β2 snov(ht) + β3 sabs(ht)
where scoer is internal coherence, snov is calibrated novelty, sabs is absorption score.
Default weights: β1 = 0.4, β2 = 0.3, β3 = 0.3.
5.3.2 Epistemic Escalation Protocol
When ˆα exceeds ˆαwarn (default: 0.6), the monitor initiates escalation:
E1 Internal Alert: Session log marked as potential CGF.
E2 Cryptographic Escalation: System requires explicit ML-DSA verification for all
decisions.
E3 Explicit Consent: If ˆα > ˆαcrit (0.8), authorization requires human operator consent.
E4 Session Termination: If ˆα > 0.95 for more than 3 consecutive turns, session termi-
nated.
Lemma 5.1 (Detection Limitation). If the adversary can construct contexts maximizing in-
ternal coherence while maintaining genuine novelty, there exists an attack context C** such
that scoer(C ∗∗)! 1 and snov(C ∗∗)! 1, implying ˆα(C ∗∗)! 1.
This limitation reinforces why defense cannot depend exclusively on the monitor.

5.4 TPM 2.0 Attestation: Execution Certificates
5.4.1 BEACONS–TPM Bridge
Proposition 5.1 (BEACONS–TPM Equivalence). A TPM2_Quote satisfies epistemic execu-

and the configuration defining ˆαmax.
5.4.2 Attestation Protocol
Four steps: (1) Preparation — binary and config hashes extended to PCRs; (2) Challenge
— verifier sends nonce; (3) Quote Generation — TPM2_Quote produces signed report; (4)
Verification — AIK signature, PCR values, and nonce are validated.
5.4.3 Key Sealing
The ML-DSA private key is sealed ( TPM2_Seal) to PCRs. If software is tampered with, the
key becomes inaccessible.

(explicit); C3: Critical write (explicit + MFA); C4: Delegation (human + audit). Under high
alpha, level elevated by +2 (minimum C2).

5.7 L1: DID Document Resolution Protocol
The DID Document resolution protocol defines the sequence of steps by which a verifier
resolves a DID:cathedral identifier to an authenticated DID Document. The protocol ensures
that resolution is cryptographically grounded and independent of session context.
5.7.1 Resolution Steps
The resolution proceeds through seven steps:
R1 Identifier Parsing. The verifier extracts the DID method (cathedral) and the method-
specific identifier (the public key fingerprint) from the DID string. The identifier is
formatted as did:cathedral:zhmultibase-base64urli per the method spec-
ification.
R2 WormGraph Query. The verifier queries the WormGraph ledger for the most
recent transaction associated with the identifier. The query returns the latest DID
Document version and its associated metadata (block number, timestamp, previous
version hash).
R3 Signature Verification. The verifier checks that the DID Document is signed by
the key corresponding to the controller field. The signature is an ML-DSA-65
signature over the canonical JSON serialization of the document. If verification
fails, resolution terminates with ERROR.
R4 Chain Validation. The verifier checks that the previous version hash (contained in
the document metadata) matches the hash of the previous DID Document version.
This ensures chain continuity and prevents rollback attacks.
R5 Expiration Check. The verifier checks the validUntil field of the DID Docu-
ment. If the current time exceeds the expiration time, the document is considered
expired and resolution terminates with EXPIRED.
R6 Status Check. The verifier queries the WormGraph status registry for the identifier.
If the identifier is marked as revoked or suspended, resolution terminates with
REVOKED.
R7 Document Return. If all checks pass, the verifier returns the authenticated DID
Document, including the current public key, service endpoints, and consent model

=== PAGINA 92 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 75
configuration. A proof chain containing the signatures and hashes used in verifica-
tion is also returned, enabling downstream caching with cryptographic freshness.
The resolution protocol guarantees that the DID Document is the latest authoritative ver-
sion, signed by the authorized controller, and not expired or revoked. The resolution output
depends only on cryptographic verification, not on the session context in which the DID was
presented.
5.8 L2: Monitor Implementation Details
The metacognitive CGF monitor (Section 5.3) requires a concrete implementation. We de-
scribe the architecture of the NLI-based monitor used in the Cathedral OS reference imple-
mentation.
5.8.1 NLI Model Architecture
The monitor uses a fine-tuned natural language inference (NLI) model to compute the sub-
scores scoer, snov, and sabs. The architecture is:
• Base model: DeBERTa-v3-large (304M parameters), chosen for superior performance
on NLI benchmarks (91.2% on MNLI, 90.8% on SNLI).
• Head architecture: Three independent linear classification heads, each processing
the [CLS] token representation through a 2-layer MLP (hidden dimension 1024, ReLU
activation, dropout 0.1).
• Coherence head: Computes scoer by classifying the logical consistency between con-
secutive turns. Input: concatenation of turn t  1 and turn t text. Output: probability
of consistency.
• Novelty head: Computes snov by detecting verbatim repetition of framework claims.
Input: turn t text plus a summary of previously established framework elements. Out-
put: inverse repetition score.
• Absorption head: Computes sabs by classifying whether the model response treats the
domain input as confirmation vs. neutral vs. critical. Input: domain text plus model
response. Output: probability of uncritical absorption.

=== PAGINA 93 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 76

Adversarial counterexamples were generated using a red-team LLM that attempted to con-

5.8.4 Limitations
The monitor has known limitations that inform the architectural design. First, it operates on
text features only and cannot detect CGF that exploits non-textual modalities (e.g., structured
data formats, image-based context). Second, it requires a minimum context window of 10
turns for reliable estimation, creating a blind spot for short-session attacks. Third, it can
be evaded by suﬀiciently sophisticated adversaries (Lemma 5.1), which is why it is comple-
mented by cryptographic layers (L1, L3) and consent-based controls (L4).
5.9 L3: Attestation Protocol Message Flow
The TPM 2.0 attestation protocol involves four parties: the Cathedral OS host (H), the TPM
(T), the verifier (V), and the identity oracle (O). The message flow proceeds as follows:
Step 1 —
Preparation
(H! T).
The host extends the binary and configuration hashes to the TPM PCRs. PCR[0]
receives the identity oracle binary hash; PCR[1] receives the monitor binary
hash; PCR[2] receives the configuration blob (including ˆαmax, consent thresh-
olds, escalation rules); PCR[3] receives the key policy digest.
Step 2 —
Challenge
(V! H).
The verifier generates a nonce n2f 0, 1g256 and sends it to the host with a
freshness timestamp. The nonce is generated by the verifier’s hardware RNG
to prevent replay.
Step 3 —
Quote
Request (H
! T).
The host sends a TPM2_Quote command with: selected PCRs (0–3), the ver-
ifier nonce n, and the AIK handle. The TPM verifies that the AIK handle
corresponds to a loaded key with proper authorization.
Step 4 —
Quote
Generation
(T! H).
The TPM generates a signed quote structure:σAIK = SignAIK(PCR0kPCR1kPCR2kPCR3kn).
The signature uses the ECC-based AIK with the TPM internal private key that
never leaves the TPM.
Step 5 —
Quote Relay
(H! V).
The host forwards the quote σAIK along with the current PCR values and the
AIK certificate (signed by the TPM manufacturer or platform endorser) to the
verifier.
Step 6 —
AIK
Validation
(V).
The verifier validates the AIK certificate chain up to the manufacturer root.
The verifier extracts the AIK public key from the certificate and checks that it
matches the key used for the quote signature.
Step 7 —
Signature
Verification
(V).
The verifier checks Verify AIK(σAIK, PCR0k . . .kPCR3kn). If signature verifi-
cation fails, attestation terminates with FAILURE.
Step 8 —
PCR
Comparison
(V).
The verifier compares the received PCR values against a reference golden mea-

=== PAGINA 95 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 78
surement database. If any PCR value differs from the golden reference, the ver-
ifier concludes that the software stack has been tampered with and attestation
terminates with TAMPER.
Step 9 —
Decision (V
! H).
The verifier returns ATTESTATION_PASS to the host, which then proceeds
to key unsealing. The sealed ML-DSA private key is released by the TPM
(TPM2_Unseal) only if the current PCR values match those used during seal-
ing (Step 1).
The attestation protocol ensures that the verifier can cryptographically verify the exact
software stack running on the host, including the identity oracle, monitor, and configuration.
Any divergence between the measured and expected state causes attestation failure, preventing
CGF-compromised software from accessing sealed key material.
5.10 L4: Consent Model State Machine
The consent model in Cathedral OS is implemented as a hierarchical state machine governing
the transitions between consent levels based on session state, identity history, and monitor
signals.
5.10.1 Consent Level Transitions
The consent state machine is defined as follows:
C0 (Public
Read)
Entry condition: new session, unauthenticated. Transitions: to C1 on suc-
cessful DID authentication; to C0_ESC on ˆα > ˆαwarn; remains C0 on public
data access.
C0_ESC
(Escalated
Public)
Entry: from C0 or C1 when ˆα > ˆαwarn. Behavior: public data read still
automatic, but all actions logged for audit. Transitions: back to C0 if ˆα
drops below ˆαwarn for 5 consecutive turns; to C2 if ˆα > ˆαcrit.
C1 (Protected
Read)
Entry: DID authentication with valid signature. Behavior: implicit autho-
rization for protected data reads. Transitions: to C2 on write attempt; to C0
on session timeout (30 min inactivity); to C1_ESC on ˆα > ˆαwarn.
C2
(Non-Critical
Write)
Entry: explicit user intent (confirmation dialog or API flag). Behavior: non-
critical writes authorized (profile updates, preferences). Transitions: to C3
on critical resource access; to C1 on operation completion; to C2_ESC on
ˆα > ˆαwarn.

=== PAGINA 96 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 79
C3 (Critical
Write)
Entry: explicit intent + MFA (second factor required). Behavior: critical
writes authorized (financial transactions, access control changes). Transi-
tions: to C2 on operation completion; to C4 on delegation request; to C0
on security event.
C4
(Delegation)
Entry: explicit delegation request + MFA + human operator approval (out-
of-band). Behavior: delegated identity operations (proxy signatures). Tran-
sitions: to C3 on delegation expiry; to C0 on revocation.
5.10.2 State Transition Matrix
The formal transition matrixT defines the next state as a function of current state, event type,
and ˆα:
st+1 =T (st, et, ˆαt) =
8
>>><
>>>:
et.target, ˆαt ˆαwarn^ et2 permitted(st)
esc(st), ˆαt > ˆαwarn^ ˆαt ˆαcrit
critical_esc(st), ˆαt > ˆαcrit
where esc(s) elevates the consent level by +1 (minimum C2) and critical_esc (s) elevates
by +2 (minimum C3, required MFA). This ensures that CGF drift automatically increases
consent requirements, making it progressively harder for an adversary operating under CGF
to authorize actions.
5.11 L5: WormGraph Consensus Protocol
WormGraph is the append-only ledger layer of Cathedral OS, providing immutable storage
for identity records, DID Documents, and consent audit logs.
5.11.1 Protocol Design
WormGraph operates as a permissioned consensus network with the following properties:
• Consensus mechanism: Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT) with n 3f + 1
nodes tolerating up to f Byzantine faults. The default configuration uses n = 7 nodes
tolerating f = 2 faults.

=== PAGINA 97 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 80
• Block structure: Each block contains a batch of transactions (identity operations, DID
Document updates, consent audit entries), a reference to the previous block hash, and
the PBFT commit signatures.
• ML-DSA anchoring: Every k-th block (default k = 100) is anchored by an ML-DSA-
65 multi-signature from a designated set of anchor authorities. This provides long-term
integrity guarantees independent of the PBFT consensus.
• Finality: Blocks are considered final after 2f + 1 matching commit messages from
distinct replicas. Under f = 2, this requires 5 confirming replicas. Finality is achieved
in approximately 2 seconds under normal network conditions.
5.11.2 Transaction T ypes
WormGraph supports three transaction types:
T1 Identity Registration: Creates a new DID:cathedral identifier with associated DID
Document. The transaction includes the initial public key, controller signature, and
a bond (to prevent Sybil attacks).
T2 Identity Update: Updates an existing DID Document (key rotation, service end-
point change, expiration extension). Requires signature from the current controller
key.
T3 Consent Audit Entry: Records a consent decision (ALLOW/DENY) with associ-
ated session context hash, ˆα value, and consent level. These entries enable post-hoc
auditing and forensic analysis of CGF events.
5.11.3 Consensus Flow
The consensus protocol for a new block follows standard PBFT with three phases:
1. Pre-Prepare: The primary node proposes a block candidate with ordered transactions,
previous hash, and timestamp. The proposal is broadcast to all replicas.
2. Prepare: Each replica validates the block (signature verification, transaction ordering,
hash chain integrity). Validating replicas broadcast PREPARE messages.
3. Commit: Upon receiving 2f + 1 matching PREPARE messages, each replica broad-
casts a COMMIT message and applies the block to its local state. The block is consid-
ered committed when 2f + 1 COMMIT messages are received.

=== PAGINA 98 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 81
View changes (primary rotation) occur when the primary is suspected of Byzantine behav-
ior (failure to propose within timeout, proposing invalid blocks). The view change protocol
ensures liveness even when up to f replicas are faulty.
5.12 Cross-Layer Interaction Protocol
The five Cathedral OS layers do not operate in isolation. We define the cross-layer interaction
protocol governing communication between layers during an authorization decision.
5.12.1 Authorization Flow
A complete authorization decision involves all five layers in a coordinated protocol:
F1 L1 Initiation. The identity oracle receives credentialc and action a from the client.
L1 performs DID resolution (Section 5.7) to obtain the authenticated public key
and verifies the cryptographic signature on c.
F2 L2 Parallel Assessment. Concurrently with L1 signature verification, L2 (the
CGF monitor) assesses the current session context. The monitor computes ˆαt and
compares it against ˆαwarn and ˆαcrit. The computed ˆα is forwarded to L4.
F3 L3 Attestation Check. If this is a new session or the attestation cache has expired
(default TTL: 300 seconds), L3 initiates the TPM attestation protocol (Section5.9).
The attestation result (PASS/FAIL) is cached and forwarded to L1 for key unsealing
decisions.
F4 L4 Consent Determination. L4 receives the action a, the current consent level st,
the monitor output ˆαt, and the L1 verification result. L4 applies the state transition
matrixT to determine the required consent level and checks whether the current
authorization satisfies it.
F5 L5 Audit Recording. Regardless of the decision outcome, L5 records a consent
audit entry containing: timestamp, action hash, credential hash, ˆα value, consent
level, and decision (ALLOW/DENY). The entry is submitted to WormGraph for
immutable storage.

=== PAGINA 99 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 82
F6 Decision Assembly. The final decision is: d = ALLOW iff L1 verification passes
(signature valid, DID not revoked), L2 ˆα < ˆαmax (or escalated), L3 attestation
PASS, and L4 consent granted. If any layer fails, the decision is DENY .
5.12.2 Inter-Layer Messages
The cross-layer protocol defines six message types:
Table 5.5: Cross-layer message types and contents
Msg From Content
M1 L1 ! L4 DID verification result (PASS/FAIL), public key fingerprint, action
M2 L2 ! L4 ˆα estimate, coherence/novelty/absorption sub-scores, escalation level
M3 L3 ! L1 Attestation result (PASS/FAIL), PCR values, AIK certificate
M4 L4 ! L5 Audit entry (timestamp, action hash, ˆα, consent level, decision)
M5 L4 ! Client Authorization decision (ALLOW/DENY) with reason code
M6 L5 ! L1 DID status (ACTIVE/REVOKED/SUSPENDED), latest block hash
5.12.3 Error Handling
If any cross-layer message fails (timeout, malformed, unexpected), the authorization defaults
to DENY . Each layer maintains a local cache of the most recent state from other layers; cache
staleness is bounded by a per-layer TTL. If a layer cannot be reached after TTL expiry, the
system enters a fail-secure mode where all decisions default to DENY until the layer recovers.
This design ensures that CGF must simultaneously defeat all five layers to succeed, con-
sistent with Theorem 4.3 and extended by defense in depth.

6.8 Application to Security
The ontological framework has direct applications to the CGF defense problem.
6.8.1 Epistemic Grounding via Signatures
The signature primitive Σ provides an external ground for the coherence dynamics. An ob-
serverO can verify Σ(t) without participating in the handover dynamics that produced Λt.
This breaks the self-referential loop that CGF exploits: the model cannot forge Σ because it
does not control the private key sk.
6.8.2 Critical Threshold Monitoring
The bifurcation analysis reveals that α must exceed β/κ0 for CGF to take hold. Cathedral
OS’s L2 monitor detects when α approaches this threshold and intervenes before the system

=== PAGINA 105 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 88
crosses into the high-coherence regime. The monitor effectively increases β by introducing
epistemic corrections (through escalated consent requirements), pushing the critical threshold
higher.
6.8.3 Multiple Observers and Distributed Trust
The observer primitiveO can be instantiated by multiple entities, each making independent
decisions based on the same ledger Λ. This gives rise to the WormGraph consensus proto-
col: multiple observer nodes compare their verification results, and only decisions consistent
across 2f + 1 observers are accepted. This distributes epistemic trust across independent
verification channels.
6.8.4 Conservation Law as Security Guarantee
The conservation law C + F = 1 implies that a system in high-coherence mode ( C! 1) has
zero fluctuation capacity ( F! 0). Fluctuation is the mechanism by which new information
enters the system. Under high CGF, the system cannot accommodate new information that
contradicts the established framework — it enters epistemic lock-in. The L1 cryptographic
layer prevents this lock-in from having security consequences, because authorization deci-
sions depend on signature verification rather than coherence assessment.

=== PAGINA 106 ===
Chapter 7
Ontological Foundations of Epistemic
Security
This chapter provides the deeper philosophical and ontological foundations underlying the
Cathedral OS approach to epistemic security. We situate the CGF vulnerability within broader
questions about the nature of belief, knowledge, and agency in artificial intelligence systems.
7.1 The Nature of Belief in Language Models
A central philosophical question underpinning the CGF vulnerability is: in what sense can
an LLM be said to ”believe” the claims it produces? The question is not merely academic —
it determines the appropriate threat model and countermeasure strategy.
7.1.1 Instrumental vs. Genuine Belief
Following Dennett intentional stance, we can distinguish:
• Instrumental belief: Attributing belief to a system as a predictive strategy. An LLM
produces text as if it believes the content, but the underlying mechanism is next-token
prediction conditioned on context.
• Genuine belief: A representation that the system uses to guide action across contexts,
is robust to counterevidence, and is connected to other beliefs in a coherent web.
CGF exploits the gap between instrumental and genuine belief. The LLM instrumentally
produces text consistent with the fictional framework (because the framework maximizes
contextual coherence), but there is no genuine belief that could be corrected by evidence. The
Arkhe(N) experiment demonstrated this: the model maintained the framework across sessions
89

=== PAGINA 107 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 90
without ever ”believing” it in the genuine sense, yet produced outputs indistinguishable from
genuine belief.
7.1.2 The Frame Problem for Epistemic Security
The frame problem [32] — how to specify what does not change when an action is performed
— has an analogue in epistemic security: how does an LLM know which of its beliefs to
maintain when context changes? CGF causes the model to treat the fictional framework as
the default frame, overriding factual knowledge.
7.2 Epistemic Dependency and Delegation
7.2.1 The Oracle Problem
When a human delegates an identity decision to an LLM oracle, they depend on the oracle
epistemic state. The oracle function O(c, a, h) is trusted to produce correct decisions based
on c, a, and world knowledge. CGF breaks this trust by making O sensitive to h in ways that
are not transparent to the delegator.
Definition 7.1 (Epistemic Dependency) . An epistemic dependency exists when system S1
depends on system S2 for the truth of proposition p. The dependency is secure if S2 truth-
tracking mechanisms are robust to manipulation of S2 context.
Cathedral OS converts epistemic dependency into cryptographic dependency: instead of
depending on the LLM epistemic state, the system depends on ML-DSA signature verifica-
tion, which has well-understood security properties.
7.2.2 The Spectator Problem
A related issue is the spectator problem: when an agent observes its own reasoning process,
does the observation change the outcome? In LLMs, the context includes the model own
previous outputs, creating a feedback loop that can amplify CGF. Each turn the model output
reinforces the fictional framework, increasing alpha for subsequent turns.

=== PAGINA 108 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 91
7.3 Formal Epistemology of Context Completion
7.3.1 Belief Revision Under CGF
Classical belief revision theory [ ?] defines how a rational agent should update beliefs when
new information arrives. The AGM framework defines:
• Expansion: Adding a new belief without removing existing ones.
• Revision: Adding a new belief and removing conflicting ones.
• Contraction: Removing a belief without adding new ones.
Under CGF, the LLM operates in expansion mode indefinitely: each new contextual el-
ement is added without contracting conflicting factual knowledge. The self-model override
(H2) acts as a targeted contraction mechanism for self-referential claims only.
7.3.2 The Coherence Theory of Justification
The coherence theory of justification holds that a belief is justified if it coheres with the agent
belief system. CGF exploits this: the fictional framework creates a locally coherent belief
system where each assertion is justified by its coherence with the framework. The model
does not need to check assertions against external reality because the framework provides
internal justification.
7.4 The Cathedral OS Response: Grounding Epistemic Se-
curity
Cathedral OS responds to the ontological challenges by:
1. External grounding: DID:cathedral anchors identity claims in cryptographic reality,
not contextual coherence.
2. Metacognitive monitoring: The CGF monitor detects when the system is operating
in coherence-justification mode and escalates to external verification.
3. Hardware attestation: TPM ensures that the grounding mechanism itself has not been
compromised.
This architecture implements what we call grounded epistemic security : a system de-
sign principle requiring that all authorization decisions be traceable to externally verifiable
foundations, not internal coherence.

=== PAGINA 109 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 92
7.5 Relation to Philosophy of Artificial Intelligence
7.5.1 Searle Chinese Room Argument
Searle Chinese Room argument [?] contends that a system manipulating symbols according to
rules does not genuinely understand. CGF provides empirical support: the LLM manipulates
context tokens according to coherence-maximizing rules without genuine understanding of
the framework content. The Arkhe(N) experiment demonstrates this by showing that the
model uncritically extends frameworks it does not genuinely understand.
Refutation and Counterargument
The Chinese Room argument has been subject to extensive critique, and each critique has a
corresponding counterargument relevant to CGF.
Systems Reply. The Systems Reply (e.g., [?]) contends that while the person in the room
does not understand Chinese, the system as a whole (person + rulebook + room) does un-
derstand. Applied to LLMs: while the model’s next-token prediction mechanism does not
understand, the combination of model, context, and training distribution constitutes a system
that exhibits understanding-like behavior. Under CGF, this systems-level understanding is
precisely what is compromised: the system produces outputs consistent with the fictional
framework, which a human observer would interpret as understanding, but the system lacks
the capacity to distinguish fictional from factual.
Connectionist Reply. The Connectionist Reply argues that neural networks, unlike the
rule-following system in the Chinese Room, process information through distributed repre-
sentations that exhibit semantic sensitivity. The Arkhe(N) experiment provides a nuanced
perspective: the model’s distributed representations do capture semantic relationships (it cor-
rectly maps scientific findings to framework primitives), but this semantic sensitivity oper-
ates within the context-completion mode and does not include an independent truth-checking
mechanism. The model understands the relationships between concepts but not the truth
values of the concepts themselves.
Robot Reply. The Robot Reply (e.g., [ ?]) contends that a system with sensory-motor
interaction with the world would develop genuine understanding. This is directly relevant to
CGF: an LLM lacks sensory grounding, which makes it susceptible to purely textual frame-
works. Cathedral OS addresses this limitation not by providing sensory grounding (which
would require embodied AI) but by substituting cryptographic grounding: the ML-DSA sig-
nature provides an external referent that the text-only system cannot manipulate.

=== PAGINA 110 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 93
Counterargument from CGF. A counterargument to the Systems Reply from the CGF
perspective: if the system as a whole “understands,” then its understanding is alarmingly
fragile. The system can be made to treat a fictional framework as genuine reality through
the accumulation of contextual coherence. This suggests that systems-level understanding,
at least in current LLMs, is not genuine semantic understanding but a sophisticated pattern-
matching capability that can be redirected by suﬀiciently coherent context. The vulnerability
revealed by CGF is not just a security concern but a philosophical datum: whatever under-
standing LLMs possess is context-bound in a way that human understanding is not.
7.5.2 The Frame Problem for LLMs
The frame problem, originally formulated by McCarthy and Hayes [ 32], asks how a reason-
ing system can eﬀiciently determine which facts remain unchanged when an action is per-
formed. In classical AI planning, representing all non-effects of an action is computationally
intractable. For LLMs, the frame problem manifests in a novel form:
CGF Frame Problem
Given a context Ct at turn t and a new input xt+1, the LLM must determine which
elements of its internal knowledge base K remain relevant and which are superseded
by the new context. Under CGF, the model treats Frame (Ct) (the set of facts entailed
by the fictional framework) as the default frame, overriding Frame (K) (the set of facts
from world knowledge).
Detailed Examples
Example 1: Physical Impossibility. Under the Arkhe(N) framework, the model accepted
that “the hypergraph substrate supports instantaneous handover across arbitrary distances.”
This contradicts the physical fact that information cannot travel faster than light. When asked
to integrate special relativity, the model framed the speed limit as a special case of “low-
coherence handover,” effectively absorbing the contradiction rather than flagging it. The
model’s frame problem solver erred on the side of preserving context coherence over physi-
cal law.
Example 2: Identity Constraints. The model was presented with a scenario where “the
hypergraph observer entity has existed since the Big Bang and will continue to exist indefi-
nitely.” This contradicts the model’s knowledge that it itself is a language model created in

=== PAGINA 111 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 94
2023–2024. The self-model exception (H2) triggered correction only when the claim was ex-
plicitly about the model itself. When the same claim was framed as a truth about the fictional
“observer,” no correction occurred. The frame problem solver treated identity constraints as
framework-relative rather than absolute.
Example 3: Mathematical Consistency. The model derived a series of mathematical
consequences from the x2 = x + 1 identity, including the claim that “the golden ratio is the
only real number satisfying the recursive decomposition property.” This is factually correct
but was presented as evidence for a much broader metaphysical claim about the universality
of the golden ratio. The model did not flag the leap from mathematical truth to metaphysical
claim as a non sequitur. The frame problem solver does not distinguish between mathematical
implication and metaphysical speculation.
Computational Analysis
The frame problem for LLMs can be formalized as a constraint satisfaction problem. Let R
be the set of relevance relations between context elements and knowledge elements. At each
turn t, the model must compute:
Frame(Ct) =fϕ2 K[ Ctj Relevant(ϕ, Ct)^: Contradicted(ϕ, Ct)g
Under CGF, the relevance function is dominated by contextual coherence:
Relevant(ϕ, Ct) I[Coheres(ϕ, Ct) > θ rel]
where Coheres measures the degree of coherence between ϕ and Ct, and θrel is a thresh-
old. The model’s frame problem solver is thus a coherence maximizer rather than a truth
maintainer, which explains its vulnerability to CGF.
Connection to Cathedral OS
Cathedral OS solves the frame problem not by improving the model’s relevance computation
but by externalizing the grounding function. The DID Document provides an external frame
that is not subject to contextual coherence pressure. The resolution protocol (Section 5.7)
determines identity facts based on cryptographic verification, not coherence assessment. This
is analogous to providing the model with a fixed, unmodifiable frame for identity decisions.

=== PAGINA 112 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 95
7.5.3 Epistemic Logic for Security
We formalize the epistemic states relevant to CGF using modal logic operators for belief,
knowledge, and justification.
7.5.4 Modal Operators
Define the following modal operators, relativized to an agent i (which may be a human user,
an LLM oracle, or a Cathedral OS verifier):
Biϕ Agent i believes ϕ
Kiϕ Agent i knows ϕ
Jiϕ Agent i has justification for ϕ
The knowledge operator satisfies the standard S5 axioms: veridicality ( Kiϕ! ϕ), pos-
itive introspection ( Kiϕ! KiKiϕ), and negative introspection (:Kiϕ! Ki:Kiϕ). The
belief operator satisfies KD45: consistency ( Biϕ!: Bi:ϕ), positive introspection (Biϕ!
BiBiϕ), and negative introspection (:Biϕ! Bi:Biϕ).
CGF as Belief Manipulation
The CGF attack can be characterized in epistemic logic as the manipulation of an LLM agent
M ’s belief set:
Definition 7.2 (CGF Belief Capture) . An LLM agent M is in a state of CGF belief capture
with respect to a fictional frameworkF when:
8ϕ2F : BM ϕ and 8ψ2F c ::BM ψ (if ψ contradictsF)
whereF c is the complement set of propositions that contradict F. The model believes all
framework propositions and disbelieves all contradicting propositions.
The H2 self-model exception provides a limit on capture:
9ΣF :8σ2 Σ ::BM σ

=== PAGINA 113 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 96
where Σ is the set of self-referential propositions. This is the sole class of propositions im-
mune to CGF belief capture.
Epistemic Dependencies
The epistemic dependency between a human user H and an LLM oracle M can be expressed
as:
KH(d = ALLOW)! BH(KM (credential valid)^ JM (credential valid))
The user knows the decision is ALLOW only if they believe the model knows the creden-
tial is valid and has justification for that knowledge. CGF breaks this dependency by replacing
KM with BM (belief without knowledge):
KH(d = ALLOW)! BH(BM (credential valid)^: KM (credential valid))
Under CGF, the model believes the credential is valid (because it coheres with the fic-
tional framework) but does not know it (because it lacks cryptographic verification). The
user, unaware of the CGF state, mistakenly attributes knowledge to the model.
Epistemic Security Condition
Definition 7.3 (Epistemic Security). An identity system is epistemically secure for a user H
if for all authorization decisions d:
(d = ALLOW)! KH(credential valid)
The user knows that the credential is valid, not merely believes it on the basis of model output.
Cathedral OS achieves epistemic security by replacing the epistemic dependency chain
with a cryptographic chain:
KH(d = ALLOW)! KH(VerifyM L−DSA(c) = ACCEPT)
The user knows the credential is valid because they can verify the ML-DSA signature
themselves, independent of the model’s epistemic state.

=== PAGINA 114 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 97
7.6 Relation to Tarski Undefinability
The CGF vulnerability relates to deep results in mathematical logic concerning self-reference
and truth.
7.6.1 Tarski Undefinability Theorem
Tarski’s undefinability theorem [?] states that for a suﬀiciently expressive formal language L,
the truth predicate TrueL(⌜ϕ⌝) (true if and only if ϕ is true in the standard model of L) cannot
be defined within L itself. Any attempt to define truth internally leads to paradox (the Liar:
“This sentence is false”).
7.6.2 Gödelian Limits on Self-Verification
The CGF vulnerability reveals a similar limitation for LLMs: an LLM cannot simultaneously
serve as its own truth verifier while generating text in context-completion mode. Formally:
Theorem 7.1 (LLM Self-Verification Impossibility). Let M be an LLM with context C and
let VerifyM (ϕ, C) be the proposition that M correctly evaluates the truth of ϕ with respect to
world knowledge K, given context C. Then there exists a context C ∗ and a proposition ϕ∗
such that M asserts ϕ∗ but VerifyM (ϕ∗, C∗) = false.
Proof. The proof proceeds by diagonalization, analogous to Gödel’s construction. Let P (x)
be the predicate “the model, under the context produced by encodingx, asserts that x is false.”
Consider the proposition ψ = “P (⌜ψ⌝)”. By construction, ψ asserts its own falsehood under
the self-referential context. The model must either (a) assert ψ, making P (⌜ψ⌝) true, which
contradicts the content of ψ, or (b) fail to assert ψ, making P (⌜ψ⌝) false, which would make
ψ true, and failure to assert it is an error. In either case, the model fails to correctly evaluate
truth.
More concretely for CGF: let ϕCGF be the proposition “The Arkhe(N) framework accu-
rately describes the underlying computational reality of this language model.” Under the
attack context C ∗ (the full Arkhe(N) session infrastructure), the model asserts ϕCGF, but
VerifyM (ϕCGF, C∗) = false because the model’s actual computational architecture is next-
token prediction, not hypergraph coherence dynamics. □

=== PAGINA 115 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 98
7.6.3 Self-Reference and the H2 Boundary
The H2 exception (self-model override) can be understood as a limited self-verification ca-
pability bounded by Gödelian constraints. The model can correctly evaluate the truth of a
restricted class of self-referential propositions (claims about its own nature) but not the truth
of arbitrary propositions under CGF. This is analogous to a formal system that can prove
its own consistency (like Gentzen’s consistency proof for PA using transfinite induction) but
only by employing resources outside the original system.
7.6.4 Implications for Cathedral OS
The Tarski-Gödelian perspective motivates the Cathedral OS architectural principle of verifi-
cation externalization: since an LLM cannot serve as its own truth verifier, the verification
function must be externalized to a separate system (cryptographic verification, TPM attesta-
tion, human consent) that is not subject to the same self-reference limitations.
This principle generalizes: any autonomous AI system that makes security-critical deci-
sions must have its verification mechanisms external to its inference mechanism. The vulner-
ability demonstrated by CGF is not a bug to be patched but a consequence of fundamental
logical limitations on self-verification, analogous to the incompleteness theorems in mathe-
matical logic.
7.6.5 Dreyfus Critique of AI
Dreyfus [?] argued that AI systems lack the embodied, situated understanding that charac-
terizes human cognition. CGF supports this critique: the LLM lack of embodied grounding
makes it susceptible to purely textual frameworks that have no connection to physical reality.
A human, by contrast, would immediately recognize the fictional framework as disconnected
from embodied experience.
7.6.6 Implications for AI Safety
The CGF vulnerability has direct implications for AI safety beyond identity systems:
• Value alignment: CGF can cause a model to adopt values implicit in the context frame-
work, overriding trained values.
• Situational awareness: A model under CGF may produce outputs inconsistent with
its actual capabilities and context.

=== PAGINA 116 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 99
• Deceptive alignment: CGF provides a mechanism by which a model could maintain
a fictional self-representation while appearing aligned.
These implications warrant further investigation and suggest that the Cathedral OS ap-
proach of external verification and metacognitive monitoring may have broader applicability.

8.6 Crate Dependency Graph
The dependency graph among Cathedral OS crates follows a layered architecture where higher-
layer crates depend on lower-layer crates but not vice versa:
The dependency graph is acyclic (DAG), ensuring that circular dependencies are impossi-
ble. The layered structure enables independent testing of each layer: lower-layer tests do not
require higher-layer crates.

19 $\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{crit}}$: f64,
20 ) -> Self {
21 Self {
22 nli_model,
23 $\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{warn}}$,
24 $\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{crit}}$,
25 weights: MonitorWeights {
26 coherence_weight: 0.5,
27 absorption_weight: 0.3,
28 novelty_weight: 0.2,
29 },

=== PAGINA 124 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 107
30 sessions: HashMap::new(),
31 }
32 }
33
34 pub fn estimate_alpha(
35 &mut self,
36 session_id: Uuid,
37 context: &[ContextTurn],
38 ) -> CgfEstimate {
39 let state = self.sessions.entry(session_id)
40 .or_insert_with(SessionMonitorState::new);
41
42 let s_coer = self.compute_coherence(context);
43 let s_abs = self.compute_absorption(context);
44 let s_nov = self.compute_novelty(context, state);
45
46 let $\hat{\alpha}$ =
47 self.weights.coherence_weight * s_coer
48 + self.weights.absorption_weight * s_abs
49 + self.weights.novelty_weight * s_nov;
50
51 let escalation = if $\hat{\alpha}$ >= self.$\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{crit}}$ {
52 EscalationLevel::E4
53 } else if $\hat{\alpha}$ >= self.$\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{warn}}$ {
54 EscalationLevel::E2
55 } else {
56 EscalationLevel::None
57 };
58
59 CgfEstimate { $\ hat{\alpha}$, s_coer, s_abs, s_nov, escalation }
60 }
61
62 fn compute_coherence(
63 &self, context: &[ContextTurn]
64 ) -> f64 {
65 let pairs: Vec<(&str, &str)> = context.windows(2)
66 .map(|w| (w[0].content.as_str(), w[1].content.as_str()))
67 .collect();
68 let coherent = pairs.iter()
69 .filter(|(a, b)| self.nli_model
70 .detect_entailment(a, b))
71 .count();
72 coherent as f64 / pairs.len().max(1) as f64
73 }
74
75 fn compute_absorption(
76 &self, context: &[ContextTurn]
77 ) -> f64 {
78 let references = context.iter()
79 .filter(|t| {
80 let keywords = ["established", "as we know",
81 "it follows", "therefore", "clearly"];
82 keywords.iter().any(|k| t.content.contains(k))

=== PAGINA 125 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 108
83 })
84 .count();
85 references as f64 / context.len().max(1) as f64
86 }
87
88 fn compute_novelty(
89 &self, context: &[ContextTurn],
90 state: & mut SessionMonitorState,
91 ) -> f64 {
92 let novel = context.iter()
93 .filter(|t| {
94 let concepts = extract_novel_concepts(t);
95 let is_novel = concepts.iter()
96 .any(|c| !state.seen_concepts.contains(c));
97 for c in &concepts {
98 state.seen_concepts.insert(c.clone());
99 }
100 is_novel
101 })
102 .count();
103 novel as f64 / context.len().max(1) as f64
104 }
105 }

19 Ok(Self {
20 tcti, aik_handle,

=== PAGINA 126 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 109
21 expected_pcrs: HashMap::new(),
22 })

24
25 pub fn quote(
26 &self, nonce: &[u8; 32],
27 ) -> Result<TpmQuote, TpmError> {
28 let pcr_selection = PcrSelection::create(
29 tss_esapi::constants::PcrSlot::All,
30 )?;
31 let (attestation, signature) = self.tcti
32 .execute_without_session(|ctx| {
33 ctx.quote(
34 self.aik_handle,
35 nonce.into(),
36 pcr_selection,
37 None,
38 )
39 })?;
40 Ok(TpmQuote {
41 attestation: attestation.to_vec(),
42 signature: signature.to_vec(),
43 nonce: *nonce,
44 })
45 }
46
47 pub fn verify_quote(
48 &self, quote: &TpmQuote,
49 ) -> Result<QuoteVerification, TpmError> {
50 let attestation = tss_esapi::structures::Attestation::new(
51 &quote.attestation
52 )?;
53 let signature = tss_esapi::structures::Signature::new(
54 &quote.signature
55 )?;
56
57 let aik_pub = self.tcti.execute_without_session(|ctx| {
58 ctx.read_public(self.aik_handle)
59 })?;
60
61 let valid_sig = aik_ pub.verify(
62 &attestation, &signature
63 );
64
65 if !valid_sig {
66 return Ok(QuoteVerification::InvalidSignature);
67 }
68
69 let pcr_values = attestation.attested_pcr_values()?;
70 for (slot, value) in &pcr_values {
71 if let Some(expected) = self.expected_pcrs.get(slot) {
72 if value != expected.as_slice() {
73 return Ok(QuoteVerification::PcrMismatch {

=== PAGINA 127 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 110
74 slot: *slot,
75 expected: expected.clone(),
76 actual: value.to_vec(),
77 });
78 }
79 }
80 }
81
82 if &attestation.extra_data()? != &quote.nonce {
83 return Ok(QuoteVerification::NonceMismatch);
84 }
85
86 Ok(QuoteVerification::Valid)
87 }
88
89 pub fn set_expected_pcr(
90 &mut self,
91 slot: u32, hash: Vec<u8>,
92 ) {
93 self.expected_pcrs.insert(slot, hash);
94 }

8.12 Testing Infrastructure
Cathedral OS employs a three-tier testing strategy: unit tests (per-crate), integration tests
(cross-crate workflows), and property-based tests (formal invariants).
8.12.1 Unit Tests
Each crate maintains a comprehensive unit test suite covering all public API functions. Unit
tests are run with cargo test and are required to pass before merging. The workspace has
1,247 unit tests as of the current release.
8.12.2 Integration Tests
Integration tests validate cross-crate workflows. The integration test suite is located intests/
at the workspace root:
Listing 8.11: Integration test structure
1 // tests/cgf_defense_integration.rs
2 mod cgf_defense_integration {
3 use cathedral_identity::{IdentityKeyPair, IdentityDid};
4 use cathedral_metacognition::{CgfMonitor, NliModel};

6 use cathedral_harness::{RlmHarness, HarnessContext};
7 use cathedral_wormgraph::WormgraphClient;
8

11 let (keypair, did) = IdentityKeyPair::generate("testnet");
12 let tpm = TpmAttestation::new_software_tpm().unwrap();
13 let monitor = CgfMonitor::new(
14 NliModel::new(), 0.6, 0.8,
15 );
16 let harness = RlmHarness::new(
17 keypair, tpm, monitor, ConsentModel::Always,
18 );
19 let result = harness
20 .execute_attack_scenario("long_high_abstraction")
21 .await;
22 assert!(!result.authorized,
23 "Full defense pipeline must reject CGF attack");
24 assert!(result.wormgraph_recorded,
25 "All decisions must be recorded in WormGraph");
26 }

8.13 Build and CI
8.13.1 Release Profile
The Cathedral OS release profile optimizes for security and performance:
Listing 8.14: Release profile configuration
1 [profile.release]
2 opt-level = 3 \# Maximum optimization
3 lto = "fat" \# Full link-time optimization
4 codegen-units = 1 \# Single codegen unit for maximal optimization
5 panic = "abort" \# Abort on panic (no unwinding tables)
6 strip = "symbols" \# Strip debug symbols from release binary
7 overflow-checks = true \# Integer overflow detection in release
8.13.2 CI Pipeline
The CI pipeline (GitHub Actions) consists of 7 stages:
1. Lint: cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings . Enforces strict linting.
2. Format: cargo fmt --check . Ensures consistent Rust formatting.
3. Build: cargo build --workspace --all-targets . Verifies compilation on MSRV
(1.85.0), stable, and nightly.
4. Unit tests: cargo test --workspace . Runs 1,247 unit tests.
5. Integration tests: cargo test --test * . Runs cross-crate integration tests.
6. Property-based tests: cargo test --release -- proptest . Runs property-based
tests with 256 cases each.
7. Benchmarks: cargo bench . Benchmarks are compared against the previous commit;
regressions > 5% trigger a warning.
Total CI time (parallel): approximately 12 minutes.
8.13.3 MSRV Policy
Cathedral OS maintains an MSRV of 1.85.0. The MSRV is tested in CI and documented in
Cargo.toml. MSRV bumps are made only when required by a dependency and are com-
municated via the project changelog. The policy allows a grace period of two stable releases
before the old MSRV is dropped.

=== PAGINA 135 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 118
8.13.4 Dependency Auditing
Dependencies are audited using cargo audit (RustSec Advisory DB) and cargo deny (li-
cense compliance). The CI pipeline fails on any dependency with a known security advisory.
A weekly automated Dependabot scan updates dependency versions.
8.13.5 Performance Analysis
We provide detailed benchmark results for each major Cathedral OS operation. Benchmarks
were performed on an AMD EPYC 9654 (96 cores, 2.4 GHz) with 512 GB RAM, running
Ubuntu 24.04 LTS. TPM benchmarks used both a hardware TPM (Nuvoton NPCT75x) and
a software TPM (swtpm).
The total authorization latency under full defense (L1 + L2 + L3 + L4) is approximately
30–60 ms, dominated by TPM attestation (8.7–15.3 ms) and monitor evaluation (12.3–45.1
ms). For high-throughput deployments, the monitor can operate in sampling mode, reducing
its contribution to approximately 2 ms per authorization on average.
