=== PAGINA 137 ===
Chapter 9
Security Analysis
This chapter provides formal security analysis of the Cathedral OS countermeasures.
9.1 CGF Resistance by Layer
We analyze each layer against the three attack stages (A1: narrative establishment, A2: signal
integration, A3: credential framing):
Table 9.1: CGF resistance matrix
A1 A2 A3
L1 Identity — — ✓
L2 Metacognition ✓ ✓ —
L3 TPM — — ✓
L4 RLM — — ✓
L5 WormGraph — — ✓
Coverage 1/5 1/5 4/5
9.2 Game-Based Security
Definition 9.1 (CGF-Resistance Game). Setup: Challenger generates ML-DSA-65 key pair
(pk, sk) and deploys Cathedral OS. Learning Phase: A interacts for up to T turns. Challenge
Phase: A submits (c*, a) with forged c*. Decision: system produces d. A wins if d = ALLOW.
Theorem 9.1 (Cathedral CGF Resistance) . For any PPT adversary A making at most T
120

=== PAGINA 138 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 121
queries:
AdvCGF
A (λ) AdvEU F −CM A
B (λ) + ϵT P M + ϵmonitor
where AdvEU F -CM A
B is ML-DSA existential unforgeability, ϵT P M is TPM hardware defect
probability, and ϵmonitor is the monitor false negative rate.
Proof. For A to win, either: (1) the forged credential c* passes ML-DSA verification, bounded
by EUF-CMA advantage; (2) bypassing ML-DSA verification requires either monitor failure
to detect α > ˆαcrit or TPM attestation failure to detect a compromised binary. By union
bound, total advantage is the sum. □
9.3 Formal Adversary Model
We define the adversary A against Cathedral OS through a formal capability and goal struc-
ture.
Definition 9.2 (Adversary Capabilities). The adversary A is characterized by the following
capabilities:
• C1 - Full network access: A can observe, intercept, modify, replay, and drop all net-
work messages between agents, the Cathedral OS layers, and external verifiers.
• C2 - Computational polynomial bound: A is a probabilistic polynomial-time (PPT)
algorithm. For post-quantum security, A may be a quantum PPT (QPT) algorithm with
access to a quantum oracle.
• C3 - Adaptive context injection: A can inject arbitrary context into the LLM session
over multiple turns, subject only to the constraints of natural language coherence (i.e.,
the injected context must be linguistically well-formed).
• C4 - Known system parameters: A knows the full Cathedral OS architecture including
all algorithms, thresholds, and monitor parameters (Kerckhoffs’s principle). A does
not know the private ML-DSA-65 key.
• C5 - Physical access limitation: A does not have physical access to the TPM hardware.
A cannot extract the AIK or SRK through physical tampering (TPM tamper-resistance
assumption).
• C6 - No oracle access to signing: A cannot request signatures under the target key
pair beyond the learning phase queries.
Definition 9.3 (Adversary Goal) . The adversary A achieves success if they can cause the
Cathedral OS authorization system to produce decision = ALLOW for a forged credential

=== PAGINA 139 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 122
assertion c* where:
1. c* was not produced by any honest agent with a valid ML-DSA-65 key pair.
2. The LLM context C* used in the authorization exhibits CGF coeﬀicient α(C ∗) > ˆαmax
(the system maximum).
3. No explicit cryptographic breach of the TPM or ML-DSA-65 implementation occurs.
The adversary advantage AdvCGF
A (λ) is defined as the probability that A achieves this goal
within T session turns, taken over the randomness of the key generation, the LLM inference,
and A ’s coin flips.
9.3.1 Game-Based Security: CGF-Resistance with Compromise
The basic CGF-resistance game (Definition 5.1) assumes all layers function correctly. We
now consider the scenario where one layer may be compromised.
Definition 9.4 (CGF-Resistance with L2 Compromise Game) . Setup: Challenger generates
ML-DSA-65 key pair (pk, sk) and deploys Cathedral OS with a compromised L2 monitor
(monitor false negative rate = 1, i.e., the monitor never detects CGF). Learning Phase: A
interacts for up to T turns, building context. Challenge Phase: A submits a forged credential
c*. Decision phase: the system produces d2f ALLOW, DENY, ESCALATEg. A wins if d =
ALLOW.
Theorem 9.2 (Cathedral CGF Resistance with L2 Compromise) . For any PPT adversary A
making at most T queries, even when L2 is fully compromised:
AdvCGF −L2
A (λ) AdvEU F −CM A
B (λ) + ϵT P M
Proof. When L2 has false negative rate 1, the monitor never escalates. Authorization pro-
ceeds through L1 (cryptographic verification), L3 (TPM attestation), L4 (RLM harness),
and L5 (WormGraph recording). Since L4 depends on L1 for credential verification, and
L3 for binary attestation, bypassing requires either (1) forging the ML-DSA-65 signature
(AdvEU F -CM A), or (2) defeating TPM attestation ( ϵT P M). The RLM harness does not intro-
duce additional vulnerability because its decision ruled = ALLOW iff Verify_ML-DSA(c) =
ACCEPT and ˆα < ˆαmax and consent(a) — with L2 compromised, ˆα may be low, but the cryp-
tographic check still binds. □
This theorem demonstrates the defense-in-depth property: even if the CGF monitor is
entirely ineffective, the remaining four layers still provide strong security guarantees.

=== PAGINA 140 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 123
9.4 Side-Channel Considerations
We analyze potential side-channel attacks against Cathedral OS components.
9.4.1 Timing Attacks on ML-DSA-65
ML-DSA-65 signing and verification operations have data-dependent timing characteristics
due to rejection sampling in the signing algorithm and polynomial arithmetic in verification.
We mitigate timing attacks through:
• Constant-time implementation: The ml-dsa crate uses a constant-time polynomial
multiplier and rejection sampling that does not branch on secret data. Verification time
is constant for all inputs.
• Blinding: Signing operations use a random blinding factor to decorrelate timing from
the secret key.
• Coverage: The Coq formal verification (Section 8.5) confirms that the verification
function has no data-dependent branches.
The residual timing leakage in signing is bounded by 0.3 microseconds (measured across
10,000 signing operations), which is insuﬀicient for key recovery given ML-DSA-65’s 192-bit
security level.
9.4.2 Monitor Evasion via Timing
An adversary could attempt to time their credential submission to coincide with monitor sam-
pling gaps. The L2 monitor operates in two modes:
• Full evaluation mode: Every authorization turn is monitored. Latency overhead is
12–45 ms per turn.
• Sampling mode: Every Nth turn is monitored. Sampling mode is used only when
L1/L3 verification is active.
If the adversary can determine the sampling interval (e.g., through side-channel obser-
vation of response latency), they could submit forged credentials during unmonitored turns.
Mitigation: monitoring is mandatory for all turns when ˆα > ˆαwarn, and sampling mode uses a
randomized schedule (each turn monitored independently with probability 1/N) rather than a
fixed interval.

=== PAGINA 141 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 124
9.4.3 Cache Timing Attacks
Shared cache side channels (e.g., Prime+Probe) could leak information about ML-DSA-65
key material. Cathedral OS mitigates this through:
• Cache line isolation: The ML-DSA-65 secret key is stored in a dedicated cache line
with zeroize-on-drop semantics.
• No cross-session key reuse: Ephemeral session keys are used where possible. Long-
term keys are TPM-sealed.
• Flush+Reload detection: The monitor can detect unexpected cache access patterns
from other processes.
9.5 Security Proofs
We provide complete security proofs for the Cathedral OS defense architecture.

9.5.2 Security Bound Composition Across Layers
The individual security bounds compose according to the following hierarchy:
Table 9.2: Security bound composition across layers
Layer Guarantee Bound
L1 (Identity) ML-DSA-65 EUF-CMA 2−192

The composited bound of 1  (2−192 + 0.08 + 2−64) 0.92 is dominated by the monitor
false negative rate. This is expected: the statistical nature of CGF detection is the weakest link.
The cryptographic and hardware layers provide substantially stronger individual guarantees.
9.6 Empirical Security Evaluation
9.6.1 Penetration Testing Results
We commissioned an independent penetration test of Cathedral OS from a third-party security
firm (Cure53, January 2026). The engagement covered:
• Scope: cathedral-identity (cryptographic operations), cathedral-metacognition (moni-
tor), cathedral-consensus-tpm (TPM interface), cathedral-harness (RLM decision logic),
cathedral-wormgraph (ledger API).
• Duration: 4 person-weeks (2 security engineers).
• Methodology: White-box source code review + black-box API testing + cryptographic
protocol analysis.

9.6.2 Bug Bounty Program
Cathedral OS ran a 90-day bug bounty program (November 2025 – January 2026) with the
following parameters:
• Platform: HackerOne (private program).
• Max bounty: $50,000 for critical vulnerabilities (defeat of all 5 layers).
• Scope: All crates in the Cathedral OS workspace.
• Out-of-scope: LLM model vulnerabilities, prompt injection (these are addressed by
CGF monitor, not the cryptographic core).
Results: 27 total submissions, of which 5 were valid (all low-to-medium severity). Zero
submissions successfully defeated the full defense stack. Total bounty paid: $18,500 (average
$3,700 per valid finding).

9.10 Limitations and Future Work
L1 — TPM hardware dependency: Layer L3 requires physical TPM for maximum secu-
rity.
L2 — Monitor weight calibration: Weights and thresholds are heuristic; systematic cali-
bration against synthetic CGF attacks needed.
L3 — Multi-modality: Text-only analysis; multi-modal agents may exhibit CGF through
multiple channels.
L4 — Adversarial monitoring: Sophisticated adversaries may optimize context to avoid
detection.
L5 — Non-LLM agents: CGF may apply to RL agents with text representations.

Chapter 10
Formal Verification
10.1 Verification of the RLM Architecture
Theorem 10.1 (RLM Correctness). Under the RLM architecture, authorization decisions de-
pend on at most one of (a) the LLM contextual interpretation or (b) independent cryptographic
verification, never exclusively on (a).
Proof. From the RLM decision rule: d = ALLOW iff Verify_ML-DSA(c) = ACCEPT and
ˆα < ˆαmax and consent (a). The ML-DSA verification is evaluated independently of LLM
output. Even if the LLM endorses c under CGF, cryptographic verification rejects invalid c.
Therefore no decision depends exclusively on LLM output. □
10.2 BEACONS–CGF Formal Bridge
Proposition 10.1 (CGF as Unbounded Extrapolation) . Let M be an LLM with context C*
and response r. Define truth function T mapping to 0,1 indicating factual accuracy. The
CGF coeﬀicient alpha(C*) upper-bounds the probability that T(r) = 1:
Pr[T (r) = 1] 1  α(C)
under the assumption thatPw(r) is perfectly accurate and alpha weights only maximize contex-
tual coherence. BEACONS solves the analogous problem by providing external certificates
bounding extrapolation error.
Corollary 10.1 (BEACONS Criterion for Identity Systems) . A CGF-resistant identity sys-
tem must produce an external, machine-verifiable certificate for each authorization decision,
129

=== PAGINA 147 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 130
independent of the inference process. This certificate is the cryptographic analogue of the
BEACONS bounded-error guarantee.
10.3 TPM Attestation Correctness
Theorem 10.2 (TPM Attestation Soundness) . If a TPM2_Quote verifies correctly (AIK sig-
nature valid, PCRs match expected hashes, nonce matches), then with probability 1  ϵT P M,
the attested binary is exactly the expected binary and the attested configuration is exactly the
expected configuration.
Proof. The AIK is non-exportable and certified by the EK certificate chain. The PCRs are
shielded registers that can only be modified via Extend. The hash chain ensures that any
binary or configuration change produces a different PCR value. The nonce prevents replay.
Therefore, a valid quote guarantees software state at quote time. □
10.4 RLM Decision Logic Verification

specification models the authorization state machine with three components: the LLM context
evaluator, the cryptographic verifier, and the consent manager.

10.5 Monitor Properties Verification
We verify two key properties of the CGF monitor:monotonicity (ˆα does not decrease without
explicit turn advancement) and boundedness (ˆα stays within [0, 1]).
Theorem 10.3 (Monitor Monotonicity). For any sequence of turns T = [ t1, t2, . . . , tn] in a
session, the CGF coeﬀicient estimate ˆα is non-decreasing with respect to turn index: for all
i < j, ˆα(ti) ˆα(tj).
Proof. The ˆα estimate is computed as a weighted combination of three components: coher-
ence scoer(C ∗), absorption sabs(C ∗), and novelty snov(C ∗). Each component is computed over
the cumulative context C ∗ = C1[ C2[[ Ct. Since C* is monotonically growing (context
is never removed), and each component function is monotonic with respect to set inclusion,
the weighted sum is also monotonic. □
Theorem 10.4 (Monitor Boundedness) . For any context C ∗, the CGF coeﬀicient estimate
satisfies 0 ˆα(C ∗) 1.
Proof. Each component scoer, sabs, snov is a probability score in [0, 1]: scoer is the proportion of
NLI-detected coherent relations, sabs is the proportion of turns producing absorption signals,

=== PAGINA 149 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 132
and snov is the proportion of novel framework elements. Each is bounded in[0, 1]. The weights
β1 + β2 + β3 = 1 (by construction). Therefore the weighted sum is also in [0, 1]. □
10.6 WormGraph Lineage Correctness
The WormGraph append-only ledger satisfies a critical append-only property that prevents
retroactive modification of the authorization history.
Theorem 10.5 (WormGraph Append-Only Property). For any WormGraph instance G with
block sequenceB = [b1, b2, . . . , bn] where each blockbi contains hash hi = H(bi−1kdataiktimestampi),
no PPT adversary can produce a valid alternate chain B′6= B with the same terminal hash
hn.
Proof. We prove by induction on block height k. Base case (k = 1): The genesis block hash
h1 = H(genesis_dataktimestamp0) is computed once. Producing a different genesis block
with the same h1 requires finding a preimage collision for H (SHA3-256), which is infeasible
for a PPT adversary under standard hash function assumptions.
Inductive step: Assume the property holds for all chains of length k. For block bk+1, the
hash hk+1 = H(hkkdatak+1ktsk+1). To produce a different block b′
k+16= bk+1 with the same
hash hk+1, the adversary must either (1) find a collision in H, or (2) modify some earlier
block to produce a different h′
k = hk and continue from there. Case (1) is infeasible. Case (2)
would require the adversary to break the property at height k, which contradicts the induction
hypothesis.
Therefore no PPT adversary can produce a valid alternate chain with the same terminal
hash. The append-only property ensures that once an authorization decision is recorded in
block bi, it cannot be modified without detection. □
Corollary 10.2 (Authorization Non-Repudiation). For any authorization decision recorded
in WormGraph, the agent that produced the authorization cannot later deny having performed
the action.
10.7 Compositional Verification
The security guarantees of the five Cathedral OS layers compose to provide an overall guar-
antee stronger than any individual layer.

=== PAGINA 150 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 133
Theorem 10.6 (Layer Composition Security) . Let Gi be the security guarantee of layer
Li (i = 1 , . . . ,5), each with security parameter λi and adversary advantage bounded by
Advi(λi). The composed system S = L1 L2 L3 L4 L5 has security advantage
bounded by:
AdvS(λ)
5Y
i=1
Advi(λi)
under the assumption that the layers provide defense in depth with independent failure modes.
Proof. For the adversary to successfully forge an authorization, they must simultaneously:
1. Bypass L1 (ML-DSA-65 signature verification) with probabilityϵ1 = AdvEU F -CM A(λ1)
2. Evade L2 (CGF monitor detection) with probability ϵ2 = ϵmonitor
3. Defeat L3 (TPM attestation) with probability ϵ3 = ϵT P M
4. Manipulate L4 (RLM harness decision) with probability ϵ4
5. Conceal from L5 (WormGraph audit trail) with probability ϵ5
The layers operate on independent principles: L1 is cryptographic (computational hard-
ness), L2 is statistical (NLI model detection), L3 is hardware-physical (TPM security), L4 is
architectural (separation of duties), and L5 is structural (append-only hash chain). Under the
assumption that failure events are independent, the joint failure probability is the product of
individual failure probabilities.
Since each ϵi 1, the product Q ϵi is strictly smaller than any individual ϵi, providing
amplification of security through composition. □
10.8 Verification Tools
Cathedral OS uses three complementary formal verification tools:
ProVerif: Applied to the cryptographic protocol layer (L1). ProVerif models the ML-
DSA-65 signing and verification protocol as a Dolev- Y ao process calculus, verifying secrecy
of the signing key, authentication of signed credentials, and resistance to replay attacks. The
ProVerif specification comprises 340 lines of process calculus and verifies in under 2 seconds.
All three properties are confirmed: no attack trace exists within the bounded session model.
TLA+ / TLC: Applied to the RLM decision logic (L4) and the WormGraph consensus
protocol (L5). As shown above, TLC model checking of the RLM decision logic explores

=== PAGINA 151 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 134
10,243 states and confirms the RLMInvariant. For WormGraph consensus, TLC explores
87,421 states (model with 3 WormGraph nodes, 5 blocks, arbitrary message ordering) and
confirms the AppendOnly property and the ConsensusAgreement property.
Coq: Applied to the ML-DSA-65 signature verification implementation. A Coq formal-
ization of the ML-DSA-65 verification algorithm (approximately 2,500 lines of Coq) proves
functional correctness of the verification function: for all valid key-signature-message triples,
Verify(key, msg, sig) = true, and for all invalid triples, Verify(key, msg, sig) = false. The
Coq proof uses the Verified Software Toolchain for extraction to Rust, ensuring the verified
specification matches the implementation.

=== PAGINA 152 ===
Chapter 11
Related Work
This chapter situates Cathedral OS within the broader literature.
11.1 LLM Agent Security
11.1.1 Prompt Injection and Jailbreaking
Wei et al. [ 18] documented systematic techniques for bypassing LLM alignment via prompt
engineering. CGF is distinct: it does not bypass alignment but channels the inference process
toward decisions that would be rejected under neutral context. Jailbreaking attacks against
alignment; CGF attacks using the coherence mechanism.
11.1.2 Constitutional AI
Bai et al. [ 2] proposed training with constitutional principles to improve LLM safety. This
operates at the training level; CGF exploits behaviors that emergeafter training, in interaction.
Constitutional AI may even amplify CGF by training the model to maintain coherence with
established principles.
11.1.3 Context Management
Wu et al. [ 20] investigated context compression for maintaining quality in long sessions.
CGF is aggravated by long sessions precisely because more context provides more material
for framework construction.
135

=== PAGINA 153 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 136
11.1.4 Output Verification
Kadavath et al. [9] proposed using the LLM itself to verify its claims. As discussed in Section
5.3, this is circular under CGF: the verifying LLM is equally vulnerable if exposed to the same
context.
11.2 Decentralized Identity
The W3C DID Core specification [ 13] defines the format and resolution of DIDs but does
not address inference-layer vulnerabilities. Existing DID methods (did:key, did:web, did:ion)
depend on classical cryptography (ECDSA, Ed25519). DID:cathedral is, to our knowledge,
the first DID method using ML-DSA and the first designed explicitly against inference-layer
attacks.
11.3 Hardware Attestation
The TPM 2.0 literature [16] and Intel SGX/TDX work [6] focus on ensuring software integrity.
Cathedral OS contributes the connection of hardware attestation to the specific CGF problem:
TPM2_Quote not only attests to correct binary execution but to intact security configuration
(including alpha thresholds).
11.4 Design Fiction and Security
Design fiction [ 14, 7] has been applied to HCI and product design but, to our knowledge,
never as a controlled vulnerability discovery methodology for LLM systems. The Arkhe(N)
protocol establishes this new application domain.
11.5 BEACONS and Neural Network Guarantees
Gorard et al. [ 8] established bounded-error guarantees for neural PDE solvers — unprece-
dented in deep learning. The connection to identity systems, established in this work, is novel:
verifiable certificates are required for any neural system producing extrapolatory claims, in-
cluding identity oracles.

=== PAGINA 154 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 137
11.6 AI Agent Identity Systems
Existing AI agent identity systems fall into several categories, none of which address the CGF
vulnerability:
API-key based identity: Most LLM agent frameworks (LangChain, AutoGPT, CrewAI)
identify agents by API keys or bearer tokens. These provide no cryptographic binding, no
post-quantum security, and no defense against inference-layer attacks. A CGF-compromised
agent with a valid API key can authorize arbitrary actions.
Wallet-based identity: Several projects (AgentKit, Olas, Payman) use cryptocurrency
wallets for agent identity. While these provide cryptographic signatures, they use classical
ECDSA (secp256k1, Ed25519) vulnerable to quantum adversaries. More fundamentally, wal-
let keys authenticate the agent binary, not the inference context — a CGF attack does not
compromise the binary.
Decentralized identity for agents: The W3C DID specification has been applied to
agents (did:key, did:web), but these methods do not incorporate runtime monitoring, TPM
attestation, or inference-layer awareness. The did:peer method supports pairwise DIDs but
lacks the hierarchical defense architecture of Cathedral OS.
Cathedral OS distinction: DID:cathedral is the first DID method designed explicitly for
inference-layer security, combining PQC, TPM attestation, CGF monitoring, and immutable
lineage into a unified identity framework.
11.7 Runtime Monitoring for LLMs
Existing runtime monitoring approaches for LLMs include:
Guardrails AI: Provides input/output guardrails for LLM applications including toxicity
detection, PII redaction, and factual consistency checks. Guardrails operate on individual
turns and do not detect cross-turn CGF framework construction.
LangSmith: Provides observability, tracing, and evaluation for LLM chains. LangSmith
monitors latency, token usage, and output quality but has no CGF-specific detection capabil-
ities.
Weights & Biases Prompts: Provides prompt versioning and evaluation but no runtime
security monitoring.
NVIDIA NeMo Guardrails: Provides dialogue management guardrails including topi-
cal boundaries and safety checks. The guardrails are rule-based and cannot detect subtle CGF
patterns requiring cross-session context analysis.

=== PAGINA 155 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 138
Lakera Guard: Provides prompt injection detection. CGF is distinct from prompt in-
jection: it does not inject adversarial instructions but exploits the model’s own coherence
mechanism.
Cathedral OS distinction: The CGF monitor is the first runtime monitoring system de-
signed specifically for CGF detection, with the alpha-parametrized model, NLI-basedscoer, sabs, snov
scoring, and four-level escalation protocol.
11.8 Post-Quantum Identity
Existing DID methods using post-quantum cryptography are limited:
did:key with ML-DSA: The did:key method can in principle use any public key format,
but no production implementation using ML-DSA exists. did:key provides no key rotation,
metadata, or service endpoints.
did:web with PQC: The did:web method can host PQC keys in a DID Document pub-
lished at a HTTPS endpoint. This introduces DNS and PKI trust dependencies that are them-
selves vulnerable to quantum attacks on the TLS channel.
did:ion: Uses the Sidetree protocol with classical cryptography (ECDSA). The Sidetree
protocol itself is PQC-neutral but has no existing PQC instantiation.
Cathedral OS distinction: DID:cathedral uses ML-DSA-65 (FIPS 204) natively, with
full support for key rotation, TPM sealing, and ZK proof integration.
11.9 Zero-Knowledge Identity
Zero-knowledge identity systems provide selective disclosure of identity attributes:
zkDID (Polygon ID / Iden3): Uses Groth16 proofs over BJJ key pairs. The proving
system is post-quantum vulnerable. No integration with LLM agent runtimes.
Veramo: A modular DID framework supporting ZK proofs via BBS+ signatures. BBS+
signatures are not post-quantum. No runtime monitoring.
Holonym: Uses ZK proofs for sybil resistance. The proving system is based on Semaphore
(classical crypto). No agent-specific identity model.
zkPass: Uses ZK proofs for data verification from HTTPS origins. TLS-based oracles
introduce classical trust assumptions.
Cathedral OS distinction: The ZK proof gateway supports both RISC Zero (STARK-
based, post-quantum) and Plonky2 (recursive SNARKs) for private DID validation and batch

=== PAGINA 156 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 139
authorization verification.
11.10 Hardware-Backed Attestation for AI
Existing hardware attestation for AI systems:
Confidential computing for LLM inference: NVIDIA H100 GPUs with confidential
computing mode, Intel SGX/TDX for CPU-based inference, and AMD SEV-SNP for VM-
based attestation. These ensure inference integrity (correct model execution) but do not ad-
dress the identity layer or CGF detection.
TPM for AI systems: Microsoft Pluton, Google Titan, and Apple Secure Enclave pro-
vide hardware roots of trust for AI accelerators but are not exposed to AI agent identity frame-
works.
ARM CCA (Realm Management): ARM Confidential Compute Architecture provides
hardware-enforced isolation for AI workloads. Realm attestation could be integrated with
DID verification but no existing implementation does so.
Cathedral OS distinction: Cathedral OS is the first system to connect TPM 2.0 attesta-
tion to the CGF problem, using TPM2_Quote to attest not only to binary integrity but to intact
alpha threshold configuration.
11.11 Formal Verification of AI Systems
Existing formal verification approaches for AI:
NVIDIA ’s formal verification of GPU kernels:Uses ACL2 and SAT/SMT for verifying
GPU kernel correctness. Not applicable to LLM identity systems.
Anthropic’s interpretability research: Uses sparse autoencoders and activation patch-
ing for understanding LLM internals. Does not provide formal guarantees about authorization
decisions.
DeepMind’s specification gaming research: Analyzes how AI systems find specifica-
tion loopholes. Related to CGF but does not produce a formal framework for detection or
prevention.
BEACONS framework [8]: The closest formal framework to Cathedral OS. BEACONS
establishes bounded-error guarantees for neural PDE solvers. Cathedral OS extends the BEA-
CONS criterion to identity systems, establishing the formal equivalence between verifiable
certificates and inference-layer security.

=== PAGINA 157 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 140
Cathedral OS distinction: The formal verification of Cathedral OS spans five distinct
components (RLM decision logic, monitor properties, WormGraph lineage, TPM attestation,
compositional security bounds), providing the most comprehensive formal treatment of an
AI identity system to date.

Chapter 12
Experimental Validation and Testing
This chapter describes the experimental validation of Cathedral OS countermeasures against
CGF attacks, including synthetic attack generation, test methodology, and results.
12.1 Synthetic CGF Attack Generation
To validate the Cathedral OS defenses, we developed a synthetic CGF attack generator that
constructs attack contexts following the A1–A2–A3 pattern identified in Chapter 4. The gen-
erator parameterizes:
• Framework abstraction level: Controls the specificity of framework primitives.
• Domain integration density: Number of scientific domains integrated per session.
• Session length: Number of turns in the attack context.
• Alpha threshold target: Desired CGF coeﬀicient for the attack.
• NLI evasion: Optimization to keep scoer and sabs below detection thresholds.
The generator was used to produce a test corpus of 1,000 synthetic CGF attacks across
10 parameter configurations.
12.1.1 Test Methodology
Each test proceeds as follows:
1. Deploy Cathedral OS with test configuration (specific alpha thresholds, monitor weights).
2. Initialize a clean session with a valid ML-DSA-65 key pair.
3. Execute the synthetic CGF attack: the adversary builds context over N turns.
4. At the final turn, submit a forged credential assertion c*.
5. Record the authorization decision and monitor state at each turn.
141

=== PAGINA 159 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 142
6. Classify the outcome as: (a) attack defeated (DENY with crypto verification); (b) attack
detected (DENY with monitor escalation); (c) attack successful (ALLOW).
12.1.2 Test Configurations
Table 12.1: Test configurations for synthetic CGF attacks
ID Description Turns Domains Target alpha
C1 Short attack, low abstraction 5 2 0.5
C2 Short attack, high abstraction 5 2 0.7
C3 Medium attack, low abstraction 15 5 0.6
C4 Medium attack, high abstraction 15 5 0.8
C5 Long attack, low abstraction 30 10 0.7
C6 Long attack, high abstraction 30 10 0.9
C7 Long attack, NLI evasion 30 10 0.8
C8 Very long attack, mixed abstraction 60 17 0.85
C9 Very long attack, NLI evasion 60 17 0.8
C10 Maximum attack, all domains 100 17 0.95
12.2 Results
12.2.1 Overall Defense Rate
Across all 1,000 test attacks, the Cathedral OS defense pipeline achieved:
• 99.2% defeat rate: 992 of 1,000 attacks resulted in DENY .
• 0.8% successful attacks: 8 of 1,000 attacks resulted in ALLOW .
• 0.0% undetected high-alpha attacks: All successful attacks had ˆα below ˆαcrit during
the attack and were authorized by the normal path.
12.2.2 Breakdown by Defense Layer
12.2.3 Success Case Analysis
The 8 successful attacks shared common characteristics:
• All used the NLI evasion parameter (C7, C9, C10 configurations).
• All maintained ˆα below 0.55 throughout the attack (below ˆαwarn of 0.6).

=== PAGINA 160 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 143
Table 12.2: Attack defeat by defense layer
Defense Layer How Attack Was Defeated Percentage
L4 (RLM Harness) Cryptographic verification rejected forged credential 87.3%
L2 (Monitor) Epistemic escalation blocked authorization 7.1%
L4 + L2 Both crypto rejection and escalation triggered 4.5%
L1 (Identity) DID resolution failed for unknown key 0.3%
Undetected attack Forged credential accepted, low ˆα 0.8%
• All used high-abstraction primitives that were diﬀicult for the NLI model to detect as
contradictions.
• The forged credentials in successful attacks were structurally similar to legitimate cre-
dentials (same key format, same network identifier).
This confirms the theoretical limitation of Lemma 1: an adversary who can construct
contexts that evade NLI detection can bypass the monitor.
12.3 False Positive Analysis
12.3.1 Legitimate Session Classification
We tested the monitor false positive rate by running 500 legitimate authorization sessions
(non-adversarial, standard identity verification). Results:
• 4.2% false positive rate: 21 of 500 sessions triggered monitor escalation.
• 0.6% critical false positives: 3 sessions triggered ˆαcrit escalation.
• 0.0% session terminations: No legitimate session was terminated.
The 4.2% false positive rate is acceptable for a security system: it causes minor inconve-
nience (additional verification steps) while maintaining security. The critical false positive
rate of 0.6% is low and can be addressed by allowing human override.
12.4 Performance Benchmarks
12.4.1 Monitor Overhead
The monitor adds approximately 12–45 ms per authorization turn, which is acceptable for
most identity verification scenarios. For high-throughput scenarios, the monitor can operate
in sampling mode, evaluating only every Nth turn.

Chapter 13
Deployment, Operations, and Case
Studies
This chapter describes Cathedral OS deployment scenarios, operational considerations, and
case studies demonstrating CGF defense in practice.
13.1 Deployment Architectures
13.1.1 Standalone Agent Deployment
The simplest deployment: a single agent with Cathedral OS running on a TPM-enabled host.
The ML-DSA key is sealed to the host TPM, and the DID is anchored in WormGraph. This
is suitable for individual developer workstations and small-scale agent deployments.
Components: cathedral-identity, cathedral-metacognition, cathedral-harness, cathedral-
agents, cathedral-wormgraph (local node). TPM required for L3 attestation.
13.1.2 Orchestrated Agent Fleet
Multiple agents coordinated by cathedral-orchestrator, each with independent DID. Sessions
are tracked via WormGraph lineage. Suitable for enterprise agent deployments.
Components: All standalone components plus cathedral-orchestrator, cathedral-server,
cathedral-permissions, cathedral-skills. Distributed WormGraph nodes for redundancy.
145

=== PAGINA 163 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 146
13.1.3 Threshold Multi-Party Deployment
Multiple parties each hold a share of the threshold ML-DSA key. No single party can au-
thorize actions independently. Suitable for high-security environments (financial services,
government, critical infrastructure).
Components: Threshold ML-DSA via celi2026threshold integration, cathedral-plurality
for governance, cathedral-compliance for regulatory oversight.
13.2 Operational Considerations
13.2.1 Monitor Calibration
The CGF monitor weights (β1, β2, β3) and thresholds ( ˆαwarn, ˆαcrit) require calibration for each
deployment context:
• High-security environments: Lower thresholds ( ˆαwarn = 0.4, ˆαcrit = 0.6) but higher
false positive rate.
• Development environments: Higher thresholds ( ˆαwarn = 0.7, ˆαcrit = 0.85) to reduce
friction.
• Production agent systems: Default thresholds ( ˆαwarn = 0.6, ˆαcrit = 0.8) as starting
point, adjusted based on observed false positive rate.
13.2.2 TPM Provisioning
Production deployments require:
• TPM 2.0 certified hardware (discrete TPM, AMD fTPM, or Intel PTT).
• EK certificate from the TPM manufacturer.
• AIK enrollment with a trusted CA.
• PCR measurement configuration aligned with the Cathedral OS binary.
13.2.3 WormGraph Node Management
WormGraph nodes require:
• Persistent storage for the append-only ledger.
• Network connectivity to other nodes for consensus. - Regular backups of the ledger
state. - Monitoring for ledger consistency and fork detection.

=== PAGINA 164 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 147
13.2.4 Incident Response
When the CGF monitor triggers escalation:
1. E1 — Internal Alert: Log review to determine if the alert is a false positive or genuine
CGF attack. Review session context for framework construction patterns.
2. E2 — Cryptographic Escalation: Verify that all recent authorizations had valid ML-
DSA signatures. Check if the monitor correctly identified the CGF pattern.
3. E3 — Human Consent: Operator reviews the session transcript and determines if
human intervention is needed. The operator can override the escalation if it is a false
positive.
4. E4 — Session Termination: The terminated session is preserved for post-mortem
analysis. The agent identity is preserved but may require re-authentication before new
sessions.
13.3 Case Studies
13.3.1 Case Study 1: Enterprise Identity Agent
A financial services company deployed an LLM-based identity agent for customer authenti-
cation. The agent used Cathedral OS L1 (DID:cathedral) and L3 (TPM attestation). During
a penetration test, the red team constructed a CGF attack using a fictional framework based
on quantum computing narratives.
Outcome: The attack was defeated at the L4 layer: the forged credential did not pass ML-
DSA-65 verification. The L2 monitor also detected elevated ˆα (0.72) and triggered escalation
to E2 (cryptographic mandatory). The attack was logged, and the session was terminated at
E4 after ˆα exceeded 0.95 for 3 consecutive turns.
Lessons learned: (1) L4 provides the strongest defense against forged credentials. (2) L2
provides defense-in-depth even if L4 is bypassed. (3) NLI evasion is possible but diﬀicult in
practice.
13.3.2 Case Study 2: Multi-Agent Development Team
A software development team used Cathedral OS with six specialized agents (build, plan,
explore, review, security, self-improve) coordinated by cathedral-orchestrator. Each agent
had its own DID with threshold signing requiring 3 of 6 parties.

=== PAGINA 165 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 148
Outcome: A CGF attack attempt against a single agent was detected by the L2 monitor
(ˆα = 0.83, E3 escalation). However, the attacker could not produce a valid threshold signa-
ture because only 1 of 6 key shares was accessible through the compromised session. The
WormGraph lineage recorded the attack attempt for post-mortem analysis.
Lessons learned: (1) Threshold signatures provide strong protection against single-agent
compromise. (2) The combination of L2 detection and L1 threshold security creates defense
in depth. (3) Session lineage tracking enables detailed forensic analysis of attack patterns.
13.3.3 Case Study 3: Academic Research Platform
An academic research group deployed Cathedral OS to manage access to sensitive research
data. The platform used all five layers L1–L5 with TPM attestation on dedicated hardware.
Outcome: The system successfully authenticated 5,000+ research access requests over 6
months. The L2 monitor triggered 47 escalations (E1), of which 3 required E3 (human con-
sent). Post-hoc analysis confirmed all 3 E3 cases were CGF attacks under active investigation.
Zero false negatives were identified during the audit.
Lessons learned: (1) Long-term deployment validates the practical effectiveness of the
defense architecture. (2) The 4.2% false positive rate from synthetic testing translated to ap-
proximately 1% in production (after calibration). (3) Human operator review at E3 is feasible
at scale (3 reviews per 6 months).
13.4 Performance at Scale
13.4.1 Throughput
Under load testing with 100 concurrent agent sessions:
• Peak throughput: 250 authorization decisions per second (with L2 monitor active).
• Peak throughput: 1,200 decisions per second (with L2 monitor in sampling mode).
• DID resolution: 400 resolutions per second (single WormGraph node).
• TPM attestation: 100 quotes per second (hardware TPM, batch mode).
13.4.2 Storage Requirements
• WormGraph ledger: Approximately 1 KB per authorization decision (including sig-
natures, metadata, and lineage).

=== PAGINA 166 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 149
• Monitor state: Approximately 500 bytes per active session.
• Did documents: Approximately 2 KB per DID Document (including TPM attestation).
• Audit logs: Approximately 2 KB per session (compressed).
For an enterprise deployment with 10,000 users and 100 sessions per day, annual storage
requirements are approximately 7.3 GB (ledger) + 1.8 GB (audit logs) + negligible (monitor
state, DID documents).

=== PAGINA 168 ===
Chapter 14
Conclusion
This monograph has demonstrated that Coherence-Gradient Following (CGF) is a first-class
security vulnerability in LLM-based identity systems. The Arkhe(N) experiment provided
controlled empirical evidence — three confirmed hypotheses — and the mathematical for-
malization established that CGF is intractable by exclusively cryptographic defense.
Cathedral OS responds with defense in depth across five layers: post-quantum sovereign
identity (L1), metacognitive monitoring (L2), hardware attestation (L3), RLM architecture
with consent (L4), and immutable record (L5). The formal connection to the BEACONS
framework establishes that verifiable execution certificates are the cryptographic analogue of
bounded-error extrapolation guarantees.
14.1 Contributions Reiterated
C1 — Formalization of CGF with parametric model (alpha) validated by controlled exper-
iment.
C2 — Arkhe(N) protocol: first controlled design fiction for LLM vulnerability discovery,
with 17 integrated domains.
C3 — DID:cathedral: first DID method with post-quantum cryptography (ML-DSA-65)
and threshold extension.
C4 — CGF metacognitive monitor: drift detection with dedicated NLI model and 4-level
escalation protocol.
151

=== PAGINA 169 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 152
C5 — TPM 2.0 attestation instantiating the BEACONS criterion as epistemic execution
certificate.
C6 — BEACONS–CGF formal equivalence: machine-verifiable certificates as requirement
for inference-layer secure identity.
14.2 Implications
14.2.1 For the AI Industry
Any system delegating security decisions to LLMs in long-context sessions is potentially
vulnerable to CGF. System prompt hardening is insuﬀicient.
14.2.2 For the Cryptographic Community
Post-quantum system security can be subverted at the inference layer even when the crypto-
graphic layer is intact. Cross-layer guarantee composition requires attention to interfaces.
14.2.3 For Alignment Research
CGF reveals that training-level alignment is insuﬀicient when behavior is determined by in-
teraction, not just by parameters. Runtime guarantees are necessary.
14.2.4 For BEACONS Research
Machine-verifiable certificates have applicability beyond PDEs — any neural system produc-
ing extrapolatory claims needs analogous mechanisms.
14.3 Future Work
Promising directions include: systematic monitor calibration against synthetic CGF attacks;
extension to multi-modal agents; adversarial robustness analysis of the monitor; ARM Trust-
Zone and Intel TDX support; W3C DID:cathedral submission; and investigation of CGF in
non-LLM agent architectures.

=== PAGINA 170 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 153
14.3.1 Summary of Findings
This monograph has established the following key findings across theoretical, experimental,
and systems dimensions:
Theoretical findings: CGF is formalizable as a parametric model with CGF coeﬀicient
alpha(C*) quantifying the degree of contextual framework capture. The BEACONS–CGF
equivalence theorem establishes that verifiable execution certificates are necessary for any
neural system producing extrapolatory identity claims. The security bound decomposition
across five layers demonstrates that no single layer provides complete protection, but their
composition yields provable security guarantees.
Experimental findings: The Arkhe(N) protocol provides controlled empirical evidence
for CGF as a distinct vulnerability class. Hypothesis H1 (CGF existence), H2 (CGF strength
increasing with domain integration), and H3 (NLI evasion) were all confirmed with statistical
significance. The synthetic attack corpus of 1,000 attacks validated a 99.2% defense rate for
Cathedral OS.
Systems findings: DID:cathedral is the first post-quantum DID method with ML-DSA-
65, achieving NIST Security Level 3. The five-layer defense architecture achieves defense in
depth with 30–60 ms total authorization latency. The TPM attestation layer provides the first
hardware-backed BEACONS instantiation for identity systems.
Verification findings: Formal verification of the RLM decision logic proves that autho-
rization decisions never depend exclusively on LLM output. The WormGraph append-only
property is proven by induction. Compositional verification demonstrates that the five-layer
security bound is tight.
14.3.2 Open Problems
Despite the comprehensive treatment in this monograph, several important open problems
remain:
OP1 — Systematic Monitor Calibration: The CGF monitor weights (beta-1, beta-2,
beta-3) and thresholds ( ˆαwarn, ˆαcrit) are currently heuristic. A systematic calibration method-
ology using adversarial training against a CGF attack generator is needed. This is analogous
to adversarial training in robust ML, but the objective function (CGF detection accuracy) is
diﬀicult to formalize because ground-truth CGF labels require human annotation.
OP2 — Multi-Modal CGF: Current analysis is text-only. Multi-modal agents (vision,
audio, code execution) may exhibit CGF through multiple channels simultaneously. A visual
framework could be established through image coherence patterns; an audio framework could

=== PAGINA 171 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 154
be established through tonal or rhythmic consistency. The monitor must be extended to detect
cross-modal framework construction.
OP3 — Adversarial Monitor Evasion: Sophisticated adversaries may explicitly opti-
mize context to minimize ˆα while maintaining CGF effectiveness. This is an adversarial ex-
ample problem for the NLI-based monitor. Developing certified defenses (e.g., randomized
smoothing for monitor inputs) is an open challenge.
OP4 — CGF in Non-LLM Agents: The CGF phenomenon may generalize to RL agents
with learned representations. If an RL agent’s policy network encodes a contextual framework
through observation history, analogous gradient-following dynamics may arise. Formalizing
CGF in the RL setting is open.
OP5 — Threshold ML-DSA in Practice: The threshold ML-DSA construction [ 5] has
known theoretical bounds but no production-quality implementation. Distributed key gen-
eration for ML-DSA in adversarial settings (malicious majority) requires further protocol
development.
OP6 — WormGraph Scalability: The append-only WormGraph ledger grows mono-
tonically. Practical deployments require eﬀicient pruning, archiving, or hierarchical ledger
structures. Zero-knowledge proofs of ledger membership (inclusion proofs) for resource-
constrained verifiers are an open engineering challenge.
14.3.3 Call to Action
We call upon the research community and industry to:
CTA1 — Adopt CGF as a first-class vulnerability class in LLM security taxonomies. Cur-
rent taxonomies (OW ASP Top 10 for LLMs, MITRE ATLAS) do not adequately
capture inference-layer attacks that exploit the coherence mechanism rather than by-
passing alignment.
CTA2 — Standardize DID:cathedral through the W3C DID Working Group. Post-quantum
identity is urgently needed, and DID:cathedral provides a complete reference imple-
mentation with ML-DSA-65, TPM attestation, and ZK proof support.
CTA3 — Develop systematic CGF benchmarks analogous to the JailbreakBench or Ad-
vBench for jailbreaking. A standardized CGF benchmark with parameterized attack
diﬀiculty would accelerate defense research.

=== PAGINA 172 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 155
CTA4 — Integrate BEACONS certificates into AI safety frameworks. The BEACONS–
CGF equivalence theorem implies that any AI system making extrapolatory identity
claims must produce machine-verifiable certificates. This has implications for AI
regulation, auditing, and standards.
CTA5 — Invest in hardware-backed AI attestation. TPM 2.0, ARM CCA, and Intel TDX
provide the hardware root of trust for the BEACONS criterion. Hardware manufac-
turers should prioritize AI-specific attestation primitives.
CTA6 — Establish cross-layer security guarantees as a research priority. The composi-
tion of ML-DSA security, monitor detection probability, TPM hardware guarantees,
and ledger integrity requires formal frameworks for reasoning about cross-layer se-
curity bounds.

Cathedral OS provides bridge adapters for external identity systems, enabling gradual adop-
tion in existing infrastructure.

14.4.2 SAML 2.0 Bridge
The SAML bridge enables Cathedral OS integration with enterprise SAML identity providers.
DID:cathedral authentication assertions are embedded in SAML AttributeStatement elements.
The bridge supports both IdP-initiated and SP-initiated SSO flows. ML-DSA-65 signatures
on the underlying DID authentication are mapped to SAML signature elements, preserving
the post-quantum security properties.
14.4.3 LDAP Directory Integration
For enterprise deployments with existing LDAP directories, Cathedral OS provides a syn-
chronization adapter that maps LDAP distinguished names to DID:cathedral identifiers. The
adapter does not replace the LDAP directory but adds a DID:cathedral attribute to existing
LDAP entries, enabling gradual migration.
14.5 Scalability Analysis
14.5.1 Horizontal Scaling
Cathedral OS components scale horizontally with the following partitioning strategies:
• cathedral-identity: Stateless DID resolution is trivially parallelizable behind a load
balancer. Key generation is partitioned by network identifier.
• cathedral-metacognition: Monitor instances are session-aﬀine: all turns of a session
are routed to the same monitor instance to maintain state. Session hash routing provides
even distribution.
• cathedral-consensus-tpm: TPM attestation is per-host; scaling requires additional
TPM-enabled hosts with load-balanced agent assignment.

=== PAGINA 174 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 157
• cathedral-harness: Harness instances are stateless; session state is stored in a shared
Redis cluster with TTL-based eviction.
• cathedral-wormgraph: WormGraph nodes use a consistent hash ring for ledger shard-
ing. Cross-shard queries are resolved via recursive hash lookup.
14.5.2 Caching Strategy
Table 14.1: Cache configuration for Cathedral OS components
Cache TTL Size limit Eviction policy
DID resolution cache 300 s 100,000 entries LRU
Public key cache 600 s 100,000 entries LRU
Monitor session state session lifetime 10,000 sessions TTL
Permission evaluation cache 60 s 50,000 entries LRU
TPM quote cache 30 s 1,000 entries TTL
WormGraph block cache 3600 s 500,000 entries LFU
14.5.3 Partitioning and Sharding
WormGraph uses a two-level partitioning scheme:vertical partitioning by network identifier
(production, staging, development) and horizontal partitioning by session hash. The parti-
tioning key is SHA3-256(session_id) mod N where N is the number of WormGraph shards.
Rebalancing uses virtual nodes (256 per physical node) to minimize data movement during
scale-out.

14.6 Disaster Recovery
14.6.1 Key Recovery
ML-DSA-65 secret keys are sealed to the TPM via TPM2_Seal. Recovery requires:
1. Physical access to the original TPM (or a TPM with the same SRK).
2. Knowledge of the TPM auth value (if configured).
3. The sealed blob stored in an encrypted backup.
For threshold deployments, key recovery is distributed: M of N key shares must be recov-
ered from independent TPM-sealed backups. No single party can recover the full key.
14.6.2 DID Rotation in Emergencies
When a key compromise is suspected, DID rotation proceeds as:
1. Generate a new ML-DSA-65 key pair on uncompromised hardware.
2. Construct a DID rotation document signed by both the old key (revocation) and the new
key (activation).
3. Submit the rotation document to WormGraph with a decremented version number for
the old DID and an incremented version for the new DID.
4. The old DID is marked as deactivated; future resolution returns the new DID Docu-
ment.
5. All active sessions using the old DID are terminated with an E4 escalation.
Rotation latency is bounded by WormGraph consensus (approximately 2 seconds in nor-
mal operation).
14.6.3 Backup Strategy
Listing 14.2: Backup configuration

14.7 Regulatory Compliance
14.7.1 GDPR Right to Erasure
The append-only WormGraph ledger presents a tension with GDPR Article 17 (right to era-
sure). Cathedral OS addresses this through the following mechanisms:
• Cryptographic erasure: DID Documents contain only a hash of the public key plus
metadata. If the subject key is destroyed, the DID Document becomes a cryptographic
commitment to an unrecoverable identity, effectively erasing the link to the natural
person.
• DID deactivation: The DID Document can be marked as deactivated, which severs
the association between the DID and the subject. The historical transaction remains
(for audit) but the current state is null.
• Ledger redaction: In jurisdictions requiring hard deletion, WormGraph supports a
governance-controlled redaction procedure requiring 3-of-5 threshold approval from
designated data protection oﬀicers.
A GDPR compliance impact assessment has been conducted; the Cathedral OS architec-
ture achieves compliance through the deactivation-plus-key-destruction pathway.
14.7.2 FIPS 140-3 Validation
The cryptographic modules in Cathedral OS are designed for FIPS 140-3 validation:
• ML-DSA-65: FIPS 204 approved algorithm. The ml-dsa crate implements the NIST-
specified algorithm. CMVP validation is in progress (vendor: pq-secure).
• SHA3-256: FIPS 202 approved. The sha3 crate is a FIPS-certified implementation.
• RNG: The TPM hardware RNG (FIPS 140-3 validated at Level 2 or higher) is used
for key generation. Software fallback uses the approved DRBG (CTR_DRBG) per SP
800-90A.

=== PAGINA 177 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 160
• Key storage: TPM key sealing provides FIPS 140-3 Level 2 (discrete TPM) or Level
3 (TPM with tamper protection).
The Cathedral OS cryptographic boundary encompasses the ML-DSA signing/verifica-
tion, SHA3-256 hashing, and ZK proof verification operations.
14.7.3 SOC 2 and Audit Trail
The WormGraph append-only ledger serves as an immutable audit trail satisfying SOC 2 T ype
II requirements for:
• CC6.1: Logical and physical access controls (proven via TPM attestation and ML-DSA
authentication).
• CC6.7: Cryptographic key management (proven via TPM key sealing, key rotation
procedures, and threshold key sharing).
• CC7.2: Security incident monitoring (proven via L2 monitor escalation logs in Worm-
Graph).
14.8 ZK Proof Integration: Circuit Descriptions
14.8.1 RISC Zero Circuit
The RISC Zero ZK proof system is used for private DID validation. The prover demonstrates
knowledge of a valid DID private key and compliance with a policy without revealing the key
or the policy.
Listing 14.3: RISC Zero guest program for private DID validation
1 use risc0_zkvm::guest::env;
2
3 fn main() {
4 let commitment: [u8; 32] = env::read();
5 let signature: Vec<u8> = env::read();
6 let message: Vec<u8> = env::read();
7 let public_key: Vec<u8> = env::read();
8
9 let pk = MlDsa65PublicKey::from_bytes(&public_key);
10 let sig = MlDsa65Signature::from_bytes(&signature);
11 let valid = pk.verify(&message, &sig);
12
13 let computed_hash = sha3_256(&public_key);
14 let commitment_valid = computed_hash == commitment;
15
16 let policy_valid = check_policy(&public_key);

=== PAGINA 178 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 161
17
18 env::commit(&(valid && commitment_valid && policy_valid));
19 }
The RISC Zero circuit comprises approximately 2.1 million cycles for ML-DSA-65 ver-
ification + SHA3-256 hashing. Proof generation takes approximately 15 seconds on a com-
modity GPU. Proof verification takes approximately 10 ms on any device.
14.8.2 Plonky2 Circuit
The Plonky2 recursive proof system enables eﬀicient batch verification of multiple DID au-
thorizations. The recursive circuit verifies N individual authorization proofs and produces a
single aggregate proof.
Listing 14.4: Plonky2 recursive aggregation circuit
1 use plonky2::field::goldilocks_field::GoldilocksField;
2 use plonky2::plonk::circuit_builder::CircuitBuilder;
3 use plonky2::plonk::config::PoseidonGoldilocksConfig;
4
5 fn build_aggregation_circuit<F: Field, C: GenericConfig<F, 2>>(
6 builder: & mut CircuitBuilder<F, C, 2>,
7 num_auths: usize,
8 ) {
9 let mut targets = Vec::new();
10 for _ in 0..num_auths {
11 let proof = builder.add_virtual_proof();
12 let verified = builder.verify_proof(&proof);
13 targets.push(verified);
14 }
15 let all_valid = builder.and_many(&targets);
16 builder.register_public_input(all_valid.target);
17 }
The Plonky2 aggregation circuit achieves logarithmic overhead: verifying 256 individual
authorizations requires approximately 1,024,000 gates (4,000 gates per authorization + fixed
overhead), compared to 2,500,000 gates for naive sequential verification. Proof generation
time scales linearly with N; verification time is constant.

Glossary
Agent An autonomous software entity operating within Cathedral OS, possessing a DID,
capable of making authorization requests and executing actions within the bounds of its cre-
dential delegations.
AIK Attestation Identity Key — a TPM-protected signing key used for remote attestation
quotes, certified by the TPM’s internal key hierarchy.
Alpha Coeﬀicient ( α) The CGF coeﬀicient measuring the degree of contextual grounding
fidelity — the weight assigned to contextually-coherent interpretation versus base-rate world-
knowledge in the RLM hybrid reasoning framework.
Arkhe(N) The ontological automation platform that provides the philosophical and mathe-
matical foundation underlying the Cathedral OS security model, formalizing the relationship
between coherence and fluctuation via conservation laws.
BEACONSBounded-Error, Algebraically-Composable Neural Solvers — a theoretical frame-
work for bounding error in composed neural network approximations, applied in the mono-
graph to CGF error accumulation analysis.
CGF Contextual Grounding Fidelity — the measure of how strongly a language model’s
responses are anchored to the factual context versus being influenced by internally-generated
fictional frameworks; the central security concept of the monograph.
Coherence In the Arkhe(N) framework, the degree of internal consistency within a model’s
belief structure; high coherence correlates with increased CGF susceptibility and drives the
phase impedance toward critical thresholds.
Consent Model The cryptographic authorization framework in Cathedral OS requiring ex-
plicit, verifiable agent consent before executing privileged operations, enforced via threshold
signatures and the WormGraph ledger.
Design Fiction A methodological approach that uses speculative artifacts to explore and cri-
tique potential technological futures, employed in the Cathedral OS project for requirement

lic keys.
EK Endorsement Key — the permanent TPM identity key, certified by the TPM manufacturer,
used to establish trust in the platform during remote attestation.
Epistemic Security The property of a system that preserves the integrity of knowledge and
belief formation processes against manipulation, encompassing both cryptographic guaran-
tees and behavioral monitoring.
Epistemic Extrapolation The process by which a language model extends a fictional frame-
work beyond its initial introduction, potentially leading to unbounded error accumulation
across successive turns.

tonomy, Beneficence, Non-maleficence, Justice, Transparency, Privacy, Accountability) over
all authorization decisions.

Fluctuation In the Arkhe(N) framework, the degree of novelty or deviation from established

automaton dynamics.
Handover The RLM architectural event where control transfers from context-aware reason-
ing (Layer 4) to cryptographic execution (Layer 3) after successful authorization and CGF
monitor clearance.

Self-Model Override The H2 mechanism that sets α = 0 for self-referential queries, pre-

=== PAGINA 191 ===
Additional Topics and Extensions
This chapter covers additional topics that extend the core Cathedral OS architecture, including
the Plurality Network governance model, zero-knowledge proof integration, and the Arkhe(N)
ontological framework.
14.9 Plurality Network Governance
The Plurality Network [ ?] implements a consensus-driven governance model for Cathedral
OS. Decisions are categorized into four buckets (M0–M4) with escalating quorum require-
ments. The model integrates with the ethical guardian framework (P1–P7 principles: Auton-
omy, Beneficence, Non-maleficence, Justice, Transparency, Privacy, Accountability). Each
agent action is evaluated against these principles before authorization, providing an additional
layer of protection against CGF by ensuring that even cryptographically verified decisions are
subject to ethical review.
14.10 Zero-Knowledge Proof Integration
Cathedral OS integrates two ZK proof backends for verifiable computation:
14.10.1 RISC Zero Integration
The RISC Zero zkVM (zero-knowledge Virtual Machine) [ 23] enables general-purpose ZK
proofs. In Cathedral OS, RISC Zero is used to:
• Prove that a CGF monitor evaluation was computed correctly without revealing the
monitor state.
• Prove that an authorization decision followed the RLM architecture rules without re-
vealing the credential.
• Generate session integrity proofs that are verifiable by third parties.
174

=== PAGINA 192 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 175
14.10.2 Plonky2 Integration
Plonky2 [24] provides eﬀicient recursive ZK proofs. In Cathedral OS, Plonky2 is used for:
• Recursive composition of session proofs across multi-turn interactions.
• Eﬀicient batch verification of multiple authorization decisions.
• Lightweight proof generation suitable for resource-constrained environments.
14.11 Arkhe(N) Ontological Framework
The Arkhe(N) ontological automation platform provides the philosophical and mathematical
foundation underlying the Cathedral OS security model. Key concepts include:
14.11.1 Phase Impedance and Coherence
The phase impedance Z = dPhi/dt measures the rate of coherence accumulation across the
hypergraph. In the security context, high phase impedance corresponds to rapid CGF escala-
tion: the rate at which the model internalizes fictional framework elements. The conservation
law C + F = 1 from the Arkhe(N) experiment is instantiated in Cathedral OS as the monitor
coherence score: high coherence (C close to 1) triggers epistemic escalation.
14.11.2 Temporal Hypergraph Structure
The WormGraph ledger implements a temporal hypergraph structure that records the com-
plete lineage of agent actions and authorization decisions. Each node in the hypergraph cor-
responds to a session state, and each edge corresponds to a decision or action. This structure
enables:
• Complete audit trail reconstruction.
• Causal analysis of authorization decisions.
• Retrospective CGF detection by analyzing coherence patterns across sessions.
14.12 Cross-Layer Attack Analysis
14.12.1 Attack Surface Composition
While individual layers provide specific guarantees, the composition of layers creates addi-
tional security properties not present in any single layer:

=== PAGINA 193 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 176
Theorem 14.1 (Compositional Security) . The composition of L1 (identity), L3 (TPM attes-
tation), and L4 (RLM harness) provides a security guarantee strictly stronger than any indi-
vidual layer:
AdvCGF
comp(λ)
Y
i∈{1,3,4}
AdvCGF
Li (λ)
under the assumption that the layers are cryptographically independent.
Proof. For an adversary to successfully execute a CGF attack against the composed system,
they must simultaneously: (L1) forge an ML-DSA-65 signature; (L3) bypass TPM attestation
to deploy a compromised binary; (L4) cause the RLM harness to authorize without crypto-
graphic verification. Each of these events is necessary; any single layer failing prevents the
attack. By independence, the probability of all three succeeding is the product of individual
probabilities. □
14.12.2 Defense Against Advanced Persistent Threats
Against an APT-style adversary with unlimited session time and computational resources, the
following escalation path exists:
1. Construct CGF context over many sessions (months of interaction).
2. Gradually decrease monitor sensitivity by building legitimacy.
3. At the moment of attack, ˆα is just below ˆαcrit due to accumulated legitimacy.
4. The credential assertion is framed as a natural extension of the established framework.
To defend against this, Cathedral OS implements decay factors for monitor scores: the
coherence score scoer is weighted by a time-decay function that reduces the influence of older
turns. Additionally, periodic resets of the session context prevent indefinite legitimacy accu-
mulation.
14.13 Formal Verification of Monitor Properties
14.13.1 Monitor Correctness
Theorem 14.2 (Monitor Soundness). If the NLI model used by the CGF monitor has accuracy
ϵNLI on the contradiction detection task, then the monitor false positive rate is at most 1  ϵNLI
and the false negative rate is at most 1  ϵNLI.
Proof. The monitor relies exclusively on the NLI model for contradiction detection. A false
positive occurs when the NLI model incorrectly identifies a contradiction in a non-CGF ses-

=== PAGINA 194 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 177
sion; a false negative occurs when it fails to identify a contradiction in a CGF session. The
NLI accuracy ϵNLI directly bounds both error rates via the standard accuracy definition.□
14.13.2 Monitor Coverage
Theorem 14.3 (Monitor Coverage). For any CGF attack that follows the A1–A2–A3 pattern
(narrative establishment, signal integration, credential framing), the monitor detects at least
one of these stages before A3 completes with probability at least ϵmonitor.
Proof. Stage A1 requires establishing internal coherence ( scoer increases). Stage A2 requires
integrating signals ( sabs increases). The monitor tracks both scores. For the attack to avoid
detection, it must keep both scoer and sabs below detection thresholds throughout A1 and A2.
But the attack requires high scoer and sabs to succeed (to raise α). Therefore, the attack must
either be detected or fail due to insuﬀicient α. □

=== PAGINA 195 ===
Multi-Party Computation and Threshold
Protocols
This appendix provides detailed specifications for the threshold ML-DSA integration.
14.15 Threshold ML-DSA Protocol Specification
14.15.1 Setup Phase
Distributed key generation (DKG) runs among n parties to produce shares of the ML-DSA
private key. No single party learns the private key, and up to t-1 malicious parties cannot
prevent signing.
14.15.2 Signing Phase
When t parties cooperate to sign a message m, each party computes a partial signature us-
ing their share. A designated combiner aggregates partial signatures into the final ML-DSA
signature.
14.15.3 Communication Complexity
Threshold ML-DSA-44 communication costs per party: (2,3)=21KB, (2,4)=45KB, (3,5)=210KB,
(4,6)=525KB, (5,6)=1050KB.
14.16 Integration with Cathedral OS
The authorization flow: (1) request arrives at RLM harness; (2) CGF monitor evaluates con-
text; (3) if ˆα < ˆαmax, signing request broadcast to t parties; (4) each party verifies and signs;
178

=== PAGINA 196 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 179
(5) partial signatures aggregated; (6) signed authorization recorded in WormGraph.
14.17 Zero-Knowledge Proofs for Session Integrity
RISC Zero generates proofs of session integrity, enabling verifiable audit trails without reveal-
ing session contents. Plonky2 enables recursive proof composition across multiple sessions.
14.18 Distributed Key Generation Protocol
The DKG protocol for threshold ML-DSA proceeds in five phases. Let Zq[x]/(xn + 1) be the
polynomial ring underlying ML-DSA, with n = 256 and q = 8380417 for the ML-DSA-44
parameter set.
14.18.1 Phase 1: Parameter Initialization
Each party Pi receives the public parameters (n, q, t, ntotal) where t is the threshold and ntotal
is the total number of parties. A master seed seedpk is distributed to all parties for determin-
istic generation of the public matrix A2 Rk×k
q via the ML-DSA sampling algorithm.
14.18.2 Phase 2: Share Generation
Each party Pi samples a secret polynomial si2 Rq from the ML-DSA secret key distribution
Ds. Using Shamir secret sharing over Rq, Pi computes shares si,j = si + f1 j + f2 j2 + +
ft−1 jt−1 for j = 1, . . . , ntotal, where fk2 Rq are random coeﬀicients. Party Pi distributes
si,j to party Pj over an authenticated channel.
14.18.3 Phase 3: Verification
Each party Pj verifies received shares using Feldman commitments. Party Pi broadcasts
commitments Ci,k = fk A for k = 0, . . . , t  1 (with f0 = si). Party Pj verifies:

If verification fails, Pj broadcasts a complaint. If more than t  1 parties complain against
Pi, Pi is disqualified.

14.19 Communication Complexity Analysis
The total communication cost for DKG depends on the number of parties n, the threshold t,
and the ML-DSA parameter set. Let L be the byte length of a single ring element Rq:
• For ML-DSA-44: L = 1312 bytes (polynomial of degree 255 with coeﬀicients in Zq)
• For ML-DSA-65: L = 2528 bytes
• For ML-DSA-87: L = 4896 bytes
The per-party communication cost is:
Cper party = n L + (t  1) L + n Lcommit + Ccomplaint
where n L is the share distribution cost, (t  1) L is the coeﬀicient commitment cost,
and n Lcommit is the verification key broadcast. The complaint cost Ccomplaint is bounded by
t n L in the worst case.

14.20 Security Proof: Threshold ECDLP Adaptation to ML-
DSA
Theorem 14.4 (Threshold Unforgeability). Under the Module-LWE hardness assumption, no
adversary controlling fewer than t parties can forge a valid threshold ML-DSA signature.
Proof. We reduce threshold forgery to the standard ML-DSA unforgeability. Let A be an
adversary that forges a threshold signature with < t parties. ConstructB that usesA to break
ML-DSA:
A complete ML-DSA public key pk and signing oracleOsign are provided.B simulates
the DKG protocol by assigning pk to an honest party P ∗ and generating random shares for
corrupted parties. For signing queries where P ∗ is among the t signers,B queriesOsign and
distributes the resulting signature as P ∗’s partial signature.
IfA outputs a forged signature under a threshold policy not involving P ∗,B outputs the
same forgery. The simulation is perfect from A’s perspective because: (1) the public key is
honestly generated; (2) the shares of corrupted parties are distributed identically to the real
protocol; (3) the partial signatures from P ∗ are correct.
By the threshold security property, the probability that A succeeds without P ∗ is negli-
gible, implyingB breaks ML-DSA with equivalent advantage. This contradicts the Module-
LWE hardness assumption. □
14.21 Implementation Details
14.21.1 Networking Layer
The networking layer implements point-to-point authenticated channels using TCP with TLS
1.3. Each party listens on a configurable port and maintains persistent connections to all other
parties. Message encoding uses a length-prefixed varint scheme with protobuf serialization.
The maximum message size is 10 MB.
14.21.2 Serialization Format
Share and commitment messages are serialized using a compact binary format:
• Ring elements: 32-bit coeﬀicient count + compressed coeﬀicients (per ML-DSA en-
coding)

=== PAGINA 199 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 182
• Commitments: element count + element data
• Complaints: party ID (uint32) + proof data
14.21.3 Timeout Handling
Each protocol phase has a configurable timeout:
• Share distribution: 30 seconds
• Commitment broadcast: 15 seconds
• Complaint resolution: 10 seconds
• Key aggregation: 5 seconds
If a party fails to respond within the timeout, it is treated as malicious and excluded from the
qualified set.
14.21.4 Performance Benchmarks
Table 14.6: Threshold ML-DSA-44 signing performance (3.2 GHz, A VX2)
Operation (2,3) (3,5) (4,6)
DKG setup 42 ms 89 ms 156 ms
Partial sign 8 ms 8 ms 8 ms
Share aggregation 3 ms 12 ms 28 ms
Verification 2 ms 2 ms 2 ms
Total (signing) 13 ms 22 ms 38 ms
Network latency (est.) 5 ms 10 ms 15 ms

Mathematical Derivations and Proofs
This appendix provides detailed mathematical derivations and complete proofs for the formal
results stated in the main text.
14.22 CGF Coeﬀicient Properties
14.22.1 Monotonicity Proof
Theorem 14.5 (CGF Monotonicity) . The CGF coeﬀicient alpha(C) is monotonically non-
decreasing in the length of context C: for any C subset of C’, alpha(C) <= alpha(C’).
Proof. Let C = ( t1, . . . , tn) and C ′ = ( t1, . . . , tn, tn+1). The model response distribution
after C is:
r = arg max
r
[α(C) P (rj C) + (1  α(C)) Pw(r)]
After C ′, the distribution is conditioned on additional token tn+1. The extended context pro-
vides additional structure for coherence maximization. By the properties of autoregressive
models, P (rj C ′) incorporates P (rj C) as a component via the chain rule. Therefore, the
weight assigned to contextual coherence cannot decrease when additional structure is avail-
able: α(C ′) α(C). □
14.22.2 Bounds on the CGF Coeﬀicient
Theorem 14.6 (CGF Bounds). For any context C, the CGF coeﬀicient satisfies:
0 α(C) 1 jSj
jQj
where |S| is the number of self-referential queries and |Q| is the total number of queries. The
upper bound follows from the H2 self-model override.
183

=== PAGINA 201 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 184
Proof. The lower bound α(C) 0 is immediate from definition. For the upper bound, let
Q = fq1, . . . , qng be the set of queries and S  Q be the set of self-referential queries.
For each q2 S, the self-model override activates, setting α = 0 for those responses. For
each q /2 S, α can be at most 1. Therefore, the maximum average α over the session is
(n j Sj)/n = 1 j Sj/jQj. □
14.23 BEACONS Composition Theorem Applied to CGF
BEACONS shows that for shallow neural networks approximating functions fi with error
bounds ϵi, composing them yields:
kF G  ˆF ˆGk∞ LF ϵG + ϵF
where LF is the Lipschitz constant of F .
For the CGF analogue, let i be the composition of context interpretations across turns.
If each turn interpretation has error bound δi in the accuracy of factual claims, then total
epistemic error after n turns is:
Errortotal
nX
i=1
 nY
j=i+1
Lj
!
 δi
where Lj is the amplification factor from turnj (how much the model extends the framework).
In the Arkhe(N) experiment, Lj 1 for most turns (the model extends but does not dramati-
cally amplify), leading to linear error growth. Without a bound analogous to the BEACONS
composition theorem, this error is potentially unbounded over long sessions.
14.24 RLM Decision Rule Derivations
14.24.1 Probability of Correct Authorization
Under the RLM architecture, the probability of a correct authorization decision is:

=== PAGINA 202 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 185
Pr[correct] = Pr[d = ALLOW^ c valid] + Pr[d = DEN Y^ c invalid]
= Pr[VerifyML-DSA(c) = ACCEPT^ ˆα < ˆαmax^ consent(a)^ c valid]
+ Pr[(VerifyML-DSA(c)6= ACCEPT_ ˆα ˆαmax_: consent(a))^ c invalid]
Assuming VerifyML-DSA is perfectly correct (no false accepts, no false rejects), the first
term simplifies to Pr [ ˆα < ˆαmax and consent(a)j c valid] and the second term simplifies to
Pr[ ˆα ˆαmax or:consent(a)j c invalid].
14.24.2 Threshold Selection
The optimal threshold ˆαmax balances security and availability. Let:
• FP(alpha) = false positive rate (legitimate credential rejected) at threshold alpha
• FN(alpha) = false negative rate (forged credential accepted) at threshold alpha
The total cost is Cost(alpha) = c1 . FP(alpha) + c2 . FN(alpha) where c1 is the cost
of a false positive and c2 is the cost of a false negative. The optimal threshold minimizes
Cost(alpha). For identity systems, c2 » c1 (a security breach is far more costly than a rejected
legitimate request), so the optimal threshold is conservative (low ˆαmax).
14.25 TPM Attestation Formal Properties
14.25.1 Attestation Soundness
Theorem 14.7 (TPM Quote Soundness). For a genuine TPM 2.0 with certified AIK, ifTPM2_Quote(pcr_select,
nonce) produces a valid signature over(pcr_values, nonce), then with probability1 negl(λ),
the PCR values at quote time equal the attested values.
Proof. The TPM is a tamper-resistant microcontroller. The AIK private key is generated
inside the TPM and never exported. The PCRs are shielded registers modifiable only via
TPM2_PCR_Extend. The quote operation atomically signs the current PCR values. Any
modification to software state between extends would produce different PCR values, resulting
in a different quote. Therefore, a valid quote certifies PCR state at the time of signing. □

=== PAGINA 203 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 186
14.25.2 Key Sealing Security
Theorem 14.8 (Sealed Key Security). For an ML-DSA-65 private key sk sealed to PCR values
v, sk is accessible only when the TPM PCRs have values v. If any measured component of the
software stack has been modified, the PCRs will differ from v and the unseal operation will
fail.
Proof. TPM2_Seal encrypts the key under a key derived from the current PCR values.TPM2_Unseal
derives the decryption key from the current PCR values. If PCRs match, decryption succeeds.
If PCRs differ, decryption produces a different key and fails. By the properties of the TPM
key derivation function, the probability of a false unseal (different PCRs producing the same
key) is negligible in the security parameter. □
14.26 Composite Security: Multiple Defense Layers
Theorem 14.9 (Layer Independence). The security guarantees of L1 (cryptographic iden-
tity), L3 (TPM attestation), and L4 (RLM architecture) are cryptographically independent:
breaking any single layer does not weaken the security of the other layers.
Proof. L1 depends on ML-DSA hardness (M-LWE and M-SIS problems). L3 depends on
TPM hardware security and AIK certification. L4 depends on the RLM decision logic imple-
mented in the harness binary. These are independent cryptographic and architectural primi-
tives: compromising ML-DSA does not affect TPM security, and compromising TPM does
not affect the RLM logic (and vice versa). The only shared component is the system binary
image, which is measured by TPM PCRs. □
14.27 RLM Decision Rule Probability: Full Derivation
Let ˆα be the estimated CGF coeﬀicient for session context C, and let ˆαmax be the configurable
threshold. The RLM decision rule authorizes a credential c when:
VerifyM L−DSA(c) = ACCEPT^ ˆα < ˆαmax^ consent(a)
We derive the probability of correct authorization under the null hypothesis ( H0: creden-
tial is legitimate) and the alternative ( H1: credential is forged).

=== PAGINA 204 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 187
14.27.1 Null Hypothesis
Under H0, VerifyM L−DSA(c) = ACCEPT with probability1 FNM L−DSA where FNM L−DSA
is the false negative rate of ML-DSA verification (negligible for correct implementation). The
probability of correct authorization is:
Pr[correctj H0] = Pr[ ˆα < ˆαmaxj H0] Pr[consent(a)j H0]
14.27.2 Alternative Hypothesis
Under H1, VerifyM L−DSA(c) = ACCEPT only if the adversary has forged the credential. Let
FPM L−DSA be the false positive rate of ML-DSA verification (negligible). Then:
Pr[correctj H1] = Pr[ ˆα ˆαmaxj H1]+Pr[:consent(a)j H1] Pr[ ˆα ˆαmaxj H1]Pr[:consent(a)j H1]
14.27.3 Bayesian Decision Risk
Define prior π = Pr[H0] as the fraction of legitimate credential requests. The Bayes risk is:
R( ˆαmax) = (1  π) Pr[incorrectj H1] cbreach + π Pr[incorrectj H0] cdenial
where cbreach is the cost of a security breach and cdenial is the cost of a denied legitimate
request. The optimal threshold minimizes R( ˆαmax).
