14.31 Key Recovery Probability for Sealed Keys
TPM key sealing binds an ML-DSA private key to specific PCR values. The recovery proba-
bility depends on the staleness of the measured software state.
14.31.1 Recovery Under Identical State
If the software stack is identical to the sealing state, recovery is guaranteed:
Pr[recoverj PCRcurrent = PCRseal] = 1  negl(λ)
14.31.2 Recovery Under Modified State
If even one component differs, the probability of recovery is negligible:
Pr[recoverj PCRcurrent6= PCRseal] = negl(λ)

=== PAGINA 208 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 191
14.31.3 Expected Recovery Over Update Cycle
Let pstable be the probability that no measured component changes between sealing and un-
sealing. The expected recovery probability is:
E[recover] = pstable (1  negl(λ)) + (1  pstable) negl(λ)
For a system with monthly software updates and a 24-hour key usage window, pstable
0.97 (assuming updates affect an average of 1 in 30 days). This yields E[recover] 0.97,
providing strong availability guarantees.

.6 Verification Method Encoding
ML-DSA public keys are encoded using the multibase and multicodec formats:
• Multicodec prefix : 0x1a2b (ML-DSA-65), 0x1a2c (ML-DSA-44), 0x1a2d (ML-
DSA-87)
• Multibase prefix: z (base58btc) or u (base64url)
• Encoding: [multicodec][key_bytes] encoded as multibase

=== PAGINA 213 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 196
The key bytes are the raw ML-DSA public key in the format specified by FIPS 204 Section
7.2. For ML-DSA-65, this is 2016 bytes: ρ (32 bytes) + t1 (1984 bytes). The full multibase-
encoded verification method identifier is:
publicKeyMultibase = base58btc(0x1a2bkρkt1)

.8 Security Considerations
.8.1 Key Binding
The DID suﬀix is computed as SHA3-256( pkM L−DSA), binding the identifier to the public
key. Any key rotation produces a new DID suﬀix, preventing key re-use across identities. The
binding is computationally binding under the collision resistance of SHA3-256.
.8.2 Replay Protection
All DID update operations require inclusion of a freshness nonce in the signed payload. The
nonce is derived from the current WormGraph block height:
nonce = SHA3-256(block_heightkprev_txid)

=== PAGINA 214 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 197
This prevents replay of update transactions across different chain states. The verification
method checks that nonce is within100 blocks of the current height.
.8.3 Freshness Requirements
Service endpoint URIs are considered stale after 30 days. DID resolvers MUST check the
updated timestamp of the DID document and reject documents older than the freshness
threshold. Implementations MAY cache resolved documents for up to 15 minutes without
re-validation.
.8.4 Threshold Authorization
For DID update and revoke operations, a threshold of t-of-n ML-DSA signatures is required.
The threshold parameter is stored in the DID document and MUST be at least t 2 for any
n > 1. Single-signature updates are only permitted for DIDs with n = 1.

.10 Expanded Crate Descriptions
.10.1 cathedral-plurality
Implements the Plurality Network governance model with M0–M4 voting tiers. Handles
proposal submission, quorum computation, and vote tallying using weighted consensus. In-
tegrates with the WormGraph ledger for proposal lifecycle tracking and with the ethical
guardian for principle-based proposal filtering.
.10.2 cathedral-plurality-adapter
Provides abstraction layer between the core Plurality Network implementation and external
governance systems. Supports federation with external DAO frameworks and bridges to
Ethereum-based governance contracts via the taproot bridge.
.10.3 cathedral-fl
Federated learning framework for training collaborative models across Cathedral OS nodes
without sharing raw training data. Implements secure aggregation using threshold cryptogra-
phy and differential privacy guarantees calibrated to the security context.
.10.4 cathedral-anomaly
Anomaly detection system monitoring agent behavior patterns and system metrics. Uses
statistical process control (SPC) and isolation forest algorithms to detect deviations from
baseline behavior. Integrates with the CGF monitor to provide additional detection signals.
.10.5 cathedral-compliance
Regulatory compliance engine supporting CFTC, SEC, GDPR, and emerging digital asset
frameworks. Provides configurable rule sets, audit report generation, and regulatory submis-
sion formatting. Rules are versioned and attested via TPM PCR measurements.
.10.6 cathedral-ethical-certification
Formal verification toolkit for certifying that agent actions satisfy the P1–P7 ethical principles.
Uses SAT/SMT solvers to check action preconditions against ethical constraints. Produces

=== PAGINA 218 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 201
machine-readable certification artifacts stored in the WormGraph ledger.
.10.7 cathedral-zk-circuits
Plonky2-based zero-knowledge circuit implementations for recursive proof composition. In-
cludes circuits for batch signature verification, session integrity proofs, and threshold au-
thorization proofs. Optimized for the ML-DSA parameter space using custom PLONKish
arithmetization.
.10.8 cathedral-zk-risc0
RISC Zero zkVM integration providing general-purpose ZK proof generation. Wraps the
RISC Zero proving system with Cathedral OS-specific guest programs for monitor evaluation
proofs and consent verification proofs. Supports both local and remote proof generation.
.10.9 cathedral-swarm
Networking layer based on libp2p gossipsub protocol for peer discovery, message propaga-
tion, and DHT-based routing. Implements Cathedral OS-specific protocol handlers for con-
sent broadcast and WormGraph transaction propagation. Includes NAT traversal and relay
support.
.10.10 cathedral-pqc
Post-quantum cryptography primitives wrapping the ML-KEM (FIPS 203) and SLH-DSA
(FIPS 205) implementations. Provides a unified API for hybrid classical-PQC key exchange
and signatures. Includes benchmarking infrastructure for performance characterization.
.10.11 cathedral-nostr
Nostr protocol integration for decentralized event publication and subscription. Maps Cathe-
dral OS authorization events to Nostr event kinds for interoperability with the broader Nostr
ecosystem. Supports relay discovery and event filtering.

=== PAGINA 219 ===
Oliveira & Bednarski 202
.10.12 cathedral-staking
Staking and consensus mechanism for the Plurality Network. Implements token staking, del-
egation, slashing conditions, and reward distribution. Staking weights influence governance
voting power. Integrates with TPM for secure staking key management.
.10.13 cathedral-taproot-bridge
Bitcoin Taproot integration enabling time-locked and multi-signature Bitcoin transactions
from Cathedral OS. Supports Taproot script path spending for threshold authorization outputs.
Bridges WormGraph events to Bitcoin transactions via a two-way peg mechanism.
.10.14 cathedral-x402-gateway
X402 payment gateway for pay-per-request API access. Implements the X402 protocol for
HTTP 402 Payment Required responses with Lightning Network micropayments. Integrates
with the consent model for authorized payment flows.

=== PAGINA 221 ===
About this Monograph
This monograph presents the formalization of Coherence-Gradient Following(CGF) — a
structural vulnerability in Large Language Model agents operating as identity oracles — and
the Cathedral OS architecture as a comprehensive set of countermeasures implemented in the
Rust programming language. The empirical foundation is the Arkhe(N) experiment, a con-
trolled design fiction protocol that confirmed three formal hypotheses about LLM epistemic
behavior, integrating seventeen real scientific domains as apparent confirmations within a
sustained fictional framework.
The countermeasures are implemented across five defense layers: post-quantum identity
anchored in ML-DSA-65 (FIPS 204), a metacognitive CGF-drift monitor with four-level epis-
temic escalation, and TPM 2.0 hardware attestation producing externally verifiable execution
certificates. The formal connection to the BEACONS framework establishes that such certifi-
cates are the cryptographic analogue of bounded-error extrapolation guarantees.
Research Group Arkhe(n) Research Group / Safe Core
Location Rio de Janeiro, Brazil
Version 2.0 — June 2026
Keywords CGF; PQC; ML -DSA; DID; TPM 2.0; LLM Security; Rust
IACR ePrint Submitted — June 2026
